Generalized Fluid Models of the Braginskii Type
P. Hunana, T. Passot, E. Khomenko, D. Martínez-Gómez, M. Collados, A. Tenerani, G. P. Zank, Y. Maneva, M. L. Goldstein, G. M. Webb

Abstract
Several generalizations of the well-known fluid model of Braginskii (Rev. of Plasma Phys., 1965) are considered. We use the Landau collisional operator and the moment method of Grad. We focus on the 21-moment model that is analogous to the Braginskii model, and we also consider a 22-moment model. Both models are formulated for general multi-species plasmas with arbitrary masses and temperatures, where all the fluid moments are described by their evolution equations. The 21-moment model contains two “heat flux vectors” (3rd and 5th-order moments) and two “viscosity-tensors” (2nd and 4th-order moments). The Braginskii model is then obtained as a particular case of a one ion-electron plasma with similar temperatures, with de-coupled heat fluxes and viscosity-tensors expressed in a quasi-static approximation. We provide all the numerical values of the Braginskii model in a fully analytic form (together with the 4th and 5th-order moments). For multi-species plasmas, the model makes calculation of transport coefficients straightforward. Formulation in fluid moments (instead of Hermite moments) is also suitable for implementation into existing numerical codes. It is emphasized that it is the quasi-static approximation which makes some Braginskii coefficients divergent in a weakly-collisional regime. Importantly, we show that the heat fluxes and viscosity-tensors are coupled even in the linear approximation, and that the fully contracted (scalar) perturbations of the 4th-order moment, which are accounted for in the 22-moment model, modify the energy exchange rates. We also provide several Appendices, which can be useful as a guide for deriving the Braginskii model with the moment method of Grad.



I
Introduction

The fluid model of
Braginskii 1958
;
Braginskii 1965
represents a cornerstone of
plasma transport theory and it is used in many different areas, from solar physics to laboratory plasmas.
The Braginskii model and its generalizations can be derived through two major classical routes
1) Chapman-Enskog expansions
(
Chapman & Cowling 1939
)
and 2) the moment method of
Grad 1949a
;
Grad 1949b
;
Grad 1958
.
There also exists a more modern route with the projection operator
(
Krommes 2018a
;
Krommes 2018b
)
.
Both classical routes were originally developed for gases, where the full Boltzmann collisional operator has to be used.
As was shown by
Landau 1936
;
Landau 1937
, for charged particles interacting through Coulomb collisions the Boltzmann
operator can be partially simplified, and this collisional operator is known as the Landau operator.
It is now well-established that for Coulomb collisions both Landau and Boltzmann operators yield the same results,
if in the Boltzmann operator one introduces integration cut-offs that remove the divergences in the same way as the Coulomb logarithm does.
With the Landau operator, the Boltzmann equation is then typically called the Landau equation. By introducing Rosenbluth potentials
the Landau operator can be re-written into a general Fokker-Planck form, and the name Fokker-Planck equation is often used as well.
Nevertheless, many authors use the Boltzmann operator during calculations even when Coulomb collisions are considered, because the simplification is not
exceedingly large. Braginskii used the Landau operator.
Of course, both routes through Chapman-Enskog expansions and the moment method of Grad have sub-variations on how the methods are implemented
that were developed over the years. For the Chapman-Enskog method where the distribution function is expanded
in Laguerre-Sonine polynomials, see for example
Braginskii 1958
;
Hinton 1983
;
Helander & Sigmar 2002
;
Kunz 2021
.

Here we use the moment method of Grad, which consists of expanding the distribution function in tensorial Hermite polynomials.
Concerning only viscosity-tensors and heat fluxes (and neglecting fully contracted scalar perturbations and higher-order tensorial “anisotropies” as

Balescu 1988
calls them), the method of Grad consists of approximating the distribution function as a series

f_{a}=f_{a}^{(0)}(1+\chi_{a});\qquad\chi_{a}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Big[{h}^{(2n)}_{ij}{H}^{(2n)}_{ij}+{h}^{(2n+1)}_{i}{H}^{(2n+1)}_{i}\Big],

(1)

where
f_{a}^{(0)}
is Maxwellian, “a” is species index, indices i and j run from 1 to 3,

H
are Hermite polynomials and
h
are Hermite moments. Matrices
h_{ij}^{(2n)}
are traceless and
can be viewed as viscosity-tensors (stress-tensors),
and vectors
{h}^{(2n+1)}_{i}
can be viewed as heat fluxes. The series is cut at some chosen
N
, and this distribution function is then used in the Landau
(or Boltzmann) equation, which is integrated to obtain a corresponding fluid model. The usual quasi-static approximation does not have to be applied, and one obtains
evolution equations for all the considered moments.
For example, prescribing a strict Maxwellian with perturbation

\chi_{a}=0
(or equivalently
N=0
) represents the 5-moment model, with evolution equations for density, fluid velocity and scalar pressure (temperature),
where stress-tensors and heat fluxes are zero.
Prescribing
N=1
represents the 13-moment model, which contains evolution equation for one traceless viscosity
tensor (5 independent components) and an evolution equation for one heat flux vector (3 independent components). This model thus contains the main
ingredients of the model of Braginskii, i.e. the usual viscosity tensor and the usual heat flux vector are present. However,
prescribing a quasi-static approximation, yields for example the coefficient of parallel electron heat conductivity (for a one-ion electron plasma with ion charge
Z_{i}=1
)
with value 1.34 instead of the Braginskii value 3.16, meaning the model is not sufficiently precise.
Prescribing
N=2
represents 21-moment model, and this model can be viewed as containing evolution equations
for two viscosity tensors and two heat flux vectors. It can be shown that expressing the viscosity tensors and heat fluxes in a
quasi-static approximation yields a model that is equivalent to
Braginskii 1965
.
In fact, as pointed out for example by
Balescu 1988
, the
Hermite polynomials are directly related to the Laguerre-Sonine polynomials; see equation (
192
), and thus
the Chapman-Enskog method and the moment method of Grad have to yield equivalent results at the end. In general, if both heat fluxes and viscosities are accounted for,
an
N
-Laguerre model therefore represents a
(5+8N)
-moment model.
For a summary of various possible models, see Section
VIII.4
with Tables
VIII.1
&
VIII.2
.

Of course, the model of Braginskii can be generalized in many different ways. Naturally, one might focus at the case of
one-ion electron plasma considered by Braginskii, and increase the order of
N
to study
convergence of transport coefficients with higher-order Laguerre (Hermite) schemes. Several studies of this kind were done in the past
(some numerically imprecise, some considering only unmagnetized plasmas, and some only ion charge
Z_{i}=1
).
For example before Braginskii,
Landshoff 1949
;
Landshoff 1951
calculated several transport coefficients with models from
N=1
to
N=4
.

Kaneko 1960
improved the numerical accuracy of Landshoff and also considered
N=5
.

Kaneko & Taguchi 1978
;
Kaneko & Yamao 1980
performed large calculations with up to a
N=49
.
Perhaps the most comprehensive study to this date was done by
Ji & Held 2013
,
who studied convergence of all the transport coefficients with up to
N=160
. Other useful references can be found in

Epperlein & Haines 1986
. The last two studies emphasize that while the transport coefficients parallel to the magnetic field (or for unmagnetized plasma)
converge rapidly for
N\geq 2
, this is not the case for some perpendicular transport coefficients.
For clarity, in the famous work of
Spitzer & Härm 1953
and the previous work of
Cohen et al. 1950
where only unmagnetized plasma is considered
and viscosity-tensors are neglected,
the perturbation

\chi_{a}
which satisfies the Landau equation was found numerically, and
the model thus technically corresponds to
N=\infty
. Their work is criticized (even though a bit unfairly)
in the monograph of
Balescu 1988
(Part 1, p. 266), who calculated all the usual transport coefficients with the moment method of Grad for the

N=2
and
N=3
cases (i.e. the 21-moment model and the 29-moment model). Note that the 3-Laguerre calculations of
Balescu 1988
were
shown to be incorrect by
Ji & Held 2013
, who were able to trace the problem to his analytic collisional matrices (they have also corrected
coefficients in collisional matrices of
Braginskii 1958
, which were fortunately not used in his
N=2
calculation).
That there is a problem with the
N=3
transport coefficients of
Balescu 1988

can be also seen for example by comparison with
Kaneko 1960
.
Here we focus at the 2-Laguerre approximation used by
Braginskii 1965
, i.e. the 21-moment model,
with the goal to extract more physical information from that scheme.

For the 5-moment model and the 13-moment model, the method of Grad was explored in great detail by
Burgers 1969
and

Schunk 1975
;
Schunk 1977
(see other references therein). The Boltzmann operator was used and several interaction potentials were considered,
such as collisions between neutral particles (hard sphere interaction), between charges (Coulomb interaction), or an induced dipole interaction
when an ion polarizes a colliding neutral (so called Maxwell molecule interaction).
These models have two important properties that the
Braginskii model does not have: 1) because the formulation is with evolution equations for stress-tensors
and heat fluxes rather than with quasi-static approximation, these models do not become divergent if a regime of low collisionality is encountered; 2)
the formulation is as a general multi-fluid description with arbitrary masses
m_{a}
,
m_{b}
and temperatures
T_{a}
,
T_{b}
.
Note that the review paper of
Braginskii 1965
also contains Section 7 about multi-component plasmas that is
often implicitly cited in the solar literature, but this section should be viewed as heuristic from a perspective that no
heat fluxes or stress-tensors were calculated. In plasma physics, the work of
Braginskii 1958
;
Braginskii 1965
is celebrated
for his results for a one ion-electron plasma.
Here we use the Landau operator and consider only Coulomb collisions. Nevertheless, we will employ the
21-moment model, and we thus improve the precision of the 13-moment model of
Burgers 1969
-
Schunk 1977
for this interaction potential,
so that the precision matches Braginskii. We will use a restriction that the relative drift velocity between two colliding species
must be small in comparison to their thermal speeds. The same restriction applies for the Braginskii model, for the Burgers-Schunk 13-moment model
(the exception is the Maxwell molecule interaction) and higher-order schemes.
For Coulomb collisions and hard-sphere collisions, only the simplest 5-moment model has been calculated
fully analytically without this restriction
(
Burgers 1969
;
Tanenbaum 1967
;
Schunk 1977
)
, yielding the runaway effect.

Several various multi-fluid descriptions with the level of precision of Braginskii have been considered in the past;
see for example
Hinton 1983
,
Zhdanov 2002
(orig. pub. 1982),

Ji & Held 2006
(who actually consider general
N
),
Simakov & Molvig 2014
;
Simakov & Molvig 2016a
;
Simakov & Molvig 2016b
, or for the case of neoclassical theory (toroidal geometry
applicable to tokamaks) see
Hirshman & Sigmar 1977
;
Hirshman & Sigmar 1981
.
Our model seems to be very close to the model of
Zhdanov 2002
, Chapter 8.1, who indeed uses the method of Grad and
calculates the 21-moment model with it. We did not verify full equivalence because of his puzzling notation.
Even if equivalence is eventually shown for the case of small temperature differences between ions,
we consider a more general case where temperatures of all the species are arbitrary.
Our clear formulation with fluid moments (instead of Hermite moments) might be also easier to implement into existing numerical
codes. Arbitrary temperatures were also considered by
Ji & Held 2006
, but we did not verify equivalence with their model either.
We only verified equivalence with their model for the special case of a one ion-electron plasma with small temperature differences of Braginskii,
by using collisional matrices from
Ji & Held 2013
.

Additionally, for all the considered moments we provide the left-hand-sides of our evolution equations in a fully non-linear form,
which is important for direct numerical simulations and which are not typically given. An important difference then arises even at the linear level, because
calculations are typically performed with de-coupled viscosity-tensors and heat fluxes, meaning that the two viscosity-tensors interact only with each other, and the
two heat fluxes interact only with each other. We consider coupling between heat fluxes and stress-tensors,
where (even at the linear level in a quasi-static approximation) a heat flux enters a stress-tensor and a stress-tensor enters a heat flux.
Such couplings are often considered in the collisionless regime; see e.g.

Macmahon 1965
,
Mikhailovskii & Smolyakov 1985
,
Ramos 2005
,
Goswami et al. 2005
,
Passot et al. 2012
,
Hunana et al. 2019a
;
Hunana et al. 2019b
,
where the effect is important for example for the perpendicular fast mode, or for the growth rate of the firehose instability (see e.g. Figure 10
in
Hunana et al. 2019b
). The coupling might be important also in the highly-collisional regime if sufficiently high frequencies
(or short wavelengths) are considered. The coupling was neglected by
Braginskii 1958
;
Braginskii 1965
and
Spitzer & Härm 1953
;
Spitzer 1962
,
and as an example we consider unmagnetized one ion-electron plasma in detail,
and we provide stress-tensors and heat fluxes where this coupling is taken into account.

The coupling between viscosity-tensors and heat fluxes then inevitably leads to the next step, by replacing equation (
1
) with

f_{a}=f_{a}^{(0)}(1+\chi_{a});\qquad\chi_{a}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Big[{h}^{(2n)}_{ij}{H}^{(2n)}_{ij}+{h}^{(2n+1)}_{i}{H}^{(2n+1)}_{i}+h^{(2n)}H^{(2n)}\Big],

(2)

where the scalar hermite moments
h^{(2n)}
can be viewed as fully contracted (scalar) perturbations of fluid moments.
The lowest-order moment
h^{(2)}=0
and all higher-order ones are generally non-zero. Thus, prescribing
N=1
still yields the 13-moment model, however,
precribing
N=2
now yields the 22-moment model. This model is a natural extension of the Braginskii model, because
it takes into account fully contracted perturbations

\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a}=m_{a}\int|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{4}(f_{a}-f_{a}^{(0)})d^{3}v
of the 4th-order fluid moment.
Accounting for the scalar perturbations according to (
2
), for
N\geq 1
an
N
-Laguerre model then represents a
(4+9N)
-moment model.
Another possibility for writing the equation (
2
) is to separate the matrices

\sum_{n=1}^{N}{h}^{(2n)}_{ij}{H}^{(2n)}_{ij}
, and for the vectors and scalars to write the sum from
n=0
, with an imposed requirement that

h^{(0)}=0
;
h^{(2)}=0
&
h^{(1)}_{i}=0
(where the first one is non-trivial).
This is for example the choice of
Balescu 1988
, p. 174, his equations (3.11) & (3.16).

Finally, the main purpose of this work is to make
the moment method of Grad and the exciting work of Braginskii more understandable, which is reflected in our relatively lengthy Appendix.

The entire paper is separated into eight Sections and fourteen Appendices. The main paper summarizes the obtained results,
while the Appendices provide the detailed calculations.

In Section
II
, we formulate the entire 21-moment model. We start with a formulation valid for a general collisional operator
C(f_{a})
, where both the
left-hand-sides and the collisional right-hand-sides of evolution equations are given in a fully non-linear form.
We then provide collisional contributions for arbitrary masses and temperatures calculated with the Landau operator. Collisional contributions
are calculated in the usual semi-linear approximation where relative drifts between species are small in comparison to their thermal speeds
(i.e. the runaway effect is not considered),
and the product of
f_{a}f_{b}
is approximated as
f_{a}f_{b}=f_{a}^{(0)}f_{b}^{(0)}(1+\chi_{a}+\chi_{b})
, where the “cross”-contributions
\chi_{a}\chi_{b}
are neglected.
We then provide a simplified model where differences in temperatures between species are small.
For clarity, we also reduce our model to the 13-moment model and we provide a formulation that is more compact than the one given by

Burgers 1969
-
Schunk 1977
(because we only consider Coulomb collisions). We then simplify the evolution equations of our
21-moment model into a semi-linear approximation where viscosity-tensors and heat fluxes are de-coupled, and these are used in Sections 3 & 4.

In Section
III
, we compare our model to
Braginskii 1965
by considering a one ion electron plasma with
similar temperatures, i.e. where the temperature differences between species are
small with respect to their mean values.
We provide all the transport coefficients in a fully analytic form, and we verify the entire Table II of
Braginskii 1965

(two of his coefficients are not precise). Parallel electron coefficients (or equivalently for an unmagnetized plasma), can be also found in
Simakov & Molvig 2014
.
We also provide analytic results for the viscosity of the 4th-order fluid moment and the heat flux of the 5th-order fluid moment, which are not
typically given.

In Section
IV
, we use the idea of for example
Hinton 1983
;
Zhdanov 2002
;
Simakov & Molvig 2014
that because of the smallness of electron/ion mass ratios,
the
electron
coefficients of Braginskii can be straightforwardly generalized to multiple ion species by introducing an
effective ion charge and effective ion velocity. All the electron analytic coefficients that are given in Section 3 are thus generalized to multi-ion species with
a simple transformation.

In Section
V
, we discuss the coupling between viscosity tensors and heat fluxes.
We provide evolution equations in the semi-linear approximation where this coupling is retained, and we introduce a technique
on how to split the moments into their first and second orders.

In Section
VI
, we consider an example of an unmagnetized one ion-electron plasma and explicitly calculate the coupling of stress-tensors and
heat fluxes. All the results are given in a fully analytic form, as well as with numerical values for the ion charge
Z_{i}=1
.

In Section
VII
, we first formulate the fully non-linear 22-moment model for a general collisional operator. We then provide
the multi-fluid collisional contributions calculated with the Landau operator in the semi-linear approximation,
and we show that the perturbations
\widetilde{X}^{(4)}
modify the energy exchange rates. We also provide quasi-static solutions for a one ion-electron plasma,
and we show that the perturbations
\widetilde{X}^{(4)}
have their own heat conductivities.

In Section
VIII
, we discuss various topics. 1) We discuss energy conservation. 2) We clarify that from a multi-fluid perspective
the Braginskii choice of ion collisional time
\tau_{i}
should be interpreted as
\tau_{i}=\tau_{ii}
, and not as
\tau_{i}=\sqrt{2}\tau_{ii}
.
3) To clarify the higher-order schemes and to double-check our evolution equations,
we calculate the fluid hierarchy for a general
N
, with an unspecified collisional operator.
4) We discuss irreducible and reducible Hermite polynomials and show that both yield the same results.
5) We provide fully non-linear Rosenbluth potentials for the 22-moment model, which might be useful in further studies of the runaway effect with this
scheme. 6) We discuss Hermite closures and their relation to fluid closures, which are required to close the fluid hierarchy.
We also correct our previous erroneous interpretation that Landau fluid closures are necessary to go beyond the 4th-order moment.
7) We discuss the inclusion of gravity.
8) We use our multi-fluid formulation to double-check the precision of
m_{e}/m_{i}
expansions. We consider unmagnetized proton-electron plasma, and calculate
the transport coefficients exactly, without using the smallness of
m_{e}/m_{i}
.
9) We discuss limitations of our approach. 10) We provide conclusions, with examples where our model might be useful.

Appendix
A
introduces the general concept of tensorial fluid moments and provides an
evolution equation for an n-th-order fluid moment
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(n)}_{a}
in presence of a general (unspecified) collisional operator, equation (
A12
).
This evolution equation also remains valid in presence of gravity; see the discussion in Section
VIII.7
.

Appendix
B
introduces the tensorial Hermite polynomials of
Grad 1949a
;
Grad 1949b
;
Grad 1958
, and discusses in detail the construction
of perturbations around the Maxwellian distribution function, i.e. equations (
1
) & (
2
),
which are summarized in Tables
VIII.1
and
VIII.2
. The construction of Hermite closures is addressed as well.

Appendix
C
derives evolution equations for the 22-moment model (for an unspecified collisional operator), by applying contractions
at the evolution equations from Appendix
A
and by using decomposition of moments and Hermite closures from Appendix
B
.

Appendix
D
uses a different technique and instead of applying contractions at equations of Appendix
A
,
simplified fluid hierarchy of a general n-th-order is obtained directly, which only consists of evolution equations for scalars, vectors and matrices. Evaluation
of these equations for a specific “n” recovers the 22-moment equations of Appendix
C
.

Appendix
E
introduces the BGK (relaxation-type) collisional operator of
Bhatnagar et al. 1954
;
Gross & Krook 1956
, which greatly
clarifies the analytic forms of the Braginskii viscosity-tensors and heat fluxes. Viscosities and heat conductivities
of both models are directly compared in Figures
3
-
5
. The nonlinear solution for the
viscosity-tensor (with respect to a general direction of magnetic field
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}
)
is addressed in Appendix
E.4
, and Appendix
E.6
clarifies
the ambipolar diffusion between two ion species.

Appendix
F
introduces a general (unspecified) Fokker-Planck collisional operator with its
dynamical friction vector
\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}
and diffusion tensor
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}
. General relations for the collisional integrals (of n-th-order) are provided,
which can be used once the
\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}
&
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}
are specified.

Appendix
G
introduces the Landau collisional operator, where the
\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}
&
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}
are expressed in the usual
form through the Rosenbluth potentials. The 5-moment model (strict Maxwellians) is then considered, and the usual collisional momentum exchange rates
\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}
and
energy exchange rates
Q_{ab}
with the assumption of small drifts between species are derived in detail in Appendices
G.1
&
G.2
.
Both contributions are then re-calculated with unrestricted drifts in Appendix
G.3
, where instead of the Rosenbluth potentials, the
“center-of-mass” transformation typically used with the Boltzmann collisional operator has to be used, because the collisional integrals seem to be too complicated
to calculate directly. This is further discussed in Appendix
G.4
.

Appendix
H
considers the 8-moment model, where the simplest heat flux is present,
and the multi-fluid model of
Burgers 1969
-
Schunk 1977
is calculated in detail.
For a direct comparison with Braginskii, a one ion-electron plasma is then considered and quasi-static heat fluxes, together with the
resulting momentum exchange rates are obtained as well. It is shown that in the limit of strong magnetic field, the perpendicular and
cross-conductivities
\kappa_{\perp}
&
\kappa_{\times}
match the Braginskii model exactly (for both the ion and electron species) and
only the parallel conductivities
\kappa_{\parallel}
are different.

Appendix
I
compares the parallel heat fluxes and momentum exchange rates of
Braginskii 1965

with models of
Burgers 1969
-
Schunk 1977
,
Killie et al. 2004
,
Landshoff 1949
;
Landshoff 1951
and
Spitzer & Härm 1953
,
see Tables
I.1
-
I.5
. Useful conversion relations for the results of
Kaneko 1960
and
Balescu 1988
are
provided as well. The notation of
Spitzer & Härm 1953
is clarified in Appendix
I.1
and it is shown
that their model, as well as the model of
Killie et al. 2004
, break the Onsager symmetry.

Appendix
J
calculates in detail the 10-moment multi-fluid model of
Burgers 1969
-
Schunk 1977
,
where the simplest viscosity-tensor is present. It is shown that in the limit of strong magnetic field, the perpendicular viscosities and
gyroviscosities
\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3},\eta_{4}
match the Braginskii model exactly (for both the ion and electron species) and only the
parallel viscosities
\eta_{0}
are different.

Appendix
K
calculates in detail the momentum exchange rates and
collisional contributions for the heat fluxes in our 21 & 22-moment multi-fluid models.
The calculations are shown on the 11-moment model where only the heat fluxes are present (and viscosities
and scalar perturbation are absent), because in the semi-linear approximation the calculations can be split.
Similarly, collisional contributions for viscosity-tensors are calculated in Appendix
L
, and contributions for the scalar perturbation of the 4th-order moment
in Appendix
M
.

Appendix
N
uses our 21-moment model and calculates heat conductivities
and viscosities for two examples of an unmagnetized plasma consisting of two ion species (collisions with electrons are neglected).
The first example (Appendix
N.1
) is a plasma consisting of protons and alpha-particles (fully ionized Helium), typical in astrophysical applications.
The second example (Appendix
N.2
) is a deuterium-tritium plasma used in plasma fusion.

II
Multi-fluid generalization of Braginskii (21-moment model)

Our model is formulated with heat flux vectors

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(3)}_{a}=m_{a}\int\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2}f_{a}d^{3}v=2\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a};\qquad\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}=m_{a}\int\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{4}f_{a}d^{3}v,

(3)

and traceless viscosity tensors

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{a}\int\big(\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2}\big)f_{a}d^{3}v;\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}=m_{a}\int\big(\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2}\big)|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2}f_{a}d^{3}v,

(4)

where the fluctuating velocity
\boldsymbol{c}_{a}=\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}
, and “a” is the species index.
We are using free wording because
\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}
is not really
a heat flux and
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}
is not really a viscosity-tensor.
Also, we use the wording viscosity-tensor and stress-tensor interchangeably in the entire text.
The species indices are moved freely up and down.
We also define the usual rate-of-strain tensor
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}=(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})^{S}-(2/3)\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}
, symmetric operator
A_{ij}^{S}=A_{ij}+A_{ji}
,
and gravitational acceleration
\boldsymbol{G}
.
All other definitions are addressed in Appendix
A
.
We note that the definition of heat flux in equation (1.21) of
Braginskii 1965
contains two well-known misprints
with prime symbols missing on his fluctuating velocities
\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}
. The heat flux is defined correctly in
Braginskii 1958
.

We first present a formulation with a general (unspecified) collisional operator
C(f_{a})
. We define (tensorial) collisional contributions

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{a}=m_{a}\int\boldsymbol{v}C(f_{a})d^{3}v;\qquad Q_{a}=\frac{m_{a}}{2}\int|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2}C(f_{a})d^{3}v;

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(2)}_{a}=m_{a}\int\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}C(f_{a})d^{3}v;\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(3)}_{a}=m_{a}\int\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}C(f_{a})d^{3}v;

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(4)}_{a}=m_{a}\int\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}C(f_{a})d^{3}v;\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(5)}_{a}=m_{a}\int\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}C(f_{a})d^{3}v,

(5)

where
\boldsymbol{R}_{a}
are the usual momentum exchange rates and
Q_{a}
are the usual energy exchange rates.
Then it can be shown that integration of the Boltzmann equation yields the following nonlinear
21-moment model (see details in Appendix
C
), where the basic evolution equations read

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}n_{a}+n_{a}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}=0;

(6)

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}-\boldsymbol{G}-\frac{eZ_{a}}{m_{a}}\Big(\boldsymbol{E}+\frac{1}{c}\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\times\boldsymbol{B}\Big)=\frac{\boldsymbol{R}_{a}}{\rho_{a}};

(7)

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}p_{a}+\frac{5}{3}p_{a}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\frac{2}{3}\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}:(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})=\frac{2}{3}Q_{a},

(8)

and are accompanied by evolution equations for the stress-tensors and heat flux vectors

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}}{dt}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}\big)^{S}+\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\big)^{S}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{2}{5}\Big[(\nabla\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a})^{S}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\Big]+p_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(2)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}\equiv\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(2)}_{a}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(2)}_{a};

(9)

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}}{dt}+\frac{7}{5}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\frac{7}{5}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\frac{2}{5}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\frac{5}{2}p_{a}\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}-\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}

\displaystyle\qquad=\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(3)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(3)}_{a}-\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a};

(10)

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}+\frac{1}{5}\Big[(\nabla\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a})^{S}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a})\Big]+\frac{9}{7}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}+\frac{9}{7}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})^{S}

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{7}\big((\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}\big)^{S}-\frac{22}{21}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})+\Omega_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}\big)^{S}+7\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}

\displaystyle-\,\frac{14}{5\rho_{a}}\Big[\big((\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\big)^{S}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\Big]

\displaystyle=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(4)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}\equiv\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(4)}_{a}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\textrm{Tr}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(4)}_{a}-\frac{14}{5\rho_{a}}\Big[(\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a})^{S}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a})\Big];

(11)

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}+\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(6)}_{a}+\frac{9}{5}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a})+\frac{9}{5}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\frac{4}{5}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}

\displaystyle+70\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}}\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)-35\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}^{2}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}-\frac{4}{\rho_{a}}\big(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}\big)\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}

\displaystyle=\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(5)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}\equiv\textrm{Tr}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(5)}_{a}-35\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}^{2}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}-\frac{4}{\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}.

(12)

The last equation is closed with a fluid closure (derived from a Hermite closure)

\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(6)}_{a}=m_{a}\int\big(\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2}\big)|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{4}f_{a}d^{3}v=18\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}-63\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}^{2}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}.

(13)

The system above thus represents a generalized model of
Braginskii 1965
, where evolution equations for all the moments are
fully non-linear and valid for a general collisional operator
C(f_{a})
. It is a 21-moment model (1 density,
3 velocity, 1 scalar pressure, 3 for each heat flux vector, 5 for each viscosity tensor).

II.1
Collisional contributions (arbitrary masses and temperatures)

We use the Landau collisional operator.
All collisional contributions are evaluated in a semi-linear approximation, with an assumption that differences in drift velocities
\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}
are small
with respect to thermal velocities. All the non-linear quantities such as
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a})
, including
|\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|^{2}
are thus neglected in the multi-fluid
description, which is consistent with models of
Burgers 1969
and
Schunk 1977
. For energy conservation and a particular
case of a one ion-electron plasma, see Section
VIII.1
. The wording semi-linear just means that expressions containing pressures and densities
such as
(p_{a}/\rho_{a})\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}
are retained and not fully linearized with their mean pressure/density values.
However, for example the last terms of collisional contributions in the equations (
10
), (
11
), (
12
) proportional
to
\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}
,
\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}
and
\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}
are neglected in the semi-linear approximation.

We introduce the usual reduced mass and reduced temperature

\mu_{ab}=\frac{m_{a}m_{b}}{m_{a}+m_{b}};\qquad T_{ab}=\frac{m_{a}T_{b}+m_{b}T_{a}}{m_{a}+m_{b}},

(14)

together with collisional frequency (
178
). The momentum exchange rates are given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{b\neq a}\nu_{ab}\Big\{\rho_{a}(\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a})+\frac{\mu_{ab}}{T_{ab}}\Big[V_{ab(1)}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}-V_{ab(2)}\frac{\rho_{a}}{\rho_{b}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\Big]

(15)

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{56}\Big(\frac{\mu_{ab}}{T_{ab}}\Big)^{2}\Big[\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}-\frac{\rho_{a}}{\rho_{b}}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{b}\Big]\Big\},

with coefficients that include both masses and temperatures, but which we simply call “mass-ratio coefficients”

\displaystyle V_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(21/10)T_{a}m_{b}+(3/5)T_{b}m_{a}}{T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a}};\qquad V_{ab(2)}=\frac{(3/5)T_{a}m_{b}+(21/10)T_{b}m_{a}}{T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a}}.

(16)

These and other mass-ratio coefficients given below come
from the Landau collisional operator introduced in Appendices
F
&
G
, where
one uses perturbed distribution functions of the 21-moment model; see Section
VIII.4
and Appendix
B
,
with calculations of the collisional integrals in Appendices
K
&
L
.
Energy exchange rates are given by

Q_{a}=\sum_{b\neq a}3\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}\frac{T_{b}-T_{a}}{m_{a}+m_{b}},

(17)

where
|\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|^{2}
are neglected as discussed above.
The heat flux exchange rates are given by

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(3)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Big[2\nu_{aa}+\sum_{b\neq a}\nu_{ab}\hat{D}_{ab(1)}\Big]\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\sum_{b\neq a}\nu_{ab}\hat{D}_{ab(2)}\frac{\rho_{a}}{\rho_{b}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}

(18)

\displaystyle+\Big[\frac{3}{70}\nu_{aa}+\sum_{b\neq a}\nu_{ab}\hat{E}_{ab(1)}\Big]\frac{\rho_{a}}{p_{a}}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}-\sum_{b\neq a}\nu_{ab}\hat{E}_{ab(2)}\frac{\rho_{b}}{p_{b}}\frac{\rho_{a}}{\rho_{b}}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{b}

\displaystyle-p_{a}\sum_{b\neq a}\nu_{ab}(\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\hat{U}_{ab(1)},

with mass-ratio coefficients

\displaystyle\hat{U}_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3m_{b}(3T_{a}m_{a}+T_{a}m_{b}-2T_{b}m_{a})}{2(T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a})(m_{a}+m_{b})};

\displaystyle\hat{D}_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\{75T_{a}^{3}m_{a}m_{b}^{3}+95T_{a}^{3}m_{b}^{4}+174T_{a}^{2}T_{b}m_{a}m_{b}^{3}+300T_{a}T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{3}m_{b}+498T_{a}T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{2}m_{b}^{2}+60T_{b}^{3}m_{a}^{4}

\displaystyle+104T_{b}^{3}m_{a}^{3}m_{b}\big\}\big[20(T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a})^{3}(m_{a}+m_{b})\big]^{-1};

\displaystyle\hat{D}_{ab(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{9T_{a}m_{b}^{2}(10T_{a}^{2}m_{a}m_{b}+6T_{a}^{2}m_{b}^{2}+45T_{a}T_{b}m_{a}^{2}+27T_{a}T_{b}m_{a}m_{b}-14T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{2})}{20(T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a})^{3}(m_{a}+m_{b})};

\displaystyle\hat{E}_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3T_{a}m_{b}(19T_{a}^{2}m_{a}m_{b}^{2}+23T_{a}^{2}m_{b}^{3}-2T_{a}T_{b}m_{a}^{2}m_{b}+36T_{a}T_{b}m_{a}m_{b}^{2}+84T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{3}+118T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{2}m_{b})}{560(T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a})^{3}(m_{a}+m_{b})};

\displaystyle\hat{E}_{ab(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{9T_{a}T_{b}m_{a}m_{b}^{2}(7T_{a}m_{a}+5T_{a}m_{b}-2T_{b}m_{a})}{112(T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a})^{3}(m_{a}+m_{b})}.

(19)

The 5th-order moment exchange rates are given by

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(5)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Big[\frac{76}{5}\nu_{aa}+\sum_{b\neq a}\nu_{ab}\hat{F}_{ab(1)}\Big]\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\sum_{b\neq a}\nu_{ab}\hat{F}_{ab(2)}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\frac{\rho_{a}}{\rho_{b}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}

(20)

\displaystyle-\Big[\frac{3}{35}\nu_{aa}+\sum_{b\neq a}\nu_{ab}\hat{G}_{ab(1)}\Big]\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}-\sum_{b\neq a}\nu_{ab}\hat{G}_{ab(2)}\frac{p_{a}}{p_{b}}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{b}

\displaystyle-\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}}\sum_{b\neq a}\nu_{ab}(\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\hat{U}_{ab(2)},

with mass-ratio coefficients

\displaystyle\hat{U}_{ab(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3m_{b}(17T_{a}^{2}m_{a}m_{b}+9T_{a}^{2}m_{b}^{2}+42T_{a}T_{b}m_{a}^{2}+6T_{a}T_{b}m_{a}m_{b}-28T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{2})}{(T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a})^{2}(m_{a}+m_{b})};

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\{855T_{a}^{5}m_{a}m_{b}^{4}+759T_{a}^{5}m_{b}^{5}+2340T_{a}^{4}T_{b}m_{a}^{2}m_{b}^{3}+1972T_{a}^{4}T_{b}m_{a}m_{b}^{4}+2640T_{a}^{3}T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{3}m_{b}^{2}

\displaystyle+2332T_{a}^{3}T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{2}m_{b}^{3}+5880T_{a}^{2}T_{b}^{3}m_{a}^{4}m_{b}+3324T_{a}^{2}T_{b}^{3}m_{a}^{3}m_{b}^{2}-3080T_{a}T_{b}^{4}m_{a}^{4}m_{b}-560T_{b}^{5}m_{a}^{5}\big\}

\displaystyle\times\big[10(T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a})^{4}(m_{a}+m_{b})T_{a}\big]^{-1};

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{ab(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3T_{a}m_{b}^{2}\big\{70T_{a}^{3}m_{a}m_{b}^{2}+102T_{a}^{3}m_{b}^{3}+385T_{a}^{2}T_{b}m_{a}^{2}m_{b}+561T_{a}^{2}T_{b}m_{a}m_{b}^{2}+1890T_{a}T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{3}

\displaystyle+1446T_{a}T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{2}m_{b}-588T_{b}^{3}m_{a}^{3}\big\}\big[10(T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a})^{4}(m_{a}+m_{b})\big]^{-1};

\displaystyle\hat{G}_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\big\{565T_{a}^{4}m_{a}m_{b}^{4}+533T_{a}^{4}m_{b}^{5}+1270T_{a}^{3}T_{b}m_{a}^{2}m_{b}^{3}+1190T_{a}^{3}T_{b}m_{a}m_{b}^{4}+1020T_{a}^{2}T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{3}m_{b}^{2}

\displaystyle+1152T_{a}^{2}T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{2}m_{b}^{3}+3640T_{a}T_{b}^{3}m_{a}^{4}m_{b}+1916T_{a}T_{b}^{3}m_{a}^{3}m_{b}^{2}-1400T_{b}^{4}m_{a}^{5}-3304T_{b}^{4}m_{a}^{4}m_{b}\big\}

\displaystyle\times\big[280(T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a})^{4}(m_{a}+m_{b})\big]^{-1};

\displaystyle\hat{G}_{ab(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\,\frac{3T_{a}T_{b}m_{a}m_{b}^{2}(3T_{a}^{2}m_{a}m_{b}-5T_{a}^{2}m_{b}^{2}-42T_{a}T_{b}m_{a}^{2}-38T_{a}T_{b}m_{a}m_{b}+12T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{2})}{8(T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a})^{4}(m_{a}+m_{b})}.

(21)

Exchange rates for the usual stress-tensor are given by

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{a}^{(2)}\,{}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\,\frac{21}{10}\nu_{aa}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}+\frac{9}{70}\nu_{aa}\frac{\rho_{a}}{p_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(4)}

(22)

\displaystyle+\sum_{b\neq a}\frac{\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}\Big[-\hat{K}_{ab(1)}\frac{1}{n_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}+\hat{K}_{ab(2)}\frac{1}{n_{b}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}

\displaystyle+L_{ab(1)}\frac{\rho_{a}}{n_{a}p_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(4)}-L_{ab(2)}\frac{\rho_{b}}{n_{b}p_{b}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(4)}\Big],

with mass-ratio coefficients

\displaystyle\hat{K}_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{10T_{a}^{2}m_{a}m_{b}^{2}+15T_{a}^{2}m_{b}^{3}+35T_{a}T_{b}m_{a}^{2}m_{b}+42T_{a}T_{b}m_{a}m_{b}^{2}+10T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{3}+12T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{2}m_{b}}{5(T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a})^{2}m_{a}};

\displaystyle\hat{K}_{ab(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{6T_{a}^{2}m_{a}m_{b}+4T_{a}^{2}m_{b}^{2}+21T_{a}T_{b}m_{a}^{2}+14T_{a}T_{b}m_{a}m_{b}-5T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{2}}{5(T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a})^{2}};

\displaystyle L_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3T_{a}m_{b}(2T_{a}m_{a}m_{b}+3T_{a}m_{b}^{2}+7T_{b}m_{a}^{2}+8T_{b}m_{a}m_{b})}{35(T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a})^{2}m_{a}};

\displaystyle L_{ab(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3m_{a}T_{b}(5T_{a}m_{a}+4T_{a}m_{b}-T_{b}m_{a})}{35(T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a})^{2}}.

(23)

Finally, the 4th-order stress-tensor exchange rates are given by

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(4)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\,\frac{53}{20}\nu_{aa}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}-\frac{79}{140}\nu_{aa}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}+\sum_{b\neq a}\nu_{ab}\Big[-\hat{M}_{ab(1)}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}

(24)

\displaystyle+\hat{M}_{ab(2)}\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}p_{b}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{b}-N_{ab(1)}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}-N_{ab(2)}\frac{p_{a}^{2}\rho_{b}}{p_{b}^{2}\rho_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{b}\Big],

with mass-ratio coefficients

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\{48T_{a}^{4}m_{a}m_{b}^{3}+36T_{a}^{4}m_{b}^{4}+216T_{a}^{3}T_{b}m_{a}^{2}m_{b}^{2}+107T_{a}^{3}T_{b}m_{a}m_{b}^{3}+378T_{a}^{2}T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{3}m_{b}

\displaystyle+36T_{a}^{2}T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{2}m_{b}^{2}-315T_{a}T_{b}^{3}m_{a}^{3}m_{b}-70T_{b}^{4}m_{a}^{4}\Big\}\Big[5(T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a})^{3}T_{a}(m_{b}+m_{a})\Big]^{-1};

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{ab(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\,\Big\{T_{b}m_{a}\big(18T_{a}^{3}m_{a}m_{b}^{2}-4T_{a}^{3}m_{b}^{3}+81T_{a}^{2}T_{b}m_{a}^{2}m_{b}-18T_{a}^{2}T_{b}m_{a}m_{b}^{2}-147T_{a}T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{3}

\displaystyle-189T_{a}T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{2}m_{b}+35T_{b}^{3}m_{a}^{3}\big)\Big\}\Big[5(T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a})^{3}T_{a}(m_{b}+m_{a})\Big]^{-1};

\displaystyle N_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\,\Big\{16T_{a}^{3}m_{a}m_{b}^{3}+12T_{a}^{3}m_{b}^{4}+72T_{a}^{2}T_{b}m_{a}^{2}m_{b}^{2}+21T_{a}^{2}T_{b}m_{a}m_{b}^{3}+126T_{a}T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{3}m_{b}

\displaystyle\quad-54T_{a}T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{2}m_{b}^{2}-140T_{b}^{3}m_{a}^{4}-273T_{b}^{3}m_{a}^{3}m_{b}\Big\}\Big[35(T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a})^{3}(m_{b}+m_{a})\Big]^{-1};

\displaystyle N_{ab(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\,\frac{3T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{2}(35T_{a}^{2}m_{a}m_{b}+12T_{a}^{2}m_{b}^{2}-35T_{a}T_{b}m_{a}^{2}-51T_{a}T_{b}m_{a}m_{b}+7T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{2})}{35(T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a})^{3}T_{a}(m_{b}+m_{a})}.

(25)

The entire system is now fully specified, and represents a multi-fluid generalization of the model of
Braginskii 1965
.
Coupled with Maxwell’s equations, it can be used in multi-fluid numerical simulations.
Importantly, when collisional frequencies become small, the right hand sides of evolution equations just
become small and no coefficients become divergent, which is in contrast to the model of Braginskii,
where the quasi-static approximation is used for the stress-tensors and heat fluxes.
For a detailed discussion on the limitations of our model in a regime of low-collisionality, see Section
VIII.9
.
The model of Braginskii is obtained as a particular case
of a one ion-electron plasma with similar temperatures,
in a quasi-static and quasi-linear approximation for the viscosity tensors and heat fluxes, where additionally,
the coupling between viscosity tensors and heat fluxes is neglected.

II.2
Collisional contributions for small temperature differences

In many instances, it might be satisfactory to consider a situation when the temperature differences between species are small.
The mass-ratio coefficients (
16
) then become

\displaystyle V_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(21/10)m_{b}+(3/5)m_{a}}{m_{b}+m_{a}};\qquad V_{ab(2)}=\frac{(3/5)m_{b}+(21/10)m_{a}}{m_{b}+m_{a}},

(26)

mass-ratio coefficients (
19
) simplify into

\displaystyle\hat{D}_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3m_{a}^{3}+(86/5)m_{a}^{2}m_{b}+(77/10)m_{a}m_{b}^{2}+(19/4)m_{b}^{3}}{(m_{a}+m_{b})^{3}};

\displaystyle\hat{D}_{ab(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(279/20)m_{a}m_{b}^{2}+(27/10)m_{b}^{3}}{(m_{a}+m_{b})^{3}};

\displaystyle\hat{E}_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(9/20)m_{a}^{2}m_{b}+(6/35)m_{a}m_{b}^{2}+(69/560)m_{b}^{3}}{(m_{a}+m_{b})^{3}};

\displaystyle\hat{E}_{ab(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(45/112)m_{a}m_{b}^{2}}{(m_{a}+m_{b})^{3}};\qquad\hat{U}_{ab(1)}=\frac{3}{2}\frac{m_{b}}{(m_{a}+m_{b})},

(27)

mass-ratio coefficients (
21
) become

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(-56)m_{a}^{4}+336m_{a}^{3}m_{b}+(1302/5)m_{a}^{2}m_{b}^{2}+(1034/5)m_{a}m_{b}^{3}+(759/10)m_{b}^{4}}{(m_{a}+m_{b})^{4}};

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{ab(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(1953/5)m_{a}^{2}m_{b}^{2}+(1587/10)m_{a}m_{b}^{3}+(153/5)m_{b}^{4}}{(m_{a}+m_{b})^{4}};

\displaystyle\hat{G}_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{5m_{a}^{4}-(31/5)m_{a}^{3}m_{b}-(30/7)m_{a}^{2}m_{b}^{2}-(611/140)m_{a}m_{b}^{3}-(533/280)m_{b}^{4}}{(m_{a}+m_{b})^{4}};

\displaystyle\hat{G}_{ab(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(45/4)m_{a}^{2}m_{b}^{2}+(15/8)m_{a}m_{b}^{3}}{(m_{a}+m_{b})^{4}};\qquad\hat{U}_{ab(2)}=\frac{42m_{a}m_{b}+27m_{b}^{2}}{(m_{a}+m_{b})^{2}},

(28)

mass-ratio coefficients (
23
) become

\displaystyle\hat{K}_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{10m_{a}^{2}+37m_{a}m_{b}+15m_{b}^{2}}{5m_{a}(m_{b}+m_{a})};\qquad\hat{K}_{ab(2)}=\frac{4(4m_{a}+m_{b})}{5(m_{b}+m_{a})};

\displaystyle L_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3(7m_{a}+3m_{b})m_{b}}{35m_{a}(m_{b}+m_{a})};\qquad L_{ab(2)}=\frac{12m_{a}}{35(m_{a}+m_{b})},

(29)

and mass-ratio coefficients (
25
) simplify into

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\,\frac{70m_{a}^{3}-133m_{a}^{2}m_{b}-119m_{a}m_{b}^{2}-36m_{b}^{3}}{5(m_{b}+m_{a})^{3}};\qquad\hat{M}_{ab(2)}=\frac{4m_{a}(28m_{a}^{2}-m_{a}m_{b}+m_{b}^{2})}{5(m_{b}+m_{a})^{3}};

\displaystyle N_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{140m_{a}^{3}+7m_{a}^{2}m_{b}-25m_{a}m_{b}^{2}-12m_{b}^{3}}{35(m_{b}+m_{a})^{3}};\qquad N_{ab(2)}=\frac{12m_{a}^{2}(7m_{a}-3m_{b})}{35(m_{b}+m_{a})^{3}}.

(30)

II.3
Reduction to 13-moment model

As a partial double-check of our calculations,
neglecting the evolution equations (
11
)-(
12
) for
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}
&
\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}
,
and in the evolution equations
(
9
)-(
10
) for
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}
&
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}
prescribing closures (which are derived from Hermite closures)

\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}=28\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a};\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}=7\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a},

(31)

our 21-moment model simplifies into the 13-moment model, given by collisional contributions

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{b\neq a}\nu_{ab}\Big[\rho_{a}(\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a})+\frac{3}{5}\frac{\mu_{ab}}{T_{ab}}\big(\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}-\frac{\rho_{a}}{\rho_{b}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\big)\Big];

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(3)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{4}{5}\nu_{aa}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\sum_{b\neq a}\nu_{ab}\Big[-\hat{D}^{*}_{ab(1)}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\hat{D}^{*}_{ab(2)}\frac{\rho_{a}}{\rho_{b}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}-p_{a}(\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\hat{U}_{ab(1)}\Big];

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{a}^{(2)}\,{}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\,\frac{6}{5}\nu_{aa}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}+\sum_{b\neq a}\frac{\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}\Big[-\hat{K}^{*}_{ab(1)}\frac{1}{n_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}+\hat{K}^{*}_{ab(2)}\frac{1}{n_{b}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}\Big],

(32)

with mass-ratio coefficients

\displaystyle\hat{D}^{*}_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{9T_{a}^{2}m_{a}m_{b}^{2}+13T_{a}^{2}m_{b}^{3}-6T_{a}T_{b}m_{a}^{2}m_{b}+20T_{a}T_{b}m_{a}m_{b}^{2}+30T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{3}+52T_{b}^{2}m_{a}^{2}m_{b}}{10(m_{a}+m_{b})(T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a})^{2}};

\displaystyle\hat{D}^{*}_{ab(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{9T_{a}m_{b}^{2}(5T_{a}m_{a}+3T_{a}m_{b}-2T_{b}m_{a})}{10(m_{a}+m_{b})(T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a})^{2}};

\displaystyle\hat{K}^{*}_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2(2T_{a}m_{a}m_{b}+3T_{a}m_{b}^{2}+5T_{b}m_{a}^{2}+6T_{b}m_{a}m_{b})}{5m_{a}(T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a})};\qquad\hat{K}^{*}_{ab(2)}=\frac{2(3T_{a}m_{a}+2T_{a}m_{b}-T_{b}m_{a})}{5(T_{a}m_{b}+T_{b}m_{a})},

(33)

where
\hat{U}_{ab(1)}
is unchanged from the 21-moment model. It can be shown that for Coulomb collisions, this model is equivalent to
equations (44)-(49) of
Schunk 1977
, first calculated by
Burgers 1969
. For small temperature differences the mass-ratio coefficients become

\displaystyle\hat{D}^{*}_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{30m_{a}^{2}+16m_{a}m_{b}+13m_{b}^{2}}{10(m_{a}+m_{b})^{2}};\qquad\hat{D}^{*}_{ab(2)}=\frac{27m_{b}^{2}}{10(m_{a}+m_{b})^{2}};

\displaystyle\hat{K}^{*}_{ab(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2m_{a}+(6/5)m_{b}}{m_{a}};\qquad\hat{K}^{*}_{ab(2)}=\frac{4}{5};\qquad\hat{U}_{ab(1)}=\frac{3}{2}\frac{m_{b}}{(m_{a}+m_{b})}.

(34)

Our new 21-moment model thus can be viewed as a generalization of the multi-fluid description of

Burgers 1969
&
Schunk 1977
, where the heat fluxes and stress-tensors are described more accurately, and with the same level of precision as in

Braginskii 1965
. Nevertheless, we only use the Landau collisional operator applicable for Coulomb collisions, whereas Burgers-Schunk use
the more general Boltzmann collisional operator and account for several different interaction potentials.

II.4
Semi-linear approximation (de-coupled stress tensors and heat fluxes)

Here we consider the 21-moment model with evolution equations (
9
)-(
12
) in the semi-linear approximation,
where additionally viscosity-tensors and heat fluxes are de-coupled. It will be shown later that
the contributions introduced by the coupling
are smaller by a factor of
1/\nu_{aa}
. Within the semi-linear approximation we also assume that there are no large-scale gradients of considered fluid moments.
For example, the de-coupling removes the last terms at the left hand side of equations (
10
), (
11
), (
12
)
proportional to
(\nabla p_{a})\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}
,
(\nabla p_{a})\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}
and
(\nabla p_{a})\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}
. We neglect these terms
within the semi-linear approximation also when the coupling is considered (see Sections
V
&
VI
).
In the presence of large-scale gradients
in pressure/temperature these terms might become significant, together with many other terms that are neglected in the semi-linear approximation.
Evolution equations for heat flux vectors simplify into

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\frac{5}{2}p_{a}\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)=\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(3)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime};

(35)

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}+70\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}}\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)=\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(5)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime},

(36)

and evolution equations for viscosity-tensors become

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}\big)^{S}+p_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{a}^{(2)}\,{}^{\prime};

(37)

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}\big)^{S}+7\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(4)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}.

(38)

The above system will be used to recover the transport coefficients of
Braginskii 1965
. In some instances, it might be actually
advantageous to suppress the non-linearities in numerical simulations, and perform
multi-fluid simulations with system (
35
)-(
38
) instead of the system (
9
)-(
12
).

III
One ion-electron plasma

III.1
Ion heat flux
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}
of Braginskii (self-collisions)

Here we consider a one ion-electron plasma of similar temperatures, which is the choice of
Braginskii 1965
.
For the ion heat flux, Braginskii neglects ion-electron collisions. Considering only
self-collisions, evolution equations for ion heat fluxes read

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\frac{5}{2}p_{a}\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)=-\frac{4}{5}\nu_{aa}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\frac{3}{70}\nu_{aa}\Big(\frac{\rho_{a}}{p_{a}}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}-28\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\Big);

(39)

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}+70\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}}\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)=-\frac{88}{5}\nu_{aa}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}-\frac{3}{35}\nu_{aa}\Big(\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}-28\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\Big).

(40)

Neglecting the evolution equation (
40
) and prescribing closure (
31
)
which neglects the second term on the r.h.s. of (
39
),
yields the ion heat flux model of Burgers-Schunk, with the well-known
-4/5
constant. However, now the equations read

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\frac{5}{2}p_{a}\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)=-2\nu_{aa}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\frac{3}{70}\nu_{aa}\frac{\rho_{a}}{p_{a}}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a};

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}+70\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}}\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)=-\frac{76}{5}\nu_{aa}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}-\frac{3}{35}\nu_{aa}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}.

(41)

Prescribing the quasi-static approximation (by canceling the
d_{a}/dt
), yields an analytic solution (see for example a general vector equation (
E23
)
with solution (
E24
))

\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}=-\kappa_{\parallel}^{a}\nabla_{\parallel}T_{a}-\kappa_{\perp}^{a}\nabla_{\perp}T_{a}+\kappa_{\times}^{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\nabla T_{a},

(42)

and thermal conductivities

\displaystyle\kappa_{\parallel}^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{125}{32}\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{aa}m_{a}};

\displaystyle\kappa_{\perp}^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{aa}m_{a}}\,\frac{2x^{2}+(648/245)}{x^{4}+(3313/1225)x^{2}+(20736/30625)};

\displaystyle\kappa_{\times}^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{aa}m_{a}}\,\frac{(5/2)x^{3}+(2277/490)x}{x^{4}+(3313/1225)x^{2}+(20736/30625)},

(43)

where
x=\Omega_{a}/\nu_{aa}
. Alternatively, by using numerical values

\displaystyle\kappa_{\parallel}^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3.906\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{aa}m_{a}};

\displaystyle\kappa_{\perp}^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{aa}m_{a}}\,\frac{2x^{2}+2.645}{x^{4}+2.704x^{2}+0.6771};

\displaystyle\kappa_{\times}^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{aa}m_{a}}\,\frac{(5/2)x^{3}+4.647x}{x^{4}+2.704x^{2}+0.6771},

(44)

which recovers the ion heat flux of
Braginskii 1965
, his equation (4.40).
We use Braginskii notation with vectors
\nabla_{\parallel}=\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\cdot\nabla
and

\nabla_{\perp}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\cdot\nabla=-\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\nabla
.

III.2
Ion heat flux
\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}
(self-collisions)

The solution for the vector
\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}
has a similar form

\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}=\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big[-\kappa_{\parallel}^{a(5)}\nabla_{\parallel}T_{a}-\kappa_{\perp}^{a(5)}\nabla_{\perp}T_{a}+\kappa_{\times}^{a(5)}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\nabla T_{a}\Big],

(45)

with “thermal conductivities”

\displaystyle\kappa_{\parallel}^{a(5)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{2975}{24}}_{123.96}\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{aa}m_{a}};

\displaystyle\kappa_{\perp}^{a(5)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{aa}m_{a}}\frac{44x^{2}+(14688/175)}{x^{4}+(3313/1225)x^{2}+(20736/30625)};

\displaystyle\kappa_{\times}^{a(5)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{aa}m_{a}}\frac{70x^{3}+(1086/7)x}{x^{4}+(3313/1225)x^{2}+(20736/30625)}.

(46)

III.3
Electron heat flux
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}
of Braginskii

Considering a one-ion electron plasma with similar temperatures, and keeping only the dominant term in a
m_{e}/m_{i}
expansion,
the mass-ratio coefficients (
26
), (
27
), (
28
) simplify into

\displaystyle V_{ei(1)}=\frac{21}{10};\quad V_{ei(2)}=\frac{3}{5};

\displaystyle\hat{D}_{ei(1)}=\frac{19}{4};\quad\hat{D}_{ei(2)}=\frac{27}{10};\quad\hat{E}_{ei(1)}=\frac{69}{560};\quad\hat{E}_{ei(2)}=\frac{45}{112}\frac{m_{e}}{m_{i}};\quad\hat{U}_{ei(1)}=\frac{3}{2}

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{ei(1)}=\frac{759}{10};\quad\hat{F}_{ei(2)}=\frac{153}{5};\quad\hat{G}_{ei(1)}=-\,\frac{533}{280};\quad\hat{G}_{ei(2)}=\frac{15}{8}\frac{m_{e}}{m_{i}};\quad\hat{U}_{ei(2)}=27,

(47)

collisional exchange rates become

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\rho_{e}\nu_{ei}\delta\boldsymbol{u}+\frac{21}{10}\frac{\rho_{e}}{p_{e}}\nu_{ei}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}-\frac{3}{56}\frac{\rho_{e}^{2}}{p_{e}^{2}}\nu_{ei}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{e};

(48)

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(3)}_{e}\,{}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{2}p_{e}\nu_{ei}\delta\boldsymbol{u}-\Big[2\nu_{ee}+\frac{19}{4}\nu_{ei}\Big]\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}+\Big[\frac{3}{70}\nu_{ee}+\frac{69}{560}\nu_{ei}\Big]\frac{\rho_{e}}{p_{e}}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{e};

(49)

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(5)}_{e}\,{}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+27\frac{p_{e}^{2}}{\rho_{e}}\nu_{ei}\delta\boldsymbol{u}-\Big[\frac{76}{5}\nu_{ee}+\frac{759}{10}\nu_{ei}\Big]\frac{p_{e}}{\rho_{e}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}-\Big[\frac{3}{35}\nu_{ee}-\frac{533}{280}\nu_{ei}\Big]\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{e},

(50)

where
\delta\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{u}_{e}-\boldsymbol{u}_{i}
, and enter the right hand side of the electron momentum equation, and evolution equations for the electron heat flux vectors

\displaystyle\frac{d_{e}}{dt}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}+\Omega_{e}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}+\frac{5}{2}p_{e}\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{e}}{\rho_{e}}\Big)=\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(3)}_{e}\,{}^{\prime};

\displaystyle\frac{d_{e}}{dt}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{e}+\Omega_{e}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{e}+70\frac{p_{e}^{2}}{\rho_{e}}\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{e}}{\rho_{e}}\Big)=\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(5)}_{e}\,{}^{\prime}.

(51)

In
Braginskii 1965
, the results are expressed through the collisional frequency
\nu_{ei}
, and conversion with
\nu_{ee}=\nu_{ei}/(Z_{i}\sqrt{2})
yields

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(3)}_{e}\,{}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{2}p_{e}\nu_{ei}\delta\boldsymbol{u}-\Big[\frac{\sqrt{2}}{Z_{i}}+\frac{19}{4}\Big]\nu_{ei}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}+\Big[\frac{3}{70\sqrt{2}Z_{i}}+\frac{69}{560}\Big]\nu_{ei}\frac{\rho_{e}}{p_{e}}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{e};

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(5)}_{e}\,{}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+27\frac{p_{e}^{2}}{\rho_{e}}\nu_{ei}\delta\boldsymbol{u}-\Big[\frac{76}{5\sqrt{2}Z_{i}}+\frac{759}{10}\Big]\nu_{ei}\frac{p_{e}}{\rho_{e}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}-\Big[\frac{3}{35\sqrt{2}Z_{i}}-\frac{533}{280}\Big]\nu_{ei}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{e}.

(52)

In a quasi-static approximation, the solution of (
51
), (
52
) recovers the famous electron heat flux of
Braginskii 1965
,
together with vector
\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{e}
(which is of course not given by Braginskii). Substituting these results into the momentum exchange rates (
48
),
recovers the
\boldsymbol{R}_{e}
of Braginskii.

We use the same notation as
Braginskii 1965
with
x=\Omega_{e}/\nu_{ei}
, except (as is the norm in more recent papers) our
\Omega_{e}
is
formulated as a general
\Omega_{a}
and is thus negative, whereas in Braginskii
\Omega_{e}
is defined as positive.
This yields a simple change of signs in front of the “cross” (
\times
) terms with respect to Braginskii. In a quasi-static approximation,
the electron heat flux is split into a thermal and frictional part
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}=\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}^{T}+\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}^{u}
, where

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}^{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\kappa_{\parallel}^{e}\nabla_{\parallel}T_{e}-\kappa_{\perp}^{e}\nabla_{\perp}T_{e}+\kappa_{\times}^{e}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\nabla T_{e};

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}^{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{0}p_{e}\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\parallel}+p_{e}\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\perp}\frac{\beta_{1}^{\prime}x^{2}+\beta_{0}^{\prime}}{\triangle}-p_{e}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\delta\boldsymbol{u}\frac{\beta_{1}^{\prime\prime}x^{3}+\beta_{0}^{\prime\prime}x}{\triangle},

(53)

and the heat conductivities are given by

\kappa_{\parallel}^{e}=\frac{p_{e}}{m_{e}\nu_{ei}}\gamma_{0};\qquad\kappa_{\perp}^{e}=\frac{p_{e}}{m_{e}\nu_{ei}}\frac{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}x^{2}+\gamma_{0}^{\prime}}{\triangle};\qquad\kappa_{\times}^{e}=\frac{p_{e}}{m_{e}\nu_{ei}}\frac{\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}x^{3}+\gamma_{0}^{\prime\prime}x}{\triangle}.

(54)

The momentum exchange rates are also split into a thermal and frictional part
\boldsymbol{R}_{e}=\boldsymbol{R}_{e}^{T}+\boldsymbol{R}_{e}^{u}

(thermal force and friction force), according to

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{e}^{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\alpha_{0}\rho_{e}\nu_{ei}\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\parallel}-\rho_{e}\nu_{ei}\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\perp}\Big(1-\frac{\alpha_{1}^{\prime}x^{2}+\alpha_{0}^{\prime}}{\triangle}\Big)-\rho_{e}\nu_{ei}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\delta\boldsymbol{u}\frac{\alpha_{1}^{\prime\prime}x^{3}+\alpha_{0}^{\prime\prime}x}{\triangle};

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{e}^{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\beta_{0}n_{e}\nabla_{\parallel}T_{e}-n_{e}\nabla_{\perp}T_{e}\frac{\beta_{1}^{\prime}x^{2}+\beta_{0}^{\prime}}{\triangle}+n_{e}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\nabla T_{e}\frac{\beta_{1}^{\prime\prime}x^{3}+\beta_{0}^{\prime\prime}x}{\triangle}.

(55)

Instead of a numerical Table II on page 25 of
Braginskii 1965
, we provide all the coefficients
in a fully analytic form for a general ion charge
Z_{i}
, which are given by

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4(16Z_{i}^{2}+61Z_{i}\sqrt{2}+72)}{217Z_{i}^{2}+604Z_{i}\sqrt{2}+288};\qquad\beta_{0}=\frac{30Z_{i}(11Z_{i}+15\sqrt{2})}{217Z_{i}^{2}+604Z_{i}\sqrt{2}+288};

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{25Z_{i}(433Z_{i}+180\sqrt{2})}{4(217Z_{i}^{2}+604Z_{i}\sqrt{2}+288)};

(56)

\displaystyle\triangle=x^{4}+\delta_{1}x^{2}+\delta_{0};

\displaystyle\delta_{0}=\Big(\frac{217Z_{i}^{2}+604Z_{i}\sqrt{2}+288}{700Z_{i}^{2}}\Big)^{2};

\displaystyle\delta_{1}=\frac{586601Z_{i}^{2}+330152Z_{i}\sqrt{2}+106016}{78400Z_{i}^{2}};

(57)

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{9(40337Z_{i}+10996\sqrt{2})}{78400Z_{i}};

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{9(217Z_{i}^{2}+604Z_{i}\sqrt{2}+288)(17Z_{i}+40\sqrt{2})}{490000Z_{i}^{3}};

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{477}{280};\qquad\alpha_{0}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{9(64Z_{i}^{2}+151Z_{i}\sqrt{2}+253)}{6125Z_{i}^{2}};

(58)

\displaystyle\beta_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3(709Z_{i}+172\sqrt{2})}{560Z_{i}};

\displaystyle\beta_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3(217Z_{i}^{2}+604Z_{i}\sqrt{2}+288)(11Z_{i}+15\sqrt{2})}{49000Z_{i}^{3}};

\displaystyle\beta_{1}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2};\qquad\beta_{0}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}=\frac{3(5729Z_{i}^{2}+6711Z_{i}\sqrt{2}+4728)}{19600Z_{i}^{2}};

(59)

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{13Z_{i}+4\sqrt{2}}{4Z_{i}};

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(217Z_{i}^{2}+604Z_{i}\sqrt{2}+288)(433Z_{i}+180\sqrt{2})}{78400Z_{i}^{3}};

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{5}{2};\qquad\gamma_{0}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{320797Z_{i}^{2}+202248Z_{i}\sqrt{2}+72864}{31360Z_{i}^{2}}.

(60)

Numerical values for
Z_{i}=1
are given in the first column of Table II of
Braginskii 1965
and for example
the parallel coefficients are
\alpha_{0}=0.5129
;
\beta_{0}=0.7110
;
\gamma_{0}=3.1616
, matching his values
exactly. We checked the entire Table II of Braginskii and his table is very precise,
except for two values. For the
\alpha_{0}
coefficient, values for
Z_{i}=2,3
should be
changed as
0.4408\to 0.4309
;
0.3965\to 0.3954
.
The rest of his table is calculated very accurately, with around handful of irrelevant
last digit rounding changes (such as
3.7703\to 3.7702
in
\delta_{0}(Z_{i}=1)
,

0.2400\to 0.2399
in
\alpha_{0}^{\prime\prime}(Z_{i}=3)
; and for
Z_{i}=4
charge
0.3752\to 0.3751
in
\alpha_{0}
;
9.055\to 9.056
in
\delta_{0}

0.4478\to 0.4477
in
\beta_{0}^{\prime}
etc.).

Analytic results (
56
) for parallel coefficients
\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\gamma_{0}
were also
obtained by
Simakov & Molvig 2014
; see later Section
IV
.
To triple-check our other results, we re-calculated our approach with analytic collisional matrices of
Ji & Held 2013
, equations (28a)-(28f),
together with their equations (40)-(44) and other formulas, which yielded the same analytic expressions.
Unfortunately, the analytic results of
Balescu 1988
are written in a such a complicated form,
(see his page 236, with collisional matrices on page 198 and the required conversion equation (5.7.13) on page 270),
that we were able to verify only an analytic match with his parallel coefficients.
The formulation of
Balescu 1988
is so different from Braginskii, that Balescu by himself (page 275) only claims a match
of below 1% for the 21-moment model, not further analyzing possible discrepancies.

III.4
Electron heat flux
\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}_{e}^{(5)}

Similarly to the usual electron heat flux
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}
,
a quasi-static solution for the heat flux vector
\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}_{e}^{(5)}
has to be split into a thermal and frictional part,
according to

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}_{e}^{(5)T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p_{e}}{\rho_{e}}\Big[-\kappa_{\parallel}^{e(5)}\nabla_{\parallel}T_{e}-\kappa_{\perp}^{e(5)}\nabla_{\perp}T_{e}+\kappa_{\times}^{e(5)}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\nabla T_{e}\Big];

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}_{e}^{(5)u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p_{e}^{2}}{\rho_{e}}\Big[\beta_{0}^{(5)}\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\parallel}+\frac{\beta_{1}^{(5)^{\prime}}x^{2}+\beta_{0}^{(5)^{\prime}}}{\triangle}\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\perp}-\frac{\beta_{1}^{(5)^{\prime\prime}}x^{3}+\beta_{0}^{(5)^{\prime\prime}}x}{\triangle}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\delta\boldsymbol{u}\Big],

(61)

with thermal conductivities

\kappa_{\parallel}^{e(5)}=\frac{p_{e}}{m_{e}\nu_{ei}}\gamma_{0}^{(5)};\qquad\kappa_{\perp}^{e(5)}=\frac{p_{e}}{m_{e}\nu_{ei}}\frac{\gamma_{1}^{(5)^{\prime}}x^{2}+\gamma_{0}^{(5)^{\prime}}}{\triangle};\qquad\kappa_{\times}^{e(5)}=\frac{p_{e}}{m_{e}\nu_{ei}}\frac{\gamma_{1}^{(5)^{\prime\prime}}x^{3}+\gamma_{0}^{(5)^{\prime\prime}}x}{\triangle}.

(62)

The analytic coefficients are given by

\displaystyle\beta_{0}^{(5)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{840Z_{i}(13\sqrt{2}+12Z_{i})}{217Z_{i}^{2}+604Z_{i}\sqrt{2}+288};

\displaystyle\beta_{1}^{(5)^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3(5829Z_{i}+1172\sqrt{2})}{280Z_{i}};

\displaystyle\beta_{0}^{(5)^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3(217Z_{i}^{2}+604Z_{i}\sqrt{2}+288)(12Z_{i}+13\sqrt{2})}{1750Z_{i}^{3}};

\displaystyle\beta_{1}^{(5)^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 27;\qquad\beta_{0}^{(5)^{\prime\prime}}=\frac{3(7611Z_{i}^{2}+8429Z_{i}\sqrt{2}+5000)}{700Z_{i}^{2}},

(63)

and

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{(5)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{175Z_{i}(204\sqrt{2}+571Z_{i})}{217Z_{i}^{2}+604Z_{i}\sqrt{2}+288};

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}^{(5)^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{113Z_{i}+44\sqrt{2}}{2Z_{i}};

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{(5)^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(217Z_{i}^{2}+604Z_{i}\sqrt{2}+288)(571Z_{i}+204\sqrt{2})}{2800Z_{i}^{3}};

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}^{(5)^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 70;\qquad\gamma_{0}^{(5)^{\prime\prime}}=\frac{430783Z_{i}^{2}+261672Z_{i}\sqrt{2}+86880}{1120Z_{i}^{2}},

(64)

with
\triangle
unchanged and given by (
57
).
These results were substituted into the momentum exchange rates
\boldsymbol{R}_{e}
, equation (
48
),
to obtain the final expression for the friction force and thermal force. Useful relations are

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\frac{21}{10}\beta_{0}+\frac{3}{56}\beta_{0}^{(5)};\qquad\alpha_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{21}{10}\beta_{1}^{\prime}-\frac{3}{56}\beta_{1}^{(5)^{\prime}};

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{21}{10}\beta_{0}^{\prime}-\frac{3}{56}\beta_{0}^{(5)^{\prime}};\qquad\alpha_{1}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{21}{10}\beta_{1}^{\prime\prime}-\frac{3}{56}\beta_{1}^{(5)^{\prime\prime}};\qquad\alpha_{0}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{21}{10}\beta_{0}^{\prime\prime}-\frac{3}{56}\beta_{0}^{(5)^{\prime\prime}};

\displaystyle\beta_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{21}{10}\gamma_{0}-\frac{3}{56}\gamma_{0}^{(5)};\qquad\beta_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{21}{10}\gamma_{1}^{\prime}-\frac{3}{56}\gamma_{1}^{(5)^{\prime}};

\displaystyle\beta_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{21}{10}\gamma_{0}^{\prime}-\frac{3}{56}\gamma_{0}^{(5)^{\prime}};\qquad\beta_{1}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{21}{10}\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}-\frac{3}{56}\gamma_{1}^{(5)^{\prime\prime}};\qquad\beta_{0}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{21}{10}\gamma_{0}^{\prime\prime}-\frac{3}{56}\gamma_{0}^{(5)^{\prime\prime}}.

(65)

For
Z_{i}=1
, transport coefficients (
63
), (
64
) have numerical values

\displaystyle\beta_{0}^{(5)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 18.778;\qquad\beta_{1}^{(5)^{\prime}}=80.212;\qquad\beta_{0}^{(5)^{\prime}}=70.797;\qquad\beta_{1}^{(5)^{\prime\prime}}=27;\qquad\beta_{0}^{(5)^{\prime\prime}}=105.135;

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{(5)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 110.664;\qquad\gamma_{1}^{(5)^{\prime}}=87.613;\qquad\gamma_{0}^{(5)^{\prime}}=417.221;\qquad\gamma_{1}^{(5)^{\prime\prime}}=70;\qquad\gamma_{0}^{(5)^{\prime\prime}}=792.610.

(66)

III.5
Ion viscosity
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}
of Braginskii (self-collisions)

Considering self-collisions, evolution equations for the ion viscosity-tensors read

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}\big)^{S}+p_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}=-\,\frac{6}{5}\nu_{aa}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}+\frac{9}{70}\nu_{aa}\Big(\frac{\rho_{a}}{p_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}-7\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}\Big);

(67)

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}\big)^{S}+7\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}=-\,\frac{33}{5}\nu_{aa}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}-\frac{79}{140}\nu_{aa}\Big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}-7\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}\Big).

(68)

Neglecting (
68
) and prescribing closure (
31
) which neglects the second term on the r.h.s. of (
67
),
yields the ion-viscosity model of Burgers-Schunk, with the well-known
-6/5
constant. However, now the equations read

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}\big)^{S}+p_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}=-\,\frac{21}{10}\nu_{aa}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}+\frac{9}{70}\nu_{aa}\frac{\rho_{a}}{p_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a};

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}\big)^{S}+7\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}=-\,\frac{53}{20}\nu_{aa}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}-\frac{79}{140}\nu_{aa}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}.

(69)

In a quasi-static approximation, solution of (
69
) yields
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}
in the following form (see for example
Appendix
E.4
)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\eta_{0}^{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}-\eta_{1}^{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1}-\eta_{2}^{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{2}+\eta_{3}^{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{3}+\eta_{4}^{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{4};

(70)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\big)\Big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\Big);

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}+\frac{1}{2}\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\big)\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\big)^{S};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\big)^{S};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\big)^{S},

(71)

which is equivalent to equations (4.41) & (4.42) of
Braginskii 1965
, after one prescribes in his
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}
that the matrix
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}
is traceless.
Alternatively, with respect to
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}=(0,0,1)
(straight magnetic field applied in the
z-direction)

\displaystyle\Pi_{xx}^{a(2)}=-\frac{\eta_{0}^{a}}{2}(W_{xx}^{a}+W_{yy}^{a})-\frac{\eta_{1}^{a}}{2}(W_{xx}^{a}-W_{yy}^{a})-\eta_{3}^{a}W_{xy}^{a};

\displaystyle\Pi_{xy}^{a(2)}=\frac{\eta_{3}^{a}}{2}(W_{xx}^{a}-W_{yy}^{a})-\eta_{1}^{a}W_{xy}^{a};

\displaystyle\Pi_{xz}^{a(2)}=-\eta_{4}^{a}W_{yz}^{a}-\eta_{2}^{a}W_{xz}^{a};

\displaystyle\Pi_{yy}^{a(2)}=-\frac{\eta_{0}^{a}}{2}(W_{xx}^{a}+W_{yy}^{a})+\frac{\eta_{1}^{a}}{2}(W_{xx}^{a}-W_{yy}^{a})+\eta_{3}^{a}W_{xy}^{a};

\displaystyle\Pi_{yz}^{a(2)}=\eta_{4}^{a}W_{xz}^{a}-\eta_{2}^{a}W_{yz}^{a};

\displaystyle\Pi_{zz}^{a(2)}=-\eta_{0}^{a}W_{zz}^{a},

(72)

which is equation (2.21) of
Braginskii 1965
. The ion viscosities are

\displaystyle\eta_{0}^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1025}{1068}\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{aa}};

\displaystyle\eta_{2}^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{aa}}\,\frac{(6/5)x^{2}+(10947/4900)}{x^{4}+(79321/19600)x^{2}+(71289/30625)};

\displaystyle\eta_{4}^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{aa}}\,\frac{x^{3}+(46561/19600)x}{x^{4}+(79321/19600)x^{2}+(71289/30625)},

(73)

where
x=\Omega_{a}/\nu_{aa}
, and
\eta_{1}^{a}(x)=\eta_{2}^{a}(2x)
;
\eta_{3}^{a}(x)=\eta_{4}^{a}(2x)

(The solution is easily obtained for the parallel “zz” direction with
\Omega_{a}=0
, and
for perpendicular directions for example by choosing coupled “xz” and “yz” directions, and solving 4 equations in 4 unknowns).
Alternatively, using numerical values

\displaystyle\eta_{0}^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.960\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{aa}};

\displaystyle\eta_{2}^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{aa}}\,\frac{(6/5)x^{2}+2.234}{x^{4}+4.047x^{2}+2.328};

\displaystyle\eta_{4}^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{aa}}\,\frac{x^{3}+2.376x}{x^{4}+4.047x^{2}+2.328},

(74)

recovering ion viscosities of
Braginskii 1965
, his equation (4.44). Numerical values in Braginskii are evaluated precisely, with
the sole exception of one value in the denominator, where his rounded 4.03 should be replaced by 4.05.

III.6
Ion viscosity
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}
(self-collisions)

The ion viscosity tensor
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}
is given by

\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}=\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big[-\eta_{0}^{a(4)}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}-\eta_{1}^{a(4)}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1}-\eta_{2}^{a(4)}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{2}+\eta_{3}^{a(4)}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{3}+\eta_{4}^{a(4)}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{4}\Big],

(75)

with matrices
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}-\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{4}
(
71
) unchanged, and viscosities

\displaystyle\eta_{0}^{a(4)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{8435}{1068}\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{aa}};
