\chi_{a}=\frac{1}{2}\tilde{h}_{ij}^{(2)}\tilde{H}_{ij}^{(2)}+\frac{1}{10}\tilde{h}_{i}^{(3)}\tilde{H}_{i}^{(3)}+\frac{1}{28}\hat{h}_{ij}^{(4)}\tilde{H}_{ij}^{(4)}+\frac{1}{280}\tilde{h}_{i}^{(5)}\tilde{H}_{i}^{(5)}
;

\chi_{a}=\frac{1}{2}\tilde{h}_{ij}^{(2)}\tilde{H}_{ij}^{(2)}+\frac{1}{10}\tilde{h}_{i}^{(3)}\tilde{H}_{i}^{(3)}+\frac{1}{28}\hat{h}_{ij}^{(4)}\tilde{H}_{ij}^{(4)}+\frac{1}{120}\tilde{h}^{(4)}\tilde{H}^{(4)}+\frac{1}{280}\tilde{h}_{i}^{(5)}\tilde{H}_{i}^{(5)};

the anisotropic (bi-Maxwellian based) 16-moment model described in Section
VIII.9
.

\qquad+\frac{1}{5p_{a}}\sqrt{\frac{m_{a}}{T_{a}}}(\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{a})(\tilde{c}_{a}^{2}-5)+\frac{1}{280p_{a}}\sqrt{\frac{m_{a}}{T_{a}}}\Big[\frac{\rho_{a}}{p_{a}}(\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{a})-28(\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{a})\Big](\tilde{c}_{a}^{4}-14\tilde{c}_{a}^{2}+35)

\qquad+\frac{1}{120}\frac{\rho_{a}}{p_{a}^{2}}\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a}(\tilde{c}_{a}^{4}-10\tilde{c}^{2}+15);

Summary of various models with the perturbation
\chi_{a}
given in fluid moments. Results for the 21 & 22-moment models
are written with normalized
\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{a}=\sqrt{m_{a}/T_{a}}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}
.

VIII.5
Rosenbluth potentials (22-moment model)

Here we summarize the Rosenbluth potentials, defined according to

\displaystyle H_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{f_{b}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime})}{|\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{v}|}d^{3}v^{\prime};\qquad G_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})=\int|\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{v}|f_{b}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime})d^{3}v^{\prime},

(203)

where the first potential should not be confused with the irreducible Hermite polynomials.
For the 22-moment model the fully non-linear results read

\displaystyle H_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n_{b}\sqrt{\frac{m_{b}}{T_{b}}}\Big\{\frac{1}{\tilde{y}}\textrm{erf}\Big(\frac{\tilde{y}}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{e^{-\tilde{y}^{2}/2}}{10}\Big(\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}}^{(3)}_{b}+(\tilde{y}^{2}-5)\frac{\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}}^{(5)}_{b}}{28}\Big)

(204)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}(\hat{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{h}}}}^{(2)}_{b}:\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}})\Big[\frac{3}{\tilde{y}^{5}}\textrm{erf}\Big(\frac{\tilde{y}}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\big(\frac{1}{\tilde{y}^{2}}+\frac{3}{\tilde{y}^{4}}\big)e^{-\tilde{y}^{2}/2}\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{28}(\hat{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{h}}}}^{(4)}_{b}:\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}})\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}e^{-\tilde{y}^{2}/2}+\frac{1}{120}\tilde{h}^{(4)}_{b}(3-\tilde{y}^{2})\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}e^{-\tilde{y}^{2}/2}\Big\};

\displaystyle G_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n_{b}\sqrt{\frac{T_{b}}{m_{b}}}\Big\{\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}e^{-\tilde{y}^{2}/2}+\big(\tilde{y}+\frac{1}{\tilde{y}}\big)\textrm{erf}\big(\frac{\tilde{y}}{\sqrt{2}}\big)

(205)

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{\textrm{erf}(\tilde{y}/\sqrt{2})}{5\tilde{y}^{3}}-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{e^{-\tilde{y}^{2}/2}}{5\tilde{y}^{2}}\Big)\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}}^{(3)}_{b}-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{e^{-\tilde{y}^{2}/2}}{140}\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}}^{(5)}_{b}

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{2}(\hat{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{h}}}}^{(2)}_{b}:\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}})\Big[\frac{3}{\tilde{y}^{4}}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}e^{-\tilde{y}^{2}/2}+\big(\frac{1}{\tilde{y}^{3}}-\frac{3}{\tilde{y}^{5}}\big)\textrm{erf}\Big(\frac{\tilde{y}}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{14}(\hat{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{h}}}}^{(4)}_{b}:\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}})\Big[\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\big(\frac{1}{\tilde{y}^{2}}+\frac{3}{\tilde{y}^{4}}\big)e^{-\tilde{y}^{2}/2}-\frac{3}{\tilde{y}^{5}}\textrm{erf}\Big(\frac{\tilde{y}}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)\Big]-\frac{1}{60}\tilde{h}^{(4)}_{b}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}e^{-\tilde{y}^{2}/2}\Big\},

where we use the variable

\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}=\sqrt{\frac{m_{b}}{T_{b}}}(\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{u}_{b}).

(206)

These Rosenbluth potentials are used to calculate the dynamical friction vector
\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}
and the diffusion
tensor
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}
, which then form the Landau collisional operator, according to

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}(\boldsymbol{v})=2\frac{c_{ab}}{m_{a}^{2}}\big(1+\frac{m_{a}}{m_{b}}\big)\frac{\partial H_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}};\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}(\boldsymbol{v})=2\frac{c_{ab}}{m_{a}^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}G_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}\partial\boldsymbol{v}};\qquad c_{ab}=2\pi e^{4}Z_{a}^{2}Z_{b}^{2}\ln\Lambda;

\displaystyle C_{ab}(f_{a},f_{b})=-\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot\Big[\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}f_{a}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}f_{a})\Big].

(207)

The dynamical friction vectors and diffusion tensors can be found in the Appendix; see equations (
K15
)-(
K16
);
(
L13
)-(
L14
) and (
M4
)-(
M5
). For clarity, we split the calculations
into heat fluxes (Appendix
K
), viscosities (Appendix
L
) and scalar perturbations (Appendix
M
).
These results are fully non-linear and could be potentially useful to construct more sophisticated models that could capture
collisional effects beyond the semi-linear approximation, or perhaps to explore the runaway effect numerically.
All the equations can be transformed from Hermite moments to fluid moments by (
202
).

VIII.6
Hermite closures

The general hierarchy of evolution equations (
189
)-(
191
) needs to be closed with appropriate closures at the last retained fluid moment.
A correct form of a fluid closure is obtained in the Hermite space, by cutting the perturbation
\chi_{a}
given by (
198
) at
an appropriate
N
. For example, the 22-moment model is obtained with
Hermite closures
\tilde{h}^{(6)}_{a}=0
and
\tilde{h}^{a(6)}_{ij}=0
, which translate into fluid closures (
134
) & (
13
).

It is useful to summarize closures for higher-order moments, with details given in Appendix
B
.
It can be shown that for vectors and scalars, fluid closures derived from Hermite closures read

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{m+n+1}\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)^{n-m}\frac{n!}{m!(n-m)!}\frac{(2n+3)!!}{(2m+3)!!}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2m+1)}_{a};

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}^{(2n)}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=2}^{n-1}(-1)^{m+n+1}\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)^{n-m}\frac{n!}{m!(n-m)!}\frac{(2n+1)!!}{(2m+1)!!}\widetilde{X}^{(2m)}_{a},

(208)

together with closures for stress-tensors

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2n)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{n-2}(-1)^{m+n}\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)^{n-m-1}\frac{(n-1)!}{m!(n-m-1)!}\frac{(2n+3)!!}{(2m+5)!!}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2m+2)},

(209)

where the result is zero if the upper summation index is less than the lower summation index, yielding
closures
\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(3)}_{a}=0
;
\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a}=0
and
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}=0
. The
closures are summarized bellow in Tables
VIII.3
and
VIII.4
.

Hermite closures

Fluid closures

\tilde{h}_{i}^{(3)}=0
;

X^{(3)}_{i}=0
;

\tilde{h}^{(4)}=0
;

\widetilde{X}^{(4)}=0
;

\tilde{h}_{i}^{(5)}=0
;

X^{(5)}_{i}=14\frac{p}{\rho}X^{(3)}_{i}
;

\tilde{h}^{(6)}=0
;

\widetilde{X}^{(6)}=21\frac{p}{\rho}\widetilde{X}^{(4)}
;

\tilde{h}_{i}^{(7)}=0
;

X^{(7)}_{i}=27\frac{p}{\rho}X^{(5)}_{i}-189\frac{p^{2}}{\rho^{2}}X_{i}^{(3)}
;

\tilde{h}^{(8)}=0
;

\widetilde{X}^{(8)}=36\frac{p}{\rho}\widetilde{X}^{(6)}-378\frac{p^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\widetilde{X}^{(4)}
;

\tilde{h}_{i}^{(9)}=0
;

X^{(9)}_{i}=44\frac{p}{\rho}X^{(7)}_{i}-594\frac{p^{2}}{\rho^{2}}X^{(5)}_{i}+2772\frac{p^{3}}{\rho^{3}}X^{(3)}_{i}
.

Table VIII.3:
Summary of (MHD) Hermite closures, together with corresponding fluid closures. Species indices “a” are dropped. The usual heat flux
q_{i}=X_{i}^{(3)}/2
.
Note that beyond the 4th-order moment both classes start to differ. It can be shown that erroneously prescribing closures at the last retained moment such as

X^{(5)}_{i}=0
or
\widetilde{X}^{(6)}=0
leads to unphysical instabilities (unless one prescribes
X^{(3)}_{i}
or
\widetilde{X}^{(4)}=0
as well),
which is later demonstrated in Appendix
B.8
, Table
B.1
.
A general form for closures corresponding to
\tilde{h}^{(2n+1)}_{i}=0
and
\tilde{h}^{(2n)}=0
is given by (
208
). An analogous
table can be constructed for CGL parallel closures; see Appendix
B.9
, Table
B.2
.

Hermite closures

Fluid closures

\hat{h}^{(2)}_{ij}=0
;

\Pi_{ij}^{(2)}=0
;

\hat{h}^{(4)}_{ij}=0
;

\Pi_{ij}^{(4)}=7\frac{p}{\rho}\Pi_{ij}^{(2)}
;

\hat{h}^{(6)}_{ij}=0
;

\Pi_{ij}^{(6)}=18\frac{p}{\rho}\Pi_{ij}^{(4)}-63\frac{p^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\Pi_{ij}^{(2)}
;

\hat{h}^{(8)}_{ij}=0
;

\Pi_{ij}^{(8)}=33\frac{p}{\rho}\Pi_{ij}^{(6)}-297\frac{p^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\Pi_{ij}^{(4)}+693\frac{p^{3}}{\rho^{3}}\Pi_{ij}^{(2)}
;

Table VIII.4:
Similar to Table
VIII.3
, but for Hermite closures
\hat{h}^{(2n)}_{ij}=0
. A general form for closures corresponding
to
\hat{h}^{(2n)}_{ij}=0
is given by (
209
).

Here we need to address one incorrect interpretation
that we used in some of our previous papers. In the last paragraph of

Hunana et al. 2018
, and also in
Hunana et al. 2019a
;
Hunana et al. 2019b
it is claimed that
Landau fluid closures are necessary to go beyond the 4th-order moment in the fluid hierarchy. This interpretation was
obtained in the CGL framework for parallel moments by considering closures at the last retained moment
\widetilde{X}^{(2n)}_{a}=0
and

\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n+1)}_{\parallel a}=0
.
It was shown (see detailed proof in Section 12.2 in
Hunana et al. 2019b
) that beyond the 4th-order moment,
all fluid models become unstable if these closures are used. The proof is constructed correctly.
What is incorrect is the interpretation, that the proof implies that
Landau fluid closures are required to overcome this issue. The much simpler Hermite closures overcome this difficulty
as well.

In another words, beyond the 4th-order moment it is not possible to cut the fluid hierarchy by simply neglecting
the next order moment with closures such as
\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}=0
or
\widetilde{X}^{(6)}_{a}=0
, and such closures should be viewed as erroneous.
For the CGL model the closures have different coefficients than for the MHD model because the moments are defined differently
(a brief summary is given in Appendix
B.9
, Table
B.2
). The CGL closures will be
addressed in detail in a separate publication.

Importantly, the problem also disappears when one decouples the fluid hierarchy. For example, higher-order
Laguerre (Hermite) schemes that are typically used to obtain more precise transport coefficients for
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}
and
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}
,
neglect all the scalar perturbations
\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a}=\cdots=\widetilde{X}^{(2n)}_{a}=0
,
together with neglecting coupling between heat fluxes and stress-tensors.
In our formulation this yields a system

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}+(2n+3)!!\frac{(n)}{3}\frac{p_{a}^{n}}{\rho_{a}^{n-1}}\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad=\vec{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}-\frac{(2n+3)!!}{3}\frac{p_{a}^{n}}{\rho_{a}^{n}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a};

(210)

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2n)}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2n)}_{a}\big)^{S}+\frac{(2n+3)!!}{15}\frac{p^{n}_{a}}{\rho_{a}^{n-1}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}

\displaystyle\qquad=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}}}^{(2n)}_{a}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}Q^{(2n)}_{a}.

(211)

Closures (
208
), (
209
) are not required, because the equations are de-coupled. We did not calculate
collisional contributions for higher-order moments, but in the semi-linear approximation equations
(
210
)-(
211
) remain de-coupled and represent two independent hierarchies.
An essential feature of the Landau (or the Boltzmann) collisional operator is that the operator couples all the heat fluxes together,
and it also couples all the stress-tensors together. Thus by going higher and higher in the fluid
hierarchy does not create new contributions in a quasi-static approximation,
but yields increasingly precise transport coefficients for
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}
and
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}
.
Also, because the momentum exchange rates
\boldsymbol{R}_{a}
contain contributions from all the heat fluxes

\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(3)}_{a}\ldots\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}
, they become increasingly precise as well. System (
210
)-(
211
) nicely
clarifies how higher-order schemes can be viewed. Reinstating the coupling between heat fluxes and viscosity-tensors introduces additional
contributions but does not change the transport coefficients of the de-coupled system. A brief comparison of
various models is presented in Appendix
I
.

VIII.7
Inclusion of gravity

We have not explicitly considered the force of gravity during our calculations in the Appendix, nevertheless, its inclusion is trivial.
With the gravitational acceleration
\boldsymbol{G}
included, the Boltzmann equation reads

\frac{\partial f_{a}}{\partial t}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla f_{a}+\Big[\boldsymbol{G}+\frac{eZ_{a}}{m_{a}}(\boldsymbol{E}+\frac{1}{c}\boldsymbol{v}\times\boldsymbol{B})\Big]\cdot\nabla_{v}f_{a}=C(f_{a}).

(212)

We use big
\boldsymbol{G}
instead of small
\boldsymbol{g}
to clearly distinguish it from the heat flux
\boldsymbol{q}
.
Gravity does not enter the collisional operator, and collisional integrals with the right hand side are not effected.
Gravity enters the left hand side, and when the Boltzmann equation is integrated gravity of course enters the fluid hierarchy of moments.
With the two exceptions of the density equation and the pressure tensor equation, gravity enters evolution equations for
all other moments, analogously as the electric field does. An explicit collisionless equation for a general n-th order moment with the
electric field present is for example
equation (12.13) of
Hunana et al. 2019b
. Because no Maxwell’s equations are used in deriving the fluid hierarchy,
the presence of gravity can be accounted for by simply replacing

\frac{eZ_{a}}{m_{a}}\boldsymbol{E}\to\boldsymbol{G}+\frac{eZ_{a}}{m_{a}}\boldsymbol{E}.

(213)

Furthermore, such a hierarchy is not very useful because the evolution equation for an n-th order moment is coupled with
“n” momentum equations. Subtracting these momentum equations yields final equation (12.16) in
Hunana et al. 2019b
, where the electric field is
not present, meaning that gravity is not present either. In other words, the collisionless equation (12.16) of Hunana et al., as well
as our new collisional equation (
A12
) remain valid in the presence of gravitational force.
The inclusion of gravity to the entire model is thus achieved trivially by adding
-\boldsymbol{G}
into the left hand side of
the momentum equation (
7
) (which we have done), and no additional calculations are required.
In the main text, the only other equation which contains gravity is the electric field equation (
96
).

VIII.8
Precision of
m_{e}/m_{i}
expansions (unmagnetized proton-electron plasma)

The multi-fluid formulation is also an excellent tool to double-check the precision of
m_{e}/m_{i}
expansions.
It is possible to again consider a one ion-electron plasma, but this time calculate the transport coefficients precisely,
without any expansions in the smallness of
m_{e}/m_{i}
. As an example we consider
an unmagnetized proton-electron plasma (
Z_{p}=1
,
m_{p}/m_{e}=1836.15267
) with similar temperatures
T_{e}=T_{p}=T_{ep}
.
Charge neutrality implies
n_{e}=n_{p}
and so
p_{e}=p_{p}
.
We however maintain
\nabla T_{e}\neq\nabla T_{p}
, because the gradients can be different. We first calculate heat fluxes.
For clarity, we are solving 4 coupled evolution equations, which are explicitly given
in Appendix
N
; see equations (
N1
)-(
N4
).

Precise calculation should not use simplified collisional times (
182
) where expansions in
m_{e}/m_{i}
have been made,
but exact collisional times (
178
) with
numerical values
\nu_{ee}=0.707299\nu_{ep}
and
\nu_{pp}=0.0165063\nu_{ep}
(we take
\ln\Lambda
to be constant).
The quasi-static approximation then yields heat fluxes

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[-3.159370\nabla T_{e}+8.301\times 10^{-6}\nabla T_{p}\Big]\frac{p_{e}}{m_{e}\nu_{ep}}+0.711046p_{e}\delta\boldsymbol{u};

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[-110.5793\nabla T_{e}+1.376\times 10^{-3}\nabla T_{p}\Big]\frac{p_{e}^{2}}{\rho_{e}m_{e}\nu_{ep}}+18.78249\frac{p_{e}^{2}}{\rho_{e}}\delta\boldsymbol{u};

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[-3.302411\nabla T_{p}+0.2516\times 10^{-3}\nabla T_{e}\Big]\frac{p_{p}}{m_{p}\nu_{pp}}+0.206535\times 10^{-4}p_{p}\delta\boldsymbol{u};

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[-103.3984\nabla T_{p}+0.7863\times 10^{-2}\nabla T_{e}\Big]\frac{p_{p}^{2}}{\rho_{p}m_{p}\nu_{pp}}+0.646475\times 10^{-3}\frac{p_{p}^{2}}{\rho_{p}}\delta\boldsymbol{u},

(214)

where
\delta\boldsymbol{u}=(\boldsymbol{u}_{e}-\boldsymbol{u}_{p})
. For the electron heat flux
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}
, note the difference of the thermal conductivity 3.1594 from the Braginskii value 3.1616.
The difference is caused by calculating the mass-ratio coefficients (
27
), (
28
) exactly without
m_{e}/m_{p}
expansions, together
with slightly different ratios of frequencies
(a less-precise calculation with neglecting proton-proton collisions by
\nu_{pp}=0
and using simplified
\nu_{ee}=\nu_{ep}/\sqrt{2}
yields 3.1600).

For the proton heat flux
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{p}
, the relatively large difference between the thermal conductivity 3.302 and Braginskii self-collisional value
125/32=3.906
is caused
by the proton-electron collisions. Similarly for the
\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{p}
, where the self-collisional value is
2975/24=123.96
.
Calculating the coupled system exactly has a nice advantage that one can
calculate the momentum exchange rates in two different ways

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nu_{ep}\Big\{-\rho_{e}\delta\boldsymbol{u}+\frac{\mu_{ep}}{T_{ep}}\Big[V_{ep(1)}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}-V_{ep(2)}\frac{\rho_{e}}{\rho_{p}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{p}\Big]-\frac{3}{56}\Big(\frac{\mu_{ep}}{T_{ep}}\Big)^{2}\Big[\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{e}-\frac{\rho_{e}}{\rho_{p}}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{p}\Big]\Big\};

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nu_{pe}\Big\{+\rho_{p}\delta\boldsymbol{u}+\frac{\mu_{ep}}{T_{ep}}\Big[V_{pe(1)}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{p}-V_{pe(2)}\frac{\rho_{p}}{\rho_{e}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}\Big]-\frac{3}{56}\Big(\frac{\mu_{ep}}{T_{ep}}\Big)^{2}\Big[\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{p}-\frac{\rho_{p}}{\rho_{e}}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{e}\Big]\Big\},

(215)

and both options yield the same result

\boldsymbol{R}_{e}=-\boldsymbol{R}_{p}=-0.711046n_{e}\nabla T_{e}-0.2065\times 10^{-4}n_{e}\nabla T_{p}-0.513306\rho_{e}\nu_{ep}\delta\boldsymbol{u}.

(216)

Viscosities of proton-electron plasma are
(for clarity, we are solving 4 equations in 4 unknowns, explicitly given by (
N5
)-(
N8
))

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[-0.730622\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{e}-0.2800\times 10^{-2}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{p}\Big]\frac{p_{e}}{\nu_{ei}};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[-6.542519\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{e}+3.1509\times 10^{-2}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{p}\Big]\frac{p_{e}^{2}}{\rho_{e}\nu_{ei}};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[-0.892105\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{p}-0.4621\times 10^{-4}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{e}\Big]\frac{p_{p}}{\nu_{pp}};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[-7.250870\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{p}-0.3759\times 10^{-3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{e}\Big]\frac{p_{p}^{2}}{\rho_{p}\nu_{pp}},

(217)

and for proton species the relatively large differences from self-collisional values
1025/1068=0.960
and
8435/1068=7.898
are
again caused by proton-electron collisions.
In Appendix
N
, we consider another examples of coupling between two species, and we calculate heat fluxes and
viscosities for protons & alpha particles (fully ionized Helium), and for the deuterium-tritium plasma used in plasma fusion.

VIII.9
Limitations of our approach

It is important to clarify the limitations of our model. In the highly-collisional regime, our limitations
are the same as for the model of
Braginskii 1965
. For example, we describe only Coulomb collisions and we do not take into account ionization & recombination
and radiative transfer.
Additionally, our approach shows that coupling of stress-tensors and heat fluxes should be ideally investigated with the 22-moment model.
Even though this model is fully formulated in Section
VII
, including its
collisional contributions calculated with the Landau operator, we did not use this model to further explore the resulting coupling.

VIII.9.1
Weakly collisional regime: expansions around bi-Maxwellians

The situation becomes more complicated in the weakly-collisional regime
where there might not be enough collisions to keep the distribution function sufficiently close to the equilibrium Maxwellian
f_{a}^{(0)}
.
The distribution function might evolve
to such an extent that the core assumptions in the entire derivation break down, i.e. equation (
1
) looses its validity.
A better approach is then to consider expansions similar to equation (
1
), but around a bi-Maxwellian
f_{a}^{(0)}
(see e.g.
Oraevskii et al. 1968
;
Chodura & Pohl 1971
;
Demars & Schunk 1979
;
Barakat & Schunk 1982
, and references therein),
which can handle much larger departures from the highly-collisional Maxwellian distribution.
In order to point out the differences and difficulties associated with this approach, it is of interest to briefly describe
how expansions around an anisotropic bi-Maxwellian would look like.
The simplest anisotropic model is known as the CGL, after the pioneering work of
Chew, Goldberger and Low
(
Chew et al. 1956
)
. The difference with our current approach starts with
the decomposition of the pressure tensor
p_{ij}^{a}
defined in (
A2
), and the decomposition reads

\displaystyle\textrm{isotropic:}\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)};

(218)

\displaystyle\textrm{anisotropic:}\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{\parallel a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}+p_{\perp a}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}-\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}})+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)\textrm{CGL}}

(219)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}+(p_{\parallel a}-p_{\perp a})\Big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\Big)+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)\textrm{CGL}},

with scalar pressures

\displaystyle p_{\parallel a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}=m_{a}\int c_{\parallel a}^{2}f_{a}d^{3}v;\qquad p_{\perp a}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}:\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}/2=\frac{m_{a}}{2}\int|\boldsymbol{c}_{\perp a}|^{2}f_{a}d^{3}v.

(220)

Directly from the above definitions, the stress-tensors satisfy

\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}=\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)\textrm{CGL}}=0;\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\neq 0;\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)\textrm{CGL}}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}=0,

(221)

and while
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}
has 5 independent components,
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)\textrm{CGL}}
has only 4.

The decomposition of the heat flux tensor
q_{ijk}^{a}
defined by equation (
A2
) is slightly more complicated.
In an arbitrary-collisional regime one needs to define two heat flux vectors

\boldsymbol{S}^{\parallel}_{a}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}=m_{a}\int c_{\parallel a}^{2}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}f_{a}d^{3}v;\qquad\boldsymbol{S}^{\perp}_{a}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}:\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}/2=\frac{m_{a}}{2}\int|\boldsymbol{c}_{\perp a}|^{2}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}f_{a}d^{3}v.

(222)

These heat flux vectors are further split by projecting them along the
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}
, which defines the
gyrotropic
(scalar) heat fluxes
q_{\parallel a}
&
q_{\perp a}
,
and the perpendicular projection defines the
non-gyrotropic heat flux vectors

\boldsymbol{S}^{\parallel}_{\perp a}
&
\boldsymbol{S}^{\perp}_{\perp a}
,
according to

\displaystyle q_{\parallel a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}^{\parallel}_{a}=m_{a}\int c_{\parallel a}^{2}c_{\parallel a}f_{a}d^{3}v;\qquad q_{\perp a}=\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}^{\perp}_{a}=\frac{m_{a}}{2}\int|\boldsymbol{c}_{\perp a}|^{2}c_{\parallel a}f_{a}d^{3}v;

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{S}^{\parallel}_{\perp a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}^{\parallel}_{a}=m_{a}\int c_{\parallel a}^{2}\boldsymbol{c}_{\perp a}f_{a}d^{3}v;\qquad\boldsymbol{S}^{\perp}_{\perp a}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}^{\perp}_{a}=\frac{m_{a}}{2}\int|\boldsymbol{c}_{\perp a}|^{2}\boldsymbol{c}_{\perp a}f_{a}d^{3}v.

(223)

The following relations then hold
\boldsymbol{S}^{\parallel}_{a}=q_{\parallel a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}+\boldsymbol{S}^{\parallel}_{\perp a}
; &

\boldsymbol{S}^{\perp}_{a}=q_{\perp a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}+\boldsymbol{S}^{\perp}_{\perp a}
; together with

\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}^{\parallel}_{\perp a}=0
; &
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}^{\perp}_{\perp a}=0
.
The two different decompositions of the entire heat flux tensor then read

\displaystyle\textrm{isotropic:}\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{5}\Big[\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\Big]^{S}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{a}^{\prime};

(224)

\displaystyle\textrm{anisotropic:}\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q_{\parallel a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}+q_{\perp a}\Big[\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\Big]^{S}+\Big[\boldsymbol{S}^{\parallel}_{\perp a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\Big]^{S}+\frac{1}{2}\Big[\boldsymbol{S}^{\perp}_{\perp a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\Big]^{S}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{a};

(225)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q_{\perp a}\big[\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\big]^{S}+(q_{\parallel a}-3q_{\perp a})\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}+\frac{1}{2}\Big[\boldsymbol{S}^{\perp}_{\perp a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\Big]^{S}+\Big[\big(\boldsymbol{S}^{\parallel}_{\perp a}-\frac{\boldsymbol{S}^{\perp}_{\perp a}}{2}\big)\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\Big]^{S}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{a},

where both
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{a}^{\prime}
and
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{a}
are traceless.
Neglecting these traceless contributions, the isotropic approach accounts for 3 (out of 10) scalar components of
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}

and represents a 13-moment model
(1 density, 3 velocity, 1 scalar pressure, 5 stress-tensor components, 3 heat flux
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}
components).
The anisotropic approach accounts for 6 scalar components of
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}
and represents a 16-moment model,
described by sixteen scalar evolution equations
(1 density, 3 velocity, 2 scalar pressures, 4 stress-tensor components,
3 for each heat flux vector
\boldsymbol{S}^{\parallel}_{a}
&
\boldsymbol{S}^{\perp}_{a}
.)
Unfortunately, such a complicated decomposition of the heat flux is necessary
in an arbitrary-collisional regime, and we only used decomposition (
224
).
For clarity, direct relation with the usual heat flux vector
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}
reads

\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}=\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{S}^{\parallel}_{a}+\boldsymbol{S}^{\perp}_{a}=\Big(\frac{1}{2}q_{\parallel a}+q_{\perp a}\Big)\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{S}^{\parallel}_{\perp a}+\boldsymbol{S}^{\perp}_{\perp a}.

(226)

Note that both
q_{\parallel a}
&
q_{\perp a}
denote components along the
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}
. The highly-collisional limit is achieved by
q_{\parallel a}=3q_{\perp a}
and

\boldsymbol{S}^{\parallel}_{\perp a}=\boldsymbol{S}^{\perp}_{\perp a}/2
, in which case
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}=(5/2)q_{\perp a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}+(5/4)\boldsymbol{S}^{\perp}_{\perp a}
or
equivalently
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}=(5/6)q_{\parallel a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}+(5/2)\boldsymbol{S}^{\parallel}_{\perp a}
. We used the same notation as for example collisionless papers by

Passot & Sulem 2007
;
Sulem & Passot 2015
;
Hunana et al. 2019a
;
Hunana et al. 2019b
.

These anisotropic decompositions must be retained in an arbitrary-collisional regime.
However, calculations with the Landau (Boltzmann) collisional operators then become very complicated.
Notably,
Chodura & Pohl 1971
;
Demars & Schunk 1979
;
Barakat & Schunk 1982
used the anisotropic 16-moment model as described above
and calculated the collisional contributions for several interaction potentials.
Judging from the papers above,
maintaining the precision of our current model (where the 4th & 5th order moments are considered) and extending it to an anisotropic (bi-Maxwellian) regime
seems to be so complicated, that it might not be worth the effort.
Curiously, in a simplified spherically symmetric radial geometry
Cuperman et al. 1980
;
Cuperman et al. 1981
;
Cuperman & Dryer 1985

considered what seems like a mixture of anisotropic and isotropic moments, with anisotropic temperatures, isotropic heat flux vector,
and the parallel (anisotropic) perturbation of the 4th-order moment (which we call
\widetilde{r}_{\parallel\parallel a}
).

VIII.9.2
Landau fluid closures for the collisionless case

In contrast to the free-streaming formula of
Hollweg 1974
;
Hollweg 1976
, in plasma physics the collisionless heat flux is typically associated with the phenomenon of Landau damping.
For example, collisionless linear kinetic theory expanded around bi-Maxwellian with mean
zero drifts
in gyrotropic limit yields
in Fourier space perturbation of the distribution function
f_{a}=f_{a}^{(0)}(1+\chi_{a})
in the following form

\chi_{a}=\frac{B_{\parallel}^{(1)}}{B_{0}}\frac{m_{a}}{2T_{\perp a}^{(0)}}\bigg[v_{\perp}^{2}+\frac{T_{\perp a}^{(0)}}{T_{\parallel a}^{(0)}}\frac{k_{\parallel}v_{\parallel}v_{\perp}^{2}}{(\omega-k_{\parallel}v_{\parallel})}\bigg]+\Phi\frac{eZ_{a}}{T_{\parallel a}^{(0)}}\frac{k_{\parallel}v_{\parallel}}{(\omega-k_{\parallel}v_{\parallel})},

(227)

with the electrostatic potential
\Phi=iE_{\parallel}^{(1)}/k_{\parallel}
. Integrating (
227
) then yields a parallel collisionless heat flux

q_{\parallel a}^{(1)}=-v_{\textrm{th}\parallel a}n_{a}^{(0)}T_{\parallel a}^{(0)}\textrm{sign}(k_{\parallel})\Big(\zeta_{a}+2\zeta_{a}^{3}R(\zeta_{a})-3\zeta_{a}R(\zeta_{a})\Big)\Big[\frac{B_{\parallel}^{(1)}}{B_{0}}\frac{T_{\perp a}^{(0)}}{T_{\parallel a}^{(0)}}+\Phi\frac{eZ_{a}}{T_{\parallel a}^{(0)}}\Big],

(228)

with variable
\zeta_{a}=\omega/(|k_{\parallel}|v_{\textrm{th}\parallel a})
; parallel thermal speed
v_{\textrm{th}\parallel a}=\sqrt{2T_{\parallel a}/m_{a}}
,
plasma response function
R(\zeta_{a})=1+\zeta_{a}Z(\zeta_{a})
and
plasma dispersion function
Z(\zeta_{a})=i\sqrt{\pi}\exp(-\zeta_{a}^{2})[1+\textrm{erf}(i\zeta_{a})]
. Such a kinetic answer can be expressed in fluid variables
by searching for Landau fluid closures, for example by replacing the
R(\zeta_{a})
with its 3-pole Padé approximants

\displaystyle R_{3,2}(\zeta_{a})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1-i\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}\zeta_{a}}{1-i\frac{3\sqrt{\pi}}{2}\zeta_{a}-2\zeta_{a}^{2}+i\sqrt{\pi}\zeta_{a}^{3}};\qquad R_{3,1}(\zeta_{a})=\frac{1-i\frac{(4-\pi)}{\sqrt{\pi}}\zeta_{a}}{1-i\frac{4}{\sqrt{\pi}}\zeta_{a}-2\zeta_{a}^{2}+2i\frac{(4-\pi)}{\sqrt{\pi}}\zeta_{a}^{3}}.

(229)

The use of Padé approximants allows one to express (
228
) through lower-order moments and eliminate the explicit dependence on
\zeta_{a}
,
yielding collisionless heat fluxes in Fourier space

\displaystyle R_{3,2}(\zeta_{a}):\qquad q_{\parallel a}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}n_{a}^{(0)}v_{\textrm{th}\parallel a}\textrm{sign}(k_{\parallel})T_{\parallel a}^{(1)};

(230)

\displaystyle R_{3,1}(\zeta_{a}):\qquad q_{\parallel a}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3\pi-8}{4-\pi}\,p^{(0)}_{\parallel a}u^{(1)}_{\parallel a}-i\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{4-\pi}n^{(0)}_{a}v_{\textrm{th}\parallel a}\textrm{sign}(k_{\parallel})T^{(1)}_{\parallel a},

(231)

where
T^{(1)}_{\parallel a}
is perturbed temperature, and
u^{(1)}_{\parallel a}
is perturbed fluid velocity (mean value
u^{(0)}_{\parallel a}=0
is assumed).
The heat flux closure (
230
) was obtained by
Hammett & Perkins 1990
&
Snyder et al. 1997
and closure (
231
) is equation (2) in

Hunana et al. 2018
(or equation (3.211) in
Hunana et al. 2019a
).
In real space these collisionless heat fluxes become

\displaystyle R_{3,2}(\zeta_{a}):\qquad q_{\parallel a}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{\pi^{3/2}}n_{a}^{(0)}v_{\textrm{th}\parallel a}\textrm{V.P.}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{T^{(1)}_{\parallel a}(z+z^{\prime})-T^{(1)}_{\parallel a}(z-z^{\prime})}{z^{\prime}}dz^{\prime};

(232)

\displaystyle R_{3,1}(\zeta_{a}):\qquad q_{\parallel a}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3\pi-8}{4-\pi}p^{(0)}_{\parallel a}u^{(1)}_{\parallel a}-\frac{n^{(0)}_{a}v_{\textrm{th}\parallel a}}{\sqrt{\pi}(4-\pi)}\textrm{V.P.}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{T^{(1)}_{\parallel a}(z+z^{\prime})-T^{(1)}_{\parallel a}(z-z^{\prime})}{z^{\prime}}dz^{\prime},

(233)

where the non-locality presents itself as an integral over the entire magnetic field line, where temperatures everywhere along that field line
matter to determine the heat flux at a specific spatial point. Note that the thermal part of (
233
) is almost two times larger then
(
232
). The Cauchy principal value can be replaced by
\lim_{\epsilon\to+0}\int_{\epsilon}^{\infty}
.
This approach thus indeed allows one to have expressions for collisionless heat fluxes in a quasi-static approximation. However, as is well-known
these expressions are not very precise with respect to kinetic theory.
For example, the precision can be easily compared by plotting normalized heat fluxes
\hat{q}_{\parallel a}=\zeta_{a}+2\zeta_{a}^{3}R(\zeta_{a})-3\zeta_{a}R(\zeta_{a})
,
which is shown in Figure
1
. Weakly-damped regime with real valued
\zeta_{a}
is considered.
The left panel shows the imaginary part of
\hat{q}_{\parallel a}
and the right panel
shows the real part of
\hat{q}_{\parallel a}
.
Exact kinetic heat flux is solid black line, heat flux
R_{3,2}
is dashed magenta line and heat flux
R_{3,1}
is dashed cyan line.
For comparison, higher-order fluid models with approximants
R_{5,3}
(dotted blue line) and
R_{7,5}
(dashed red line)
are shown as well (see equation (A11) & (A38) in
Hunana et al. 2019a
).
The
R_{5,3}
model represents a dynamic closure at the 4th-order moment and the
R_{7,5}
model
represents a dynamic closure at the 6th-order moment, given by equation (5) & (8) of Hunana et al. 2018. The heat fluxes in these higher-order models are thus described
by their usual evolution equations, nevertheless, their precision can be compared with the same technique.
Which quasi-static heat flux is a better choice depends on the value of
\zeta_{a}
, because
the
R_{3,1}
has a higher power-series precision (for small
\zeta_{a}
) and the
R_{3,2}
has a higher asymptotic-series precision (for large
\zeta_{a}
).
Regime
\zeta_{a}\ll 1
can be viewed as isothermal and regime
\zeta_{a}\gg 1
can be viewed as adiabatic.
In the left panel of Figure
1
the
R_{3,1}
is more precise up to roughly
\zeta_{a}=2.3
and in the right panel up to
\zeta_{a}=1.6
. For larger
\zeta_{a}

values than shown, the
R_{3,1}
heat flux converges much slower to the correct zero values than the
R_{3,2}
, especially for the real part.

Figure 1:
Comparison of normalized collisionless heat fluxes
\hat{q}_{\parallel a}=\zeta_{a}+(2\zeta_{a}^{3}-3\zeta_{a})R(\zeta_{a})
in a weakly-damped regime
with real valued
\zeta_{a}
. Left panel: imaginary part of
\hat{q}_{\parallel a}
. Right panel: real part of
\hat{q}_{\parallel a}
.
Colors are described in the text. Our Braginskii-type models do not contain these
collisionless heat fluxes.

The major obstacle in precision for the quasi-static heat fluxes of Landau fluid models actually comes from the perpendicular heat flux
q_{\perp a}
(which is along the
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}
), because only a closure of
Snyder et al. 1997
with a crude Padé approximant
R_{1}(\zeta_{a})=1/(1-i\sqrt{\pi}\zeta_{a})
is available.
As a consequence, for large
\zeta_{a}
values the quasi-static heat flux
q_{\perp a}
fails to disappear and instead converges to a constant value. To recover the adiabatic behavior for
q_{\perp a}
, one has to abandon the idea of quasi-static
q_{\perp a}

and consider its evolution equation, with a closure at the 4th-order moment.
There is a vast amount of literature about Landau fluids with various approaches; see e. g.

Hammett & Perkins 1990
;
Hammett et al. 1992
;
Snyder et al. 1997
;
Snyder & Hammett 2001
;
Goswami et al. 2005
;
Passot & Sulem 2007
;
Passot et al. 2012
;
Sulem & Passot 2015
;
Joseph & Dimits 2016
;
Hunana et al. 2018
;
Ji & Joseph 2018
;
Chen et al. 2019
;
Wang et al. 2019
and references therein, where some authors also include
collisional effects. For a simple introductory guide to collisionless Landau fluids see
Hunana et al. 2019a
.
As a side note, Landau fluid closures are not constructed with any specific mode in mind
(as incorrectly criticized for example by
Scudder 2021
).
The closures are constructed universally for all the modes, so that
numerical simulations can be performed; see e.g.
Perrone et al. 2018
.
Interestingly, as discussed by
Meyrand et al. 2019
, from a non-linear perspective the effect of Landau damping might be canceled
out by the effect of plasma echo. From a linear perspective, the presence of drifts also modifies the Landau damping, because the variable
\zeta_{a}
which enters the
plasma response function
R(\zeta_{a})
then contains the drift velocity
u_{\parallel a}
. For sufficiently large drifts the sound mode
can be generated by the current-driven ion-acoustic instability; see e.g.
Gurnett & Bhattacharjee 2005
p. 368, or
Fitzpatrick 2015
p. 258;
and for a 3-component plasma which allows the net current to be zero by the ion-ion
(or the electron-ion & electron-electron) acoustic instability, see
Gary 1993
p. 44-55.

VIII.9.3
Ion-sound wave damping in homogeneous media: comparison of various models

To further clarify our limitations, it is useful to explore the linear properties of waves propagating along the ambient
magnetic field (assumed to be straight and aligned with the
z
-coordinate) in a homogeneous medium, in regimes that range from
the highly-collisional to the weakly-collisional ones.
Let us in particular consider the damping of a monochromatic ion-sound wave of parallel wavenumber
k_{\|}
in a proton-electron plasma where
the electrons are assumed to be cold. The latter assumption is not physically appropriate
because kinetic theory is not well-defined for cold electrons (see e.g. discussion in
Hunana et al. 2019a
p. 73)
but it allows one to simplify the presentation with a goal to describe the general behavior
and not to provide precise values of the damping rates. Four different models are compared in Figure
2
, all using the heuristic
BGK collisional operator,
which leads to much simpler calculations for models with a distribution function expanded around a bi-Maxwellian.

Figure 2:
Normalized damping rate
\zeta_{i}=\omega_{i}/(|k_{\parallel}|v_{\textrm{th}\parallel})
for a parallel propagating ion-sound wave as
a function of
k_{\parallel}\lambda_{\textrm{mfp}}
, where
\lambda_{\textrm{mfp}}
is a mean-free-path. Red line: Braginskii-type (isotropic) 13-moment model
with quasi-static stress-tensor and heat flux; Green line: Braginskii-type 13-moment model with evolution equations for the stress-tensor and heat flux;
Blue line: bi-Maxwellian 16-moment model with evolution equations for parallel and perpendicular pressures and (gyrotropic) heat fluxes;
Magenta line: Landau fluid model with quasi-static heat fluxes of
Snyder et al. 1997
.

The x-axis shows
k_{\|}\lambda_{\textrm{mfp}}
where
\lambda_{\textrm{mfp}}=v_{{\textrm{th}}\|}/\nu
is the ion mean-free-path
and
\nu
is the collisional frequency, so that
k_{\|}\lambda_{\textrm{mfp}}\ll 1
represents a highly-collisional regime and
k_{\|}\lambda_{\textrm{mfp}}\gg 1

represents a weakly-collisional regime.
The y-axis shows a damping rate as an imaginary part of
\zeta=\omega/(|k_{\parallel}|v_{\textrm{th}\parallel})
.
The usual isotropic 13-moment model (green line) and the anisotropic 16-moment model (blue line) with all the moments described by their time-dependent (dynamical) evolution equations
were discussed after equation (
225
). For the parallel sound mode at the linear level considered here, the 13-moment model is reduced to evolution equations
for
\rho,u_{z},p,\Pi_{zz},q_{z}
(we consider the case where
\Pi_{zz}
and
q_{z}
are coupled) and
the 16-moment model reduces to evolution equations for
\rho,u_{z},p_{\parallel},p_{\perp},q_{\parallel},q_{\perp}
(we consider mean equal pressures
p_{\parallel}^{(0)}=p_{\perp}^{(0)}
).
Figure
2
shows that these two models behave in a similar way:
both reach a maximum damping rate around
k_{\|}\lambda_{\textrm{mfp}}\sim 0.5-1

and converge towards zero damping rate in the collisionless regime (with only a small shift in
k_{\|}\lambda_{\textrm{mfp}}
between them).
In contrast, the red line, corresponding to the 13-moment model with the
\Pi_{zz}
&
q_{z}
taken in the quasi-static approximation,
shows that the damping rate does not reach a
maximum and instead continues to increase in a weakly-collisional regime,
and around
k_{\|}\lambda_{\textrm{mfp}}\sim 6.3
the sound mode stops existing (it becomes non-propagating with zero real frequency).
This is consequence of the quasi-static approximation for the stress-tensor
\Pi_{zz}\sim 1/\nu
,
which in the collisionless regime becomes unbounded (the parallel heat flux

q_{z}\sim 1/\nu
becomes unbounded as well, but this simply reflects an isothermal behavior with no damping present).
While a vanishing damping is preferred against a quantity that blows up in a weakly-collisional regime,
all three models are technically incorrect, because Landau damping provides a significant contribution for the damping rate
as the plasma becomes weakly collisional.
To illustrate the importance of Landau damping, we display by a magenta line the damping rate obtained with a Landau fluid
model which contains evolution equations for
\rho,u_{z},p_{\parallel},p_{\perp}
, but where the quasi-static
q_{\parallel}
&
q_{\perp}
are given by the collisionaly modified
3+1 closures of
Snyder et al. 1997
, i.e. their equations (48)-(49),
which for isotropic mean temperatures
T_{\parallel a}^{(0)}=T_{\perp a}^{(0)}
considered here, are equivalent to
(our thermal speed contains a factor of 2, which is not the case in that paper)

\displaystyle q_{\parallel a}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\,\frac{\frac{4}{3\pi-8}n_{a}^{(0)}v_{\textrm{th}\parallel a}^{2}}{\bar{\nu}_{a}+\frac{2\sqrt{\pi}}{3\pi-8}v_{\textrm{th}\parallel a}|k_{\parallel}|}ik_{\parallel}T_{\parallel a}^{(1)};\qquad q_{\perp a}^{(1)}=-\,\frac{\frac{1}{2}n_{a}^{(0)}v_{\textrm{th}\parallel a}^{2}}{\bar{\nu}_{a}+\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}v_{\textrm{th}\parallel a}|k_{\parallel}|}ik_{\parallel}T_{\perp a}^{(1)},

(234)

where in general
\bar{\nu}_{a}=\sum_{b}\nu_{ab}
. Technically, closures (
234
) are only applicable to a weakly-collisional regime because

q_{\parallel a}\neq 3q_{\perp a}
in the highly-collisional limit.
In spite of this and the additional difficulty associated with the cold electron limit considered here, an interesting point is that the behavior
of the damping rate is very close to the prediction of the three other models in the highly-collisional regime, while the damping rate
converges to a constant value
in the collisionless case. This is in fact analogous to the case of the damping of a pure sound wave in rarefied media,
which was considered by
Stubbe 1994
and
Stubbe & Sukhorukov 1999
.
In the former paper, the result of an experiment by
Meyer & Sessler 1957

(measuring the damping length of a sound wave of a given frequency
\omega
, emitted at one end of the domain filled with a rarefied neutral gas)
are compared with various theoretical models. The results are very similar to those presented here, and show in particular that the damping is
dominated by a nonlocal effect analogous to Landau damping when
2\nu/\omega
decreases below unity (see Fig. 6 and 7 of
Stubbe 1994
).
This simple result on the damping of an ion-sound wave shows that, in a homogeneous medium, a Braginskii-type model provides reasonable predictions
as long as the typical wavelength is larger than the mean free path, or equivalently, when its frequency stays below the collision frequency.
More sophisticated models are needed in the weakly collisional case, that should retain new contributions originating from a Landau-fluid closure.

VIII.9.4
Large gradients and large drifts

It is now of interest to consider inhomogeneous situations, where other applicability conditions apply for the Braginskii-type models.
In high energy-density laser-produced plasmas,
there are often situations relevant for inertial confinement fusion experiments, where the typical electron mean-free-path
becomes of the order of the typical scale of electron temperature gradients, or even larger. In this case the usual Braginskii formulas,
used for example for the Nernst effect (see e.g.
Lancia et al. 2014
) become invalid and have to be replaced by nonlocal expressions.
In this context an explicit nonlocal formula was proposed by
Luciani et al. 1983
for the electron thermal heat flux due to steep temperature gradients,
offering an improvement (in the one-dimensional case) for the Spitzer-Braginskii heat flux,
where one required proportionality constant is obtained by fitting from Fokker-Planck simulations.
Further extension to three dimensions was proposed by
Schurtz et al. 2000
, but it is to be noted that this approach is not appropriate
in the very weakly collisional case, as for example in the Solar corona when the density has significantly decreased.

Additional complications arise in a regime of weak collisionality.
In space physics, the collisionless heat flux
is typically associated with the free-streaming formula of
Hollweg 1974
;
Hollweg 1976

\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}^{\textrm{Hollweg}}=\frac{3}{2}p_{e}\boldsymbol{u}_{sw}\alpha,

(235)

where one multiplies the thermal energy of one electron
(3/2)T_{e}
(we take
k_{b}=1
in the entire paper) with the number density
n_{e}
and the solar wind
speed
\boldsymbol{u}_{sw}
. The free “bugger factor”
\alpha
as
Hollweg 1974
calls it,
is dependent on a given form of an electron distribution function where the tail had departed and run away. Note that the parallel
frictional
heat fluxes
(i.e. due to
small
differences in drifts
\delta\boldsymbol{u}
) of
Spitzer & Härm 1953
and
Braginskii 1965
are also independent of collisional frequencies,
even though derived from collisions, and up to the numerical values have the same form as (
235
).
As a side note, in the numerical model of
Spitzer & Härm 1953
the frictional heat flux
is technically incorrect because it does not satisfy the Onsager symmetry; see our Tables
I.4
&
I.2
, which was already criticized
by
Balescu 1988
(p. 268).
Of course, in our usual fluid formalism a tail of a distribution function can not suddenly depart. Even though our model contains
evolution equations for the perturbation of the 4th-order moment (i.e. a “reduced kurtosis” which describes if a distribution is tail-heavy or tail-light)
and also for the 5th-order moment (sometimes called a hyper-skewness), our distribution functions still have to remain sufficiently close to Maxwellian.
For the isotropic 5-moment model (i.e. strict Maxwellians),
the runaway effect is just represented through collisional contributions
\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}
and
Q_{ab}

which decrease to zero for large drifts
(see equations (
171
)-(
173
) derived in Appendix
G.3
;
see also
Dreicer 1959
;
Tanenbaum 1967
;
Burgers 1969
;
Schunk 1977
;
Balescu 1988
). We note that for sufficiently large drifts between species various
instabilities can develop with a subsequent development of turbulence, which should restrict the runaway effect long before relativistic effects.
Importantly, it is unclear how the heat flux collisional contributions
\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(3)}_{ab}\,{}^{\prime}
(and higher) would look like for unrestricted drifts,
because the collisional integrals seem exceedingly complicated. Even if calculated, only the drifts between species would be
allowed to be unrestricted, the distribution of each species will have to be restricted to remain close to Maxwellian.
For the simplest CGL plasmas (i.e. considering colliding strict bi-Maxwellians with no stress-tensors or heat fluxes), the corresponding
collisional integrals where numerically evaluated for selected cases by
Barakat & Schunk 1981
.
For a further particular case of unrestricted drifts only
along
the magnetic field and of Coulomb collisions,
Hellinger & Trávníček 2009
obtained exact analytic
forms for the collisional integrals (for
p_{\parallel}
&
p_{\perp}
),
which are however expressed through a double hypergeometric functions. Judging from the two papers above, a proper extension of our model
to an anisotropic regime with unrestricted drifts seems overly complicated.
Other approach for the heat flux modeling was presented by
Canullo et al. 1996
.

VIII.9.5
Comments on the positivity of the perturbed distribution function

An additional complication arises in a low-collisionality regime in the presence of sufficiently strong large-scale gradients.
Perturbations of the distribution function considered in equation (
1
) might become so large, that the corresponding model might become invalid.
The distribution function around which to expand is indeed not well-defined in this case.
Strictly speaking, in a weakly-collisional
(or a collisionless) regime, one should abandon the construction of fluid models derived from the Boltzmann equation, and perform kinetic simulations by directly evolving the Boltzmann equation.
Perhaps the best example is a radially expanding flow, such as the solar corona with emerging solar wind, where the spherical expansion
creates strong large-scale gradients and simultaneously drives the system towards a collisionless regime.
It seems that in this extreme case it might be indeed possible (but not with certainty) that the underlying distribution function can even become negative,
f_{a}<0
,
which is of course unphysical. We expect that our 21 & 22-moment models might fail in this particular situation,
even if evolution equations are retained, but as discussed below, we were unfortunately not able to reach clear conclusion and
further research is needed to clearly establish the areas of validity.

The
f_{a}<0
was criticized for example by
Scudder 2021
;
Cranmer & Schiff 2021
(and references therein), on an example of 8-moment models in
a quasi-static approximation. It is in fact questionable if the
f_{a}<0
can be shown in a quasi-static approximation.
It is necessary to distinguish between two different cases, depending if large-scale gradients are present or absent during the
transition into the low-collisionality regime.
In the homogeneous case, the situation is clear because one needs to describe the presence of waves with frequencies
\omega
,
and neglecting the time-derivative
d/dt
in the evolution equations automatically imposes requirement
\omega\ll\nu
, i.e.
the collisional frequencies
\nu
must remain sufficiently large. In this case, it is erroneous to simply take the quasi-static heat flux
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\sim 1/\nu
,
evaluate it for some arbitrarily small
\nu
and claim that
f_{a}<0
. Instead, it is necessary to retain the evolution equations with
d\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}/dt
; see e.g.
(
41
), (
51
) or the coupled system (
135
)-(
139
),
which precludes one from reaching direct interpretation that
f_{a}<0
(unless one calculates the eigenvector and shows otherwise).
The negativity of the distribution function
may not take place, and as a consequence, the procedure seems inadequate for disproving the moment method of Grad in a homogeneous low-collisionality regime.
The situation is much less clear when large-scale gradients are present, as in the example of the solar wind expansion.
In that case, it is possible to argue that keeping the evolution equations
and solving an initial value problem might help only temporarily, because the system eventually has to converge to some stationary solution,
which might show that
f_{a}<0
. Such a possibility seems to be implied by the simple 1D radially expanding quasi-static models (see e.g.
Cranmer & Schiff 2021
,
and references therein). The quasi-static approximation can however be questioned in this case as well, but from a different perspective.
Introducing a heat flux or a stress-tensor is analogous to introducing a new degree of freedom into a system, and if this new degree of freedom is not restricted in any way,
it might of course yield an unphysical system. In plasma physics, degrees of freedom are usually restricted by associated instabilities that
develop, which can not be revealed in a quasi-static approximation (even if an instability is non-propagating).
Useful examples are the anisotropic CGL and 16-moment models described above.
Using a quasi-static approximation, one might erroneously conclude that the temperature anisotropy can grow without bounds in these models, whereas considering
evolution equations reveals the firehose and mirror instabilities, which can restrict the anisotropy. Similar situation might be applicable here,
where sufficiently large drifts (and possibly large heat fluxes and stress-tensors) might cause various instabilities and also development of
turbulence, but further clarifications are needed if our fluid models contain some of these instabilities,
especially considering that our collisional contributions are valid only when differences in drifts between species are
much smaller than their thermal velocities. In this regard, it is not clear if it is appropriate to neglect the Alfvénic fluctuations
in the radially expanding models. Finally, it is also not clear if it is physically meaningful to show
f_{a}<0
by skipping the stress-tensor in the expansions of Grad (which is a 2nd-order moment before the 3rd-order heat flux moment), because its contributions to a total
f_{a}

might be significant. For a sufficient proof that the
f_{a}
can become negative, it might be necessary to consider at least the 13-moment model,
where both stress-tensors and heat fluxes are retained.

VIII.10
Conclusions

We have discussed various generalizations of the 21-moment model of
Braginskii 1958
;
Braginskii 1965
:
1) We have presented a multi-fluid formulation
for arbitrary masses
m_{a}
&
m_{b}
and arbitrary temperatures
T_{a}
&
T_{b}
. 2) All the
fluid moments are described by their evolution equations, whose left hand sides are given
in a fully non-linear form. 3) Formulation with evolution equations has an important consequence that the model
does not become divergent (unbounded) if a regime of low-collisionality is encountered.
4) For a one ion-electron plasma we have provided all the Braginskii transport coefficients in a fully analytic form
for a general ion charge
Z_{i}
(and arbitrary strength of magnetic field).
5) We have also provided fully analytic higher-order transport coefficients (for
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}
and
\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}
), which are not typically given.
6) All the electron coefficients were further
generalized to multi-ion plasmas. 7) We have considered coupling between viscosity-tensors and heat fluxes, where a heat flux enters a
viscosity-tensor and a viscosity-tensor enters a heat flux. As a consequence, we have introduced new higher-order physical
effects even for the simplest case of unmagnetized one ion-electron plasma of
Spitzer & Härm 1953
.
For example, the electron rate of strain tensor
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{e}
enters the electron heat fluxes even linearly, and thus, it subsequently linearly enters the
momentum exchange rates; see equation (
127
).
8) We have formulated the 22-moment model which is a natural extension of the 21-moment model, where one takes into account fully contracted
scalar perturbations

\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a}
entering the decomposition of the 4-th order moment
X_{ijkl}^{a(4)}
; see equation (
129
).
Collisional contributions for this model with arbitrary masses and temperatures are given in Section
VII.1

and supplement those given in Section
II.1
for the
21-moment model. Interestingly, scalar perturbations
\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a}
modify the energy exchange rates,
see equation (
140
) or (
177
). In the quasi-static approximation, scalar perturbations
\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a}

can be written as divergence of heat flux vectors with their own heat conductivities;
see for example solutions for a one ion-electron plasma with the ion heat conductivities (
149
) and
the electron heat conductivities (
162
). These corrections remain small in the highly-collisional regime, but might
become significant at small wavelengths and/or at large frequencies.

Our model can be useful for direct numerical simulations, as well as for quick calculation of transport coefficients
in a quasi-static approximation. We provide three examples for coupling between two species. Thermal conductivities
and viscosities for unmagnetized proton-electron plasma (without
m_{e}/m_{p}
expansions) were presented in Section
VIII.8
,
and two examples for proton-alpha particles and deuterium-tritium were moved to Appendix
N
.
Our model can also be useful from an observational perspective.
For example, the parallel thermal heat flux
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}
of
Braginskii 1965
and
Spitzer & Härm 1953
(they differ only by 3.16 vs 3.20 factors
rounded as 3.2) is sometimes analyzed in observational studies; see e.g.
Salem et al. 2003
;
Bale et al. 2013
;
Halekas et al. 2021
and
Verscharen et al. 2019
(page 61).
It is also measured in (exospheric) kinetic numerical simulations
(
Landi et al. 2014
)
.
Our model suggests that it would be beneficial to analyze both parallel heat fluxes, which for
Z_{i}=1
read

\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}=\frac{\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(3)}_{e}}{2}=-3.2\frac{p_{e}}{m_{e}\nu_{ei}}\nabla T_{e};\qquad\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{e}=-110.7\frac{p_{e}^{2}}{\rho_{e}m_{e}\nu_{ei}}\nabla T_{e},

(236)

and which can be analyzed with the same techniques.
For long parallel mean-free-paths (low collisionality regime), both heat fluxes naturally have to become non-local and independent of the mean-free-path.
Our limitations are described in Section
VIII.9
, and “flattening/saturation” of heat fluxes due to the runaway effect and
Landau damping is not captured in our model. Our model is aimed at the highly-collisional regime and in the
low-collisionality regime our heat fluxes are just described by their evolution equations, where
the collisional right hand sides are small. Nevertheless, it would be interesting to see if
in observational studies or kinetic simulations the
\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{e}
could be
described by a free-streaming formula similar to the one of
Hollweg 1974
;
Hollweg 1976
, in a form
\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{e}=(3/2)(p_{e}^{2}/\rho_{e})\boldsymbol{u}_{sw}\alpha_{5}
, where the
“bugger factor”
\alpha_{5}
has to be determined from a given form of a distribution function, or if such a concept does not apply for
\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{e}
.
As a side note, concerning collisionless heat fluxes for plasmas where spherical expansion and large drifts are not present and Landau damping dominates,
our model actually implies
that a correct interpretation should not be that Landau damping diminishes/saturates the heat flux in a low-collisionality regime. The
correct interpretation is that Landau damping creates the collisionless heat flux. Collisionless Landau fluid closures for quasi-static
parallel scalar
X^{(5)}_{\parallel}
can be found in
Hunana et al. 2019a
(p. 84).
In addition to (
236
), it might be also useful to analyze the scalar perturbation, which for
Z_{i}=1
reads

\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{e}=+83.8\frac{p_{e}^{2}}{\nu_{ei}^{2}\rho_{e}m_{e}}\nabla^{2}T_{e}.

(237)

Our multi-fluid model might be also useful for modeling of enrichment of minor ion abundancies
in stellar atmospheres, because of the very precise thermal force (thermal diffusion).
Let us summarize the thermal force description in three major models: the model of

Burgers 1969
-
Schunk 1977
, the model of
Killie et al. 2004
, and our model. Of course, all three models are formulated as a general multi-fluid,
but for a simplicity of the discussion let us simplify and compare only thermal forces given by

\displaystyle\textrm{Burgers-Schunk:}\qquad\boldsymbol{R}_{e}^{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{5}\frac{\rho_{e}}{p_{e}}\nu_{ei}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e};

(238)

\displaystyle\textrm{Killie et al.:}\qquad\boldsymbol{R}_{e}^{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+\frac{6}{35}\frac{\rho_{e}}{p_{e}}\nu_{ei}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e};

(239)

\displaystyle\textrm{present paper:}\qquad\boldsymbol{R}_{e}^{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+\frac{21}{10}\frac{\rho_{e}}{p_{e}}\nu_{ei}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}-\frac{3}{56}\frac{\rho_{e}^{2}}{p_{e}^{2}}\nu_{ei}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{e}.

(240)

Note that the viscosity-tensors are not required to describe the thermal force,
and focusing only at the heat fluxes, instead of the 13-moment model of Burgers-Schunk, one can consider only the 8-moment model.
Similarly, instead of our 21 & 22-moment models, one can consider only the 11-moment model.
In general, the parallel thermal heat flux is given by
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}=-\gamma_{0}p_{e}/(m_{e}\nu_{ei})\nabla T_{e}
and
the resulting parallel thermal force by
\boldsymbol{R}_{e}^{T}=-\beta_{0}n_{e}\nabla T_{e}
,
with coefficients
\gamma_{0}
and
\beta_{0}
. From the work of
Spitzer & Härm 1953
, for
Z_{i}=1
the correct coefficient of thermal conductivity is
\gamma_{0}=3.203
and
the correct coefficient of thermal force is
\beta_{0}=0.703
.
The model of Burgers-Schunk (
238
) has thermal conductivity
\gamma_{0}=1.34
,
and with that value it describes
the thermal force actually quite accurately, yielding
\beta_{0}=0.804

(for other
Z_{i}
values see comparison in Table
I.2
in Appendix
I
).
However, a problem arises if one uses the correct value of thermal conductivity
\gamma_{0}=3.2
in the expression (
238
), which
overestimates the thermal force.

Killie et al. 2004
developed a different 8-moment model, where the expansion is done differently than in equation (
1
),
with the goal to improve the heat flux and the thermal
force of Burgers-Schunk. The model is described in Appendix
I.2
. For
Z_{i}=1
, its heat flux value is
\gamma_{0}=3.92
, which greatly improves the
model of Burgers-Schunk and for that value it also improves the thermal force, yielding
\beta_{0}=0.672
.
Additionally, now one can use the correct
\gamma_{0}=3.2
value in expression (
239
) and the thermal force will be roughly
correct (and 7/2 times smaller than Burgers-Schunk). However, as we point out in Appendix
I

(see Table
I.4
), the model of
Killie et al. 2004
breaks the Onsager symmetry between
the frictional heat flux and the thermal force. The numerical model of
Spitzer & Härm 1953
also does not satisfy
the Onsager symmetry and its frictional heat flux is technically incorrect, even though in this case the discrepancies are small.
Our model satisfies the Onsager symmetry, it has thermal conductivity
\gamma_{0}=3.1616
and thermal force
\beta_{0}=0.711

(the same as Braginskii). In summary, our multi-fluid model has a very precise thermal force (
240
) with precision equal to
Braginskii 1965
,
and we thus offer an improvement to the multi-fluid models of
Burgers 1969
-
Schunk 1977
and
Killie et al. 2004
.

This work was supported by the European Research Council in the frame
of the Consolidating Grant ERC-2017-CoG771310-PI2FA “Partial Ionisation: Two-Fluid Approach”, led by Elena Khomenko.
Anna Tenerani acknowledges support of the NASA Heliophysics Supporting Research Grant #80NSSC18K1211.
We acknowledge support of the NSF EPSCoR RII-Track-1 Cooperative Agreement No. OIA-1655280 “Connecting the Plasma Universe to Plasma
Technology in Alabama”, led by Gary P. Zank. Gary M. Webb was funded in part by NASA grant 80NSSC19K0075.

Appendix A
General evolution equations

We consider Boltzmann equation (in CGS units)

\frac{\partial f_{a}}{\partial t}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla f_{a}+\frac{eZ_{a}}{m_{a}}(\boldsymbol{E}+\frac{1}{c}\boldsymbol{v}\times\boldsymbol{B})\cdot\nabla_{v}f_{a}=C(f_{a}),

(A1)

where “a” is a species index and
C(f_{a})=\sum_{b}C_{ab}(f_{a},f_{b})
is the Landau collisional operator, so equation (
A1
) is called the Landau equation.
One defines the usual number density
n_{a}=\int f_{a}d^{3}v
;
density
\rho_{a}=m_{a}n_{a}
, fluid velocity
\boldsymbol{u}_{a}=(1/n_{a})\int\boldsymbol{v}f_{a}d^{3}v
and fluctuating velocity
\boldsymbol{c}_{a}=\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}
,
and further defines the pressure tensor
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}
, heat flux tensor
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}
, 4th-order moment
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}
, and 5th-order & 6th-order moments
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(5)}_{a}
,

\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(6)}_{a}
according to

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{a}\int\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}f_{a}d^{3}v;\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}=m_{a}\int\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}f_{a}d^{3}v;\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}=m_{a}\int\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}f_{a}d^{3}v;

(A2)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{a}^{(5)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{a}\int\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}f_{a}d^{3}v;\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{a}^{(6)}=m_{a}\int\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}f_{a}d^{3}v.

(A3)

Writing of the tensor product
\otimes
is suppressed everywhere and

\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}=\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\otimes\boldsymbol{c}_{a}
. For complicated fluid models the species index
{}^{\prime}a^{\prime}
often blurs the clarity of the tensor algebra, and thus
in the vector notation (
A2
) we emphasize tensors of second-rank and above with the double overbar symbol.
Sometimes we move the index
{}^{\prime}a^{\prime}
freely up and down (which here does not represent any mathematical operation),
and in the index notation the index
{}^{\prime}a^{\prime}
is often dropped completely, so for example
p_{ij}^{a}=m_{a}\int c_{i}^{a}c_{j}^{a}f_{a}d^{3}v

and
p_{ij}=m\int c_{i}c_{j}fd^{3}v
are equivalent. The Einstein summation convention does not apply for the species index
{}^{\prime}a^{\prime}
,
and summations over other particle species are written down explicitly. The divergence is defined through the first index
(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})_{j}=\partial_{i}p^{a}_{ij}
.

Here we do not consider ionization and recombination processes and the Landau collisional operator conserves
the number of particles
\int C(f_{a})d^{3}v=0
for each species. One defines a unit vector in the direction of magnetic field

\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}=\boldsymbol{B}/|\boldsymbol{B}|
, cyclotron frequency
\Omega_{a}=eZ_{a}|\boldsymbol{B}|/(m_{a}c)
and convective derivative
d_{a}/dt=\partial/\partial t+\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\cdot\nabla
.
It is also useful to define a symmetric operator
{}^{\prime}S^{\prime}
,
which acts on a matrix as
A_{ij}^{S}=A_{ij}+A_{ji}
and on a tensor of 3rd-rank as
A_{ijk}^{S}=A_{ijk}+A_{jki}+A_{kij}
, i.e. it cycles around
all indices. We often use operator trace
Tr
and unit matrix
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}
, where
\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{A}}}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}:\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{A}}}
, and operator
’:’ represents double contraction. We also use
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}-\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}
.

To derive the model of
Braginskii 1965
with the moment method of Grad, it is necessary to consider the evolution equation for the 5th-order moment

\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(5)}_{a}
and perform a closure at
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(6)}_{a}
. Integrating (
A1
) over velocity space yields the the following hierarchy of evolution equations

\displaystyle\frac{\partial n_{a}}{\partial t}+\nabla\cdot(n_{a}\boldsymbol{u}_{a})=0;

(A4)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{u}_{a}}{\partial t}+\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}-\frac{eZ_{a}}{m_{a}}\Big(\boldsymbol{E}+\frac{1}{c}\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\times\boldsymbol{B}\Big)=\frac{\boldsymbol{R}_{a}}{\rho_{a}};

(A5)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}}{\partial t}+\nabla\cdot\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}+\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}\big)+\Big[\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}\Big]^{S}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(2)}_{a};

(A6)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}}{\partial t}+\nabla\cdot\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}+\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}\big)+\Big[\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}\Big]^{S}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(3)}_{a}-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}\Big[\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}\Big]^{S};

(A7)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}+\nabla\cdot\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(5)}_{a}+\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}\big)+\Big[\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}\big(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}\big)\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}\Big]^{S}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(4)}_{a}-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}\big[\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}\big]^{S};

(A8)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(5)}_{a}+\nabla\cdot\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(6)}_{a}+\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(5)}_{a}\big)+\Big[\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(5)}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(5)}_{a}-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}\big(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}\big)\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}\Big]^{S}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(5)}_{a}-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}\big[\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}\big]^{S},

(A9)

where the collisional contributions on the right hand sides are given by (
5
).
It is also possible to define a general n-th-order moment
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(n)}_{a}
and collisional contributions
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(n)}_{a}

X^{(n)}_{r_{1}r_{2}\ldots r_{n}}=m\int c_{r_{1}}c_{r_{2}}\ldots c_{r_{n}}fd^{3}v;\qquad Q^{(n)}_{r_{1}r_{2}\ldots r_{n}}=m\int c_{r_{1}}c_{r_{2}}\ldots c_{r_{n}}C(f)d^{3}v,

(A10)

together with symmetric operator ’S’ that cycles around all of its indices

\big[X^{(n)}\big]^{S}_{r_{1}r_{2}r_{3}\ldots r_{n}}=X^{(n)}_{r_{1}r_{2}r_{3}\ldots r_{n}}+X^{(n)}_{r_{2}r_{3}\ldots r_{n}r_{1}}+X^{(n)}_{r_{3}\ldots r_{n}r_{1}r_{2}}+\cdots\cdots+X^{(n)}_{r_{n}r_{1}r_{2}r_{3}\ldots r_{n-1}},

(A11)

(so that it contains “n” terms) and derive the following evolution equation for
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(n)}_{a}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(n)}_{a}+\nabla\cdot\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(n+1)}_{a}+\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(n)}_{a}\big)+\Big[\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(n)}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(n)}_{a}-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(n-1)}_{a}\Big]^{S}

\displaystyle=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(n)}_{a}-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}\Big[\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(n-1)}_{a}\Big]^{S},

(A12)

valid for
n\geq 2
. The left hand side of (
A12
) is equal to the collisionless equation (12.16) of
Hunana et al. 2019b
.
Evolution equations (
A6
)-(
A9
) then can be obtained easily by
evaluation of (
A12
). Note that definition (
A10
) yields

\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}
,
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(3)}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}
,
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(4)}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}
, however,
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(1)}=0
.

As was pointed out already by
Grad 1949a
;
Grad 1949b
, who developed the moment approach considering rarified gases, because fluid moments are symmetric in all of their indices,
a general n-th order moment
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(n)}
contains
\binom{n+2}{n}=(n+1)(n+2)/2
distinct
(scalar) components. So the density has 1, velocity has 3, pressure tensor has 6, heat flux tensor has 10,

\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(4)}
has 15, and
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(5)}
has 21 scalar components. The system (
A4
)-(
A9
) thus represents 56-moment model.

Appendix B
Tensorial Hermite Decomposition

In the famous work of
Grad 1949a
;
Grad 1949b
;
Grad 1958
,
the so-called
tensorial
Hermite decomposition is used, which is a generalization of the 1D version.
The 1D Hermite polynomials of order “m” are defined as

{H}^{(m)}(x)=(-1)^{m}e^{\frac{x^{2}}{2}}\frac{d^{m}}{dx^{m}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}},

(B1)

and evaluated step by step as
{H}^{(0)}=1
;
{H}^{(1)}=x
;
{H}^{(2)}=x^{2}-1
;
{H}^{(3)}=x^{3}-3x
;

{H}^{(4)}=x^{4}-6x^{2}+3
;
{H}^{(5)}=x^{5}-10x^{3}+15x
. So polynomials of even-order contain only even-powers of
x
and polynomials of odd-order
contain only odd-powers of
x
. These polynomials are orthogonal to each other by

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{H}^{(n)}(x){H}^{(m)}(x)e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}dx=n!\delta_{nm}.

(B2)

Note that the “weight”
\exp(-x^{2}/2)
was used by Grad instead of quantum-mechanical
\exp(-x^{2})
.
Of course, it is important to use the correct weight with both classes of Hermite polynomials.
Curiously, if the weight is accidentally mismatched (i.e. by using
\exp(-x^{2})
in our (
B2
) or
\exp(-x^{2}/2)
in the quantum version),
in addition to naturally wrong numerical constants, the even-even and odd-odd couples of polynomials are not orthogonal any more!
Generalization to tensors for isotropic Maxwellian distribution reads

\tilde{H}^{(m)}_{r_{1},r_{2}\ldots r_{m}}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}})=(-1)^{m}e^{\frac{\tilde{c}^{2}}{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{c}_{r_{1}}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{c}_{r_{2}}}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{c}_{r_{m}}}e^{-\frac{\tilde{c}^{2}}{2}}.

(B3)

We use the same notation as
Balescu 1988
, where
reducible
Hermite polynomials are denoted with tilde, and

irreducible
polynomials have no tilde. We added tilde on normalized
\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}
to make transitioning to usual fluid moments straightforward.
Then explicit evaluation step by step gives

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{(0)}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}})=1;

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{(1)}_{i}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}})=\tilde{c}_{i};

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{(2)}_{ij}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}})=\tilde{c}_{i}\tilde{c}_{j}-\delta_{ij};

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{(3)}_{ijk}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}})=\tilde{c}_{i}\tilde{c}_{j}\tilde{c}_{k}-\big(\delta_{ij}\tilde{c}_{k}+\delta_{jk}\tilde{c}_{i}+\delta_{ik}\tilde{c}_{j}\big);

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{(4)}_{ijkl}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}})=\tilde{c}_{i}\tilde{c}_{j}\tilde{c}_{k}\tilde{c}_{l}-\big(\delta_{ij}\tilde{c}_{k}\tilde{c}_{l}+\delta_{jk}\tilde{c}_{l}\tilde{c}_{i}+\delta_{kl}\tilde{c}_{i}\tilde{c}_{j}+\delta_{li}\tilde{c}_{j}\tilde{c}_{k}+\delta_{ik}\tilde{c}_{j}\tilde{c}_{l}+\delta_{jl}\tilde{c}_{i}\tilde{c}_{k}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad+\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{il}\delta_{jk},

(B4)

and quickly starts to grow

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{(5)}_{ijklm}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{i}\tilde{c}_{j}\tilde{c}_{k}\tilde{c}_{l}\tilde{c}_{m}-\big(\delta_{ij}\tilde{c}_{k}\tilde{c}_{l}\tilde{c}_{m}+\delta_{jk}\tilde{c}_{l}\tilde{c}_{i}\tilde{c}_{m}+\delta_{kl}\tilde{c}_{i}\tilde{c}_{j}\tilde{c}_{m}+\delta_{li}\tilde{c}_{j}\tilde{c}_{k}\tilde{c}_{m}+\delta_{ik}\tilde{c}_{j}\tilde{c}_{l}\tilde{c}_{m}

(B5)

\displaystyle+\delta_{jl}\tilde{c}_{i}\tilde{c}_{k}\tilde{c}_{m}+\delta_{im}\tilde{c}_{j}\tilde{c}_{k}\tilde{c}_{l}+\delta_{jm}\tilde{c}_{i}\tilde{c}_{k}\tilde{c}_{l}+\delta_{km}\tilde{c}_{i}\tilde{c}_{j}\tilde{c}_{l}+\delta_{lm}\tilde{c}_{i}\tilde{c}_{j}\tilde{c}_{k}\big)

\displaystyle+\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}\tilde{c}_{m}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}\tilde{c}_{m}+\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}\tilde{c}_{m}+\delta_{ij}\delta_{km}\tilde{c}_{l}+\delta_{ij}\delta_{lm}\tilde{c}_{k}

\displaystyle+\delta_{jk}\delta_{lm}\tilde{c}_{i}+\delta_{jk}\delta_{im}\tilde{c}_{l}+\delta_{kl}\delta_{im}\tilde{c}_{j}+\delta_{kl}\delta_{jm}\tilde{c}_{i}+\delta_{li}\delta_{jm}\tilde{c}_{k}

\displaystyle+\delta_{li}\delta_{km}\tilde{c}_{j}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jm}\tilde{c}_{l}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{lm}\tilde{c}_{j}+\delta_{jl}\delta_{im}\tilde{c}_{k}+\delta_{jl}\delta_{km}\tilde{c}_{i}.

The choice of Grad with
\exp(-x^{2}/2)
has a great benefit, because no numerical constants are present
in the entire hierarchy of Hermite polynomials, which is not the case for the weight
\exp(-x^{2})
.
Here, numerical factors appear only after one applies contractions (trace) at the above expressions. Similarly to the 1D case,
polynomials of even-order contain only terms with even number of velocities
\tilde{c}
, and polynomials of odd-order only terms
with odd number of
\tilde{c}
.

For Maxwellian distribution, the normalized velocity is

\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}=\sqrt{\frac{m_{a}}{T_{a}}}(\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a})=\sqrt{\frac{m_{a}}{T_{a}}}\boldsymbol{c},

(B6)

where for simplicity we suppress to write species index “a” for velocity
\boldsymbol{c}
in the expressions that follow, and for many other variables as well
(the Hermite decomposition is done indendently for each species, and species variable “a” just makes expressions more blurry).
It is possible to work both in normalized and
physical units. The entire distribution function can be written as

f_{a}=f^{(0)}_{a}(1+\chi_{a})=n_{a}\Big(\frac{m_{a}}{T_{a}}\Big)^{3/2}\phi^{(0)}(1+\chi_{a});\qquad\phi^{(0)}=\frac{e^{-\frac{\tilde{c}^{2}}{2}}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}},

(B7)
