\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{n-m+1}\frac{(2n+1)!}{2^{n-m}(2m+1)!(n-m)!}\Big(\frac{p}{\rho}\Big)^{n-m}X^{(2m+1)};

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}^{(2n)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=2}^{n-1}(-1)^{n-m+1}\frac{(2n)!}{2^{n-m}(2m)!(n-m)!}\Big(\frac{p}{\rho}\Big)^{n-m}\widetilde{X}^{(2m)}.

(B142)

By using equations (12.49)-(12.54) from
Hunana et al. 2019b
, we calculated the corresponding dispersion relations, which are
summarized in Table
B.3
.

\pm\zeta=

;

;

;

;

;

Table B.3:
Summary of Hermite closures and corresponding dispersion relations for parallel propagating ion-acoustic mode (electrons are cold),
where
\zeta=\omega/(|k_{\parallel}|v_{\textrm{th}})
. No spurious instabilities are present. Spurious instabilities occur if one
prescribes at the last retained moment closures
\widetilde{X}^{(2n)}=0
or
X^{(2n+1)}=0
.

Curiously, from
Hunana et al. 2019a
the not “well-behaved” Padé approximants of plasma dispersion function
R(\zeta)
that contain no Landau damping
read

\displaystyle R_{4,5}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1-(2/3)\zeta^{2}}{1-4\zeta^{2}+(4/3)\zeta^{4}};

\displaystyle R_{6,9}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1-(8/5)\zeta^{2}+(4/15)\zeta^{4}}{1-6\zeta^{2}+4\zeta^{4}-(8/15)\zeta^{6}};

\displaystyle R_{8,13}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1-(94/35)\zeta^{2}+(20/21)\zeta^{4}-(8/105)\zeta^{6}}{1-8\zeta^{2}+8\zeta^{4}-(32/15)\zeta^{6}+(16/105)\zeta^{8}}.

(B143)

Comparing (
B143
) with Table
B.3
, one comes to a non-obvious observation that
denominators of the above approximants are equal to dispersion relations obtained
with Hermite closures
h^{(4)}=0
,
h^{(6)}=0
and
h^{(8)}=0
. This observation is analogous with Landau fluid closures
when electrons are cold; see equation (3.358) of
Hunana et al. 2019a
.
Thus, it is expected that for proton-electron plasma with finite temperatures
(and with electron inertia retained) these three dispersion relations will be equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{T_{\parallel e}^{(0)}}{T_{\parallel p}^{(0)}}R_{n,n^{\prime}}(\zeta_{p})+R_{n,n^{\prime}}(\zeta_{e})=0,

(B144)

which we did not verify.

Appendix C
Evolution equations for 22-moment model

Here we use evolution equations (
A4
)-(
A9
), and by applying contractions at these equations we obtain
the 22-moment model in detail.
The pressure tensor is decomposed as
p_{ij}^{a}=p_{a}\delta_{ij}+\Pi^{a(2)}_{ij}
, where the scalar pressure
p_{a}=p_{ii}^{a}/3
.
Instead of considering full moments
X^{(3)}_{ijk}
,
X^{(4)}_{ijkl}
,
X^{(5)}_{ijklm}
,
X^{(6)}_{ijklmn}
, one only considers contracted vectors and matrices

X^{a(3)}_{i}=X^{a(3)}_{ijj};\qquad X^{a(4)}_{ij}=X^{a(4)}_{ijkk};\qquad X^{a(5)}_{i}=X^{a(5)}_{ijjkk};\qquad X^{a(6)}_{ij}=X^{a(6)}_{ijkkll}.

(C1)

The even-order moments are decomposed by separating the traceless viscosity-tensors
\Pi^{(2n)}_{ij}

X^{a(4)}_{ij}=\frac{\delta_{ij}}{3}X^{a(4)}+\Pi^{a(4)}_{ij};\qquad X^{a(6)}_{ij}=\frac{\delta_{ij}}{3}X^{a(6)}+\Pi^{a(6)}_{ij},

(C2)

where the fully contracted (scalars)
X^{a(4)}=X^{a(4)}_{iijj}
,
X^{a(6)}=X^{a(6)}_{iijjkk}
.
The scalars are further decomposed into their “Maxwellian core” and
a perturbation around this core (which is denoted by wide tilde)

X^{(4)}_{a}=\textrm{Tr}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}=15\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}}+\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a};\qquad X^{(6)}_{a}=\textrm{Tr}\textrm{Tr}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(6)}_{a}=105\frac{p_{a}^{3}}{\rho_{a}^{2}}+\widetilde{X}^{(6)}_{a}.

(C3)

As in
Braginskii 1965
, we use notation with the Boltzmann constant
k_{B}=1
,
and the temperature is defined as
T_{a}=p_{a}/n_{a}
. Note that
m_{a}/T_{a}=\rho_{a}/p_{a}
.

C.1
Decomposition of moments

The heat flux tensor
q_{ijk}
and moments
X^{(4)}_{ijkl}
,
X^{(5)}_{ijklm}
are decomposed according to (see Appendix
B
)

\displaystyle q_{ijk}^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{5}\Big[\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}^{a}\big]_{ijk}^{S};

(C4)

\displaystyle X_{ijkl}^{a(4)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{15}\Big(15\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}}+\widetilde{X}^{a(4)}\Big)\big(\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}\big)

(C5)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{7}\Big[\Pi_{ij}^{a(4)}\delta_{kl}+\Pi_{ik}^{a(4)}\delta_{jl}+\Pi_{il}^{a(4)}\delta_{jk}+\Pi_{jk}^{a(4)}\delta_{il}+\Pi_{jl}^{a(4)}\delta_{ik}+\Pi_{kl}^{a(4)}\delta_{ij}\Big];

\displaystyle X^{a(5)}_{s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}s_{4}s_{5}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{35}\Big[X_{s_{1}}^{a(5)}\big(\delta_{s_{2}s_{3}}\delta_{s_{4}s_{5}}+\delta_{s_{2}s_{4}}\delta_{s_{3}s_{5}}+\delta_{s_{2}s_{5}}\delta_{s_{3}s_{4}}\big)

(C6)

\displaystyle+X_{s_{2}}^{a(5)}\big(\delta_{s_{1}s_{3}}\delta_{s_{4}s_{5}}+\delta_{s_{1}s_{4}}\delta_{s_{3}s_{5}}+\delta_{s_{1}s_{5}}\delta_{s_{3}s_{4}}\big)

\displaystyle+X_{s_{3}}^{a(5)}\big(\delta_{s_{1}s_{2}}\delta_{s_{4}s_{5}}+\delta_{s_{1}s_{4}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{5}}+\delta_{s_{1}s_{5}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{4}}\big)

\displaystyle+X_{s_{4}}^{a(5)}\big(\delta_{s_{1}s_{2}}\delta_{s_{3}s_{5}}+\delta_{s_{1}s_{3}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{5}}+\delta_{s_{1}s_{5}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{3}}\big)

\displaystyle+X_{s_{5}}^{a(5)}\big(\delta_{s_{1}s_{2}}\delta_{s_{3}s_{4}}+\delta_{s_{1}s_{3}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{4}}+\delta_{s_{1}s_{4}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{3}}\big)\Big],

where the highest-order irreducible parts of moments (
C4
)-(
C6
) denoted as
\sigma^{(3)^{\prime}}_{ijk}
,
\sigma^{(4)^{\prime}}_{ijkl}
,

\sigma^{(5)^{\prime}}_{ijklm}
are neglected (which provides the reduction from 56-moment model to 22-moment model).

C.2
Evolution equation for scalar pressure
p_{a}

By using decomposition
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}=p_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}
, evolution equation for scalar pressure
p_{a}
is obtained by applying

(1/3)\textrm{Tr}
on the pressure tensor equation (
A6
), yielding

\displaystyle\frac{\partial p_{a}}{\partial t}+\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\cdot\nabla p_{a}+\frac{5}{3}p_{a}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\frac{2}{3}\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}:(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})=\frac{1}{3}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(2)}_{a}=\frac{2}{3}Q_{a}.

(C7)

Alternativelly, by using temperature
T_{a}=p_{a}/n_{a}
yields the following equation

\frac{3}{2}n_{a}\frac{d_{a}T_{a}}{dt}+p_{a}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}:(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})=\frac{1}{2}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(2)}_{a}=Q_{a},

(C8)

which identifies with equation (2.3) of
Braginskii 1965
. The collisional energy exchange rates

Q_{a}=\frac{m_{a}}{2}\int|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2}C(f_{a})d^{3}v.

(C9)

C.3
Evolution equation for viscosity tensor
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}

Evolution equation for the usual viscosity-tensor is obtained
by subtracting
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}
times (
C7
) from (
A6
), yielding

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}}{dt}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}\big)^{S}+\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\big)^{S}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})+\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}

\displaystyle\qquad+p_{a}\Big[(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})^{S}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\Big]=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(2)}_{a}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(2)}_{a}.

(C10)

It is possible to define the well-known rate-of-strain tensor

\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}=(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})^{S}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}.

(C11)

Equation (
C10
) is exact.
By using heat flux decomposition (
C4
) yields
\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}=(2/5)((\nabla\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a})^{S}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a})
, and so
equation (
C10
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}}{dt}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}\big)^{S}+\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\big)^{S}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})+\frac{2}{5}\Big[(\nabla\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a})^{S}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad+p_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(2)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}\equiv\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(2)}_{a}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(2)}_{a},

(C12)

which for example identifies with equations (39)-(40) of
Schunk 1977
.
It is possible to define

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}^{q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{5}\Big[(\nabla\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a})^{S}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\Big],

(C13)

where we used a heat flux superscript ’
q
’ to differentiate it from (
C11
).
As a double check, applying trace on (
C12
) yields that both sides are zero.

C.4
Evolution equation for heat flux vector
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}

Evolution equation for
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}
is obtained by applying
(1/2)\textrm{Tr}
on (
A7
),
yielding

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}}{dt}+\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\frac{1}{2}\textrm{Tr}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}\Big[\frac{3}{2}p_{a}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}+(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{2}\textrm{Tr}\Big[\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(3)}_{a}-\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}(\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}})^{S}\Big]-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a},

(C14)

where
\textrm{Tr}(\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}})^{S}=5\boldsymbol{R}_{a}
. This equation is exact.
By using heat flux decomposition (
C4
) yields

\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}=(2/5)\big[\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\big],

(C15)

and applying trace at decomposition (
C5
) yields

\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}=5\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}+\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a},

(C16)

which is of course equivalent to decomposition (
C2
), (
C3
).
Note that a closure
\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}=5\frac{p^{2}_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}
can be viewed as an isotropic analogy of the anisotropic bi-Maxwellian “normal” closure

r_{\parallel\parallel a}=\frac{3p_{\parallel a}^{2}}{\rho}
,
r_{\parallel\perp a}=\frac{p_{\parallel a}p_{\perp a}}{\rho_{a}}
,

r_{\perp\perp a}=\frac{2p_{\perp a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}}
with
p_{\parallel a}=p_{\perp a}=p_{a}
,
because the following general identity holds for any gyrotropic distribution function

\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}^{\textrm{g}}_{a}=r_{\parallel\parallel a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}+r_{\parallel\perp a}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}+\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}})+2r_{\perp\perp a}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}-\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}})
.
Then one calculates

\frac{1}{2}\textrm{Tr}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}=\frac{5}{2}\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)+\frac{1}{6}\nabla\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a},

(C17)

together with

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\textrm{Tr}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}\Big[\frac{3}{2}p_{a}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}+(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{5}{2}p_{a}\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)+\frac{1}{6}\nabla\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}

(C18)

\displaystyle-\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a},

and evolution equation (
C14
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}}{dt}+\frac{7}{5}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\frac{7}{5}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\frac{2}{5}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\frac{5}{2}p_{a}\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{6}\nabla\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}-\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}

\displaystyle\qquad=\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(3)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(3)}_{a}-\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}.

(C19)

As a double-check, reducing the 22-moment model into 13-moment model with closures
\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a}=0
and
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}=7(p_{a}/\rho_{a})\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}
,
so that

\frac{1}{2}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}-\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}\overset{\textrm{13-m}}{\to}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}+\frac{7}{2}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}\cdot\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big),

(C20)

then evolution equation (
C19
) recovers equations (39)-(40) of
Schunk 1977
.

C.5
Evolution equation for viscosity-tensor
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}

Nonlinear evolution equation for the 4th-order moment
r_{ijkl}^{a}=X^{a(4)}_{ijkl}
is given by (
A8
).
First, we need to obtain evolution equation for matrix

(\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}^{a})_{ij}=X^{a(4)}_{ij}
, which is further decomposed into (
C2
) & (
C3
).
Applying trace at (
A8
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}+\nabla\cdot\big(\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(5)}_{a}\big)+(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}+2\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}

\displaystyle+\Big[(\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a})\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times(\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a})-\frac{2}{\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\Big]^{S}-\frac{2}{\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(4)}_{a}-\frac{2}{\rho_{a}}\Big[\big(\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\big)^{S}+\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}\Big].

(C21)

As a quick double-check, equation (
C21
) appears equivalent to equation (3.4.35), page 154 of
Balescu 1988
(after accounting for different
normalization constants of
1/2
and adding a missing “s” index to his 4th-order moment
S_{rsnm}
).
Applying another trace at (
C21
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}X^{(4)}_{a}+\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}+(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a})X^{(4)}_{a}+4(\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}):\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}-\frac{8}{\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(4)}_{a}-\frac{8}{\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}.

(C22)

To obtain evolution equation for matrix
\Pi^{a(4)}_{ij}
, we need to subtract
(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}/3)
times (
C22
) from (
C21
).
For example, we need to calculate

\displaystyle X_{ijk}^{a(5)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{5}\Big(X_{i}^{a(5)}\delta_{jk}+X_{j}^{a(5)}\delta_{ik}+X_{k}^{a(5)}\delta_{ij}\Big);

\displaystyle\partial_{k}X_{kij}^{a(5)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{5}\Big(\partial_{j}X_{i}^{a(5)}+\partial_{i}X_{j}^{a(5)}+\delta_{ij}\partial_{k}X_{k}^{a(5)}\Big);

\displaystyle(\partial_{k}X_{kij}^{a(5)})-\frac{\delta_{ij}}{3}\partial_{k}X_{k}^{a(5)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{5}\Big(\partial_{j}X_{i}^{a(5)}+\partial_{i}X_{j}^{a(5)}-\frac{2}{3}\delta_{ij}\partial_{k}X_{k}^{a(5)}\Big),

(C23)

together with

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{15}X^{(4)}_{a}\Big((\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})^{S}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\Big)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{7}\Big[\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a})+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})+\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\big)^{S}+\big((\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}\big)^{S}\Big];

\displaystyle\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}X^{(4)}_{a}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a})+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a};

\displaystyle 2\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}4\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{15}X^{(4)}_{a}\Big((\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})^{S}-\frac{7}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\Big)-\frac{22}{21}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})

(C24)

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{7}\Big[\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a})+\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\big)^{S}+\big((\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}\big)^{S}\Big],

and useful identities are

\displaystyle\big[(\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a})\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\big]^{S}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}X^{(4)}_{a}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})^{S}+\big[\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\big]^{S};

\displaystyle\big[\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times(\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{a})\big]^{S}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big[\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}\big]^{S}.

(C25)

The heat flux contributions calculate

(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}=\frac{2}{5}\Big[\big((\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\big)^{S}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\Big],

so the heat fluxes are added as

\displaystyle-\,2\big((\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\big)^{S}-2(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\frac{8}{3}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}

\displaystyle=-\,\frac{14}{5}\Big[\big((\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\big)^{S}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\Big].

(C26)

The fully nonlinear evolution equation for matrix
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}
thus reads

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}+\frac{1}{5}\Big[(\nabla\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a})^{S}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a})\Big]+\frac{9}{7}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}+\frac{9}{7}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})^{S}

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{7}\big((\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}\big)^{S}-\frac{22}{21}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})-\,\frac{14}{5\rho_{a}}\Big[\big((\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\big)^{S}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\Big]

\displaystyle+\Omega_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}\big)^{S}+\frac{7}{15}\big(15\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}}+\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a}\big)\Big[(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})^{S}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\Big]

\displaystyle=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(4)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}\equiv\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(4)}_{a}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\textrm{Tr}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(4)}_{a}-\frac{14}{5\rho_{a}}\Big[(\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a})^{S}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a})\Big].

(C27)

At the semi-linear level (while keeping the
d/dt
) evolution equation (
C27
) simplifies into

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}+\frac{1}{5}\Big[(\nabla\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a})^{S}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a})\Big]+\Omega_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}\big)^{S}

\displaystyle+7\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}}\Big[(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})^{S}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\Big]=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(4)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}=\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(4)}_{a}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\textrm{Tr}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(4)}_{a}.

(C28)

Finally, neglecting the coupling between heat fluxes and viscosities (which is the choice of Braginskii),
the simplest evolution equation reads

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}\big)^{S}+7\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(4)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime},

(C29)

where
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}=(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})^{S}-(2/3)\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a})
is the usual rate-of-strain tensor.

C.6
Evolution equation for perturbation
\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a}

Fully non-linear evolution equation (
C22
) for
X^{(4)}_{a}
reads

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}X^{(4)}_{a}+\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}+\frac{7}{3}X^{(4)}_{a}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a})+4\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}-\frac{8}{\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(4)}_{a}-\frac{8}{\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}.

(C30)

Then by using
X^{(4)}_{a}=15(p_{a}^{2}/\rho_{a})+\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a}
with

\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\Big(\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)=\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big[-\frac{7}{3}p_{a}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}-\frac{4}{3}\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}-\frac{4}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\frac{4}{3}Q_{a}\Big],

(C31)

one obtains fully non-linear evolution equation for
\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a}

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a}+\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}-20\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\frac{7}{3}\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a})+4\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}-5\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}\big):\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}

\displaystyle-\frac{8}{\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}=\widetilde{Q}^{(4)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}\equiv\textrm{Tr}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(4)}_{a}-20\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}Q_{a}-\frac{8}{\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a},

(C32)

and at the semi-linear level

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a}+\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}-20\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}=\widetilde{Q}^{(4)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}=\textrm{Tr}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(4)}_{a}-20\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}Q_{a}.

(C33)

Collisional contributions can be found in Section
VII.1
; see equation (
142
).

C.7
Evolution equation for heat flux vector
\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}

Applying trace twice at (
A9
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\textrm{Tr}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{a(5)}+\nabla\cdot\big(\textrm{Tr}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{a(6)}\big)+\nabla\cdot\big(\boldsymbol{u}^{a}\textrm{Tr}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{a(5)}\big)+\big(\textrm{Tr}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{a(5)}\cdot\nabla\big)\boldsymbol{u}^{a}

\displaystyle+4\big(\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{a(5)}\big):\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{a}+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\big(\textrm{Tr}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{a(5)}\big)-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}\Big[\big(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}^{a}\big)\textrm{Tr}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{a(4)}+4\big(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}^{a}\big)\cdot\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{a(4)}\Big]

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{a(5)}-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}\Big[\boldsymbol{R}^{a}\textrm{Tr}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{a(4)}+4\boldsymbol{R}^{a}\cdot\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{a(4)}\Big].

(C34)

By using definition of vectors
\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}=\textrm{Tr}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(5)}
,
\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(5)}=\textrm{Tr}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(5)}
and

\displaystyle X^{(5)}_{ijk}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{5}\Big[X^{(5)}_{i}\delta_{jk}+X^{(5)}_{j}\delta_{ik}+X^{(5)}_{k}\delta_{ij}\Big];

\displaystyle X^{(5)}_{ijk}\partial_{j}u_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{5}\Big[X^{(5)}_{i}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+X^{(5)}_{j}\partial_{j}u_{i}^{a}+X^{(5)}_{k}\partial_{i}u_{k}^{a}\Big],

(C35)

together with decompositions (
C2
) & (
C3
),
the fully non-linear evolution equation becomes

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}+\frac{1}{3}\nabla\widetilde{X}^{(6)}_{a}+\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(6)}_{a}

\displaystyle+\frac{9}{5}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a})+\frac{9}{5}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\frac{4}{5}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}

\displaystyle+70\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}}\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)-35\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}^{2}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}-\frac{7}{3\rho_{a}}\big(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}^{a}\big)\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a}-\frac{4}{\rho_{a}}\big(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}^{a}\big)\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}

\displaystyle=\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(5)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}\equiv\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(5)}_{a}-35\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}^{2}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}-\frac{7}{3\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a}-\frac{4}{\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}.

(C36)

Because we do not go higher in the hierarchy, the model is closed with closures (see equations (
B130
) & (
B131
)
or Section
VIII.6
with Tables
VIII.3
&
VIII.4
)

\widetilde{X}^{(6)}_{a}=21\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a};\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(6)}_{a}=18\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}-63\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}^{2}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}.

(C37)

At a semi-linear level equation (
C36
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}+7\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\nabla\widetilde{X}^{(4)}_{a}+18\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(4)}_{a}-98\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}^{2}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}

\displaystyle+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(5)}_{a}+70\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}}\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)=\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(5)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}=\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(5)}_{a}-35\frac{p_{a}^{2}}{\rho_{a}^{2}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}.

(C38)

Appendix D
Simplified general fluid hierarchy

Previously, we introduced a full fluid hierarchy in Section
A
, which contains n-dimensional moments
X^{(n)}_{ijk\ldots n}
.
By applying contractions at these moments in Appendix
C
, we have derived evolution equations for the 22-moment model.
Instead of doing that, it is of course possible to obtain evolution equations for the contracted moments directly from the
Boltzmann equation. This simplified hierarchy is formulated with heat fluxes (vectors) and stress-tensors (matrices)

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}=m_{a}\int\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2n}f_{a}d^{3}v;\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2n)}_{a}=m_{a}\int\big(\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2}\big)|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2n-2}f_{a}d^{3}v,

(D1)

together with fully contracted scalars which are decomposed into a Maxwellian core and perturbation (notation with tilde)

X^{(2n)}_{a}=m_{a}\int|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2n}f_{a}d^{3}v=(2n+1)!!\frac{p^{n}_{a}}{\rho^{n-1}_{a}}+\widetilde{X}^{(2n)}_{a},

(D2)

meaning a definition
\widetilde{X}^{(2n)}_{a}=m_{a}\int|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2n}(f_{a}-f_{a}^{(0)})d^{3}v
, where
f_{a}^{(0)}
is Maxwellian.
In another words, one considers matrices

X^{a(2n)}_{ij}=m_{a}\int|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2n-2}c^{a}_{i}c^{a}_{j}f_{a}d^{3}v=\frac{\delta_{ij}}{3}X^{a(2n)}+\Pi^{a(2n)}_{ij},

(D3)

which are decomposed into fully contracted scalars and stress-tensors.
Note that
\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(1)}_{a}=0
and
\widetilde{X}^{(2)}_{a}=0
.

Unfortunatelly, the traditional definition of the heat flux vector
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}=(1/2)\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}
which contains a factor of
1/2
,
goes against the general ideology that no additional factors are introduced by contractions. Also, we have previously reserved
vector
\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(3)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}
for the right hand side of the heat flux
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}
evolution equation, and not for
\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(3)}_{a}
.
Obviously, our previous notation is not ideal for generalization to an n-th order moments. To circumvent all the problems with
the previous definitions, we define new collisional contributions for heat fluxes and stress-tensors
with
\mathcal{Q}
(mathcal of Q), as vectors and matrices

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{a(2n+1)}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{a}\int|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2n}c^{a}_{i}C(f_{a})d^{3}v;

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{a(2n)}_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{a}\int|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2n-2}c^{a}_{i}c^{a}_{j}C(f_{a})d^{3}v;

(D4)

together with fully contracted

\displaystyle Q^{(2n)}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{a}\int|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2n}C(f_{a})d^{3}v;\qquad Q_{a}=\frac{m_{a}}{2}\int|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2}C(f_{a})d^{3}v.

(D5)

The energy exchange rates
Q_{a}
contain the traditional factor of
1/2
, and
Q^{(2)}_{a}=2Q_{a}
. The momentum
exchange rates
\boldsymbol{R}_{a}=m_{a}\int\boldsymbol{v}C(f_{a})d^{3}v
. In the vector notation matrix

\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}}}^{a(2n)}=\textrm{Tr}\textrm{Tr}\ldots\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{a(2n)}
.

Then, direct integration of the Boltzmann equation and subtraction of momentum equations yields evolution equations for scalars

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}X^{a(2n)}+\partial_{k}(u^{a}_{k}X^{a(2n)})+\partial_{k}X^{a(2n+1)}_{k}+(2n)X^{a(2n)}_{ik}\partial_{k}u^{a}_{i}

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{(2n)}{\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}^{a})_{k}X^{a(2n-1)}_{k}=Q^{a(2n)}-\frac{(2n)}{\rho_{a}}R^{a}_{k}X^{a(2n-1)}_{k},

(D6)

where
(n)
without species index should not be confused with the number density,
evolution equations for vectors

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}X^{a(2n+1)}_{i}+\partial_{k}(u_{k}^{a}X^{a(2n+1)}_{i})+\partial_{k}X^{a(2n+2)}_{ki}+X^{a(2n+1)}_{k}\partial_{k}u_{i}^{a}+(2n)X^{a(2n+1)}_{ijk}\partial_{k}u_{j}^{a}

\displaystyle-\frac{(2n)}{\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}^{a})_{k}X^{a(2n)}_{ki}-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}^{a})_{i}X^{a(2n)}+\Omega_{a}(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{a(2n+1)})_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}^{a(2n+1)}_{i}-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}R^{a}_{i}X^{a(2n)}-\frac{(2n)}{\rho_{a}}R^{a}_{k}X^{a(2n)}_{ki},

(D7)

and matrices

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}X^{a(2n)}_{ij}+\partial_{k}(u_{k}^{a}X^{a(2n)}_{ij})+\partial_{k}X^{a(2n+1)}_{kij}+(2n-2)X^{a(2n)}_{ijkl}(\partial_{k}u_{l}^{a})

\displaystyle+\Big[X^{a(2n)}_{ik}\partial_{k}u_{j}^{a}+\Omega_{a}(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{a(2n)})_{ij}-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}^{a})_{i}X^{a(2n-1)}_{j}\Big]^{S}-\frac{(2n-2)}{\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}^{a})_{k}X^{a(2n-1)}_{kij}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}^{a(2n)}_{ij}-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}\Big[R_{i}^{a}X^{a(2n-1)}_{j}\Big]^{S}-\frac{(2n-2)}{\rho_{a}}R_{k}^{a}X^{a(2n-1)}_{kij},

(D8)

which are valid for
n\geq 1
. For example evaluating (
D6
) for
n=1
yield evolution equation for scalar pressure
p_{a}
.
Applying trace at (
D8
) recovers (
D6
).

Matrices
X_{ij}^{a(2n)}
are then decomposed according to (
D3
) where stress-tensors
\Pi^{a(2n)}_{ij}
are traceless,
and higher-order tensors are decomposed according to (where tensors
\sigma
are neglected, which is the core of the hierarchy simplification)

\displaystyle X^{a(2n+1)}_{ijk}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{5}\Big[X^{a(2n+1)}_{i}\delta_{jk}+X^{a(2n+1)}_{j}\delta_{ik}+X^{a(2n+1)}_{k}\delta_{ij}\Big];

(D9)

\displaystyle X_{ijkl}^{a(2n)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{15}X^{a(2n)}\big(\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}\big)

(D10)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{7}\Big[\Pi_{ij}^{a(2n)}\delta_{kl}+\Pi_{ik}^{a(2n)}\delta_{jl}+\Pi_{il}^{a(2n)}\delta_{jk}+\Pi_{jk}^{a(2n)}\delta_{il}+\Pi_{jl}^{a(2n)}\delta_{ik}+\Pi_{kl}^{a(2n)}\delta_{ij}\Big].

Applying trace at (
D9
) yields identity, and applying trace at (
D10
) yields
decomposition (
D3
). Evolution equations for fully contracted moments (scalars) then become

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}X^{(2n)}_{a}+\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}+\frac{(2n+3)}{3}X^{(2n)}_{a}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+(2n)\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2n)}_{a}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{(2n)}{\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n-1)}_{a}=Q^{(2n)}_{a}-\frac{(2n)}{\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n-1)}_{a},

(D11)

for heat fluxes (vectors)

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}+\frac{(2n+5)}{5}\Big[\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\Big]+\frac{(2n)}{5}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{3}\nabla X^{(2n+2)}_{a}+\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2n+2)}_{a}-\frac{(2n+3)}{3\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})X^{(2n)}_{a}-\frac{(2n)}{\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2n)}_{a}

\displaystyle+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}=\vec{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}-\frac{(2n+3)}{3\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}X^{(2n)}_{a}-\frac{(2n)}{\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2n)}_{a},

(D12)

and for stress-tensors (matrices)

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2n)}_{a}+\frac{1}{5}\Big[\big(\nabla\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}\big)^{S}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}\Big]+\frac{(2n+5)}{7}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2n)}_{a}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a})

\displaystyle+\Big[\frac{(2n+5)}{7}\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2n)}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\big)^{S}+\frac{(2n-2)}{7}\big((\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2n)}_{a}\big)^{S}-\frac{2(4n+3)}{21}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2n)}_{a}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\Big]

\displaystyle-\frac{(2n+3)}{5\rho_{a}}\Big[\big((\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n-1)}_{a}\big)^{S}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n-1)}_{a}\Big]

\displaystyle+\Omega_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2n)}_{a}\big)^{S}+\frac{(2n+3)}{15}X^{(2n)}_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}

\displaystyle=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}}}^{(2n)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}\equiv\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}}}^{(2n)}_{a}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}Q^{(2n)}_{a}-\frac{(2n+3)}{5\rho_{a}}\Big[\big(\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n-1)}_{a}\big)^{S}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n-1)}_{a}\Big].

(D13)

By applying trace at equation (
D13
) it can be verified that it is traceless.

The fully contracted scalar variables are then decomposed into a Maxwellian core and perturbation (with tilde) according to (
D2
),
yielding evolution equation for scalars

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\widetilde{X}^{(2n)}_{a}+\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}+\frac{(2n+3)}{3}\widetilde{X}^{(2n)}_{a}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+(2n)\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2n)}_{a}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}

\displaystyle-(2n+1)!!\frac{(2n)}{3}\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)^{n-1}\Big[\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\Big]-\frac{(2n)}{\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n-1)}_{a}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{Q}^{(2n)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}\equiv Q^{(2n)}_{a}-(2n+1)!!\frac{(2n)}{3}\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)^{n-1}Q_{a}-\frac{(2n)}{\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n-1)}_{a},

(D14)

and heat fluxes

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{dt}\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}+\frac{(2n+5)}{5}\Big[\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\Big]+\frac{(2n)}{5}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{3}\nabla\widetilde{X}^{(2n+2)}_{a}+\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2n+2)}_{a}-\frac{(2n+3)}{3\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\widetilde{X}^{(2n)}_{a}-\frac{(2n)}{\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2n)}_{a}

\displaystyle+(2n+3)!!\frac{(n)}{3}\frac{p_{a}^{n}}{\rho_{a}^{n-1}}\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)-\frac{(2n+3)!!}{3}\frac{p_{a}^{n}}{\rho_{a}^{n}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}

\displaystyle=\vec{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}\equiv\vec{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}}^{(2n+1)}_{a}-\frac{(2n+3)}{3\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\widetilde{X}^{(2n)}_{a}-\frac{(2n+3)!!}{3}\frac{p_{a}^{n}}{\rho_{a}^{n}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}-\frac{(2n)}{\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2n)}_{a}.

(D15)

Evolution equation for stress-tensors (
D13
) contains only one trivial term with
X^{(2n)}_{a}
, where

\frac{(2n+3)}{15}X^{(2n)}_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}=\frac{(2n+3)!!}{15}\frac{p^{n}_{a}}{\rho_{a}^{n-1}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}+\frac{(2n+3)}{15}\widetilde{X}^{(2n)}_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a},

and we do not re-write the full equation. Equations (
D13
)-(
D15
) are valid for
n\geq 1
, where
for
n=1
(
D14
) reduces to zero, so this equation is meaningfull only for
n\geq 2
. In the semi-linear approximation,
the hierarchy simplifies into (
189
)-(
191
).

Appendix E
BGK collisional operator

Before calculations with the Landau collisional operator, it is beneficial to first get familiar with the
heuristic relaxation-type operator known as BGK, after Bhatnagar-Gross-Krook
(
Bhatnagar et al. 1954
;
Gross & Krook 1956
)
, written in the following form

C(f_{a})=\sum_{b}C_{ab}(f_{a})=-\sum_{b}\nu_{ab}(f_{a}-f^{(0)}_{ab}).

(E1)

The Maxwellian
f^{(0)}_{ab}
has two indices and is defined as

f_{ab}^{(0)}=n_{a}\Big(\frac{m_{a}}{2\pi T_{a}}\Big)^{3/2}\exp\Big(-\frac{m_{a}|\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{u}_{b}|^{2}}{2T_{a}}\Big).

(E2)

Note that only velocity
\boldsymbol{u}_{b}
has index “b” and that temperature, mass and density has index “a”. To account for different temperatures
is possible by considering generalized BGK operators of
Haack et al. 2017
.
The simple BGK operator yields momentum and energy exchange rates

\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}=\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}(\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a});\qquad Q_{ab}=\frac{1}{2}\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}|\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|^{2},

(E3)

where both the momentum and energy are conserved (note that for heuristic operators it is advisable to directly calculate
both
\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}
and
\boldsymbol{R}_{ba}
together with
Q_{ab}
and
Q_{ba}
to verify that they are well defined).
This BGK operator also satisfies the Boltzmann H-theorem, which for multi-species plasmas has a general form

\int C_{ab}(f_{a})\ln f_{a}d^{3}v+\int C_{ba}(f_{b})\ln f_{b}d^{3}v\leq 0,

(E4)

where the equality is true only if
f_{a}
and
f_{b}
are Maxwellians.
For the BGK operator, each part of the H-theorem (
E4
) is satisfied independently.
It can be shown that
\int(f_{a}-f_{ab}^{(0)})\ln f_{ab}^{(0)}d^{3}v=0
, and subtracting this integral from the first term
of (
E4
) yields

\displaystyle\int C_{ab}(f_{a})\ln f_{a}d^{3}v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nu_{ab}\int(f_{ab}^{(0)}-f_{a})\ln f_{a}d^{3}v-\underbrace{\nu_{ab}\int(f_{ab}^{(0)}-f_{a})\ln f_{ab}^{(0)}d^{3}v}_{0}

(E5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nu_{ab}\int(f_{ab}^{(0)}-f_{a})\ln\Big(\frac{f_{a}}{f_{ab}^{(0)}}\Big)d^{3}v\leq 0,

where in the last step one uses that for any real numbers
a>0
and
b>0
the following identity holds

(a-b)\ln(b/a)\leq 0
(the identity is easily verified, because for
a>b
the first term is positive and the logarithm is negative, and
for
a<b
the first term is negative and the logarithm is positive; the identity is equal to zero only if
a=b
).

The BGK collisional contributions calculate

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(2)}_{ab}=m_{a}\int\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}C_{ab}(f_{a})d^{3}v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\nu_{ab}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}+\nu_{ab}\rho_{a}\delta\boldsymbol{u}\delta\boldsymbol{u};

(E6)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(3)}_{ab}=m_{a}\int\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}C_{ab}(f_{a})d^{3}v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\nu_{ab}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{a}+\nu_{ab}p_{a}\big[\delta\boldsymbol{u}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\big]^{S}+\nu_{ab}\rho_{a}\delta\boldsymbol{u}\delta\boldsymbol{u}\delta\boldsymbol{u},

(E7)

where
\delta\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}
.

E.1
Viscosity-tensor
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}

Collisional contributions that enter the r.h.s. of evolution equation (
C12
) are

\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(2)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}\equiv\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(2)}_{a}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(2)}_{a}=-\bar{\nu}_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}-\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}^{\textrm{frict}},

(E8)

where we defined

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{b}\nu_{ab};

(E9)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}^{\textrm{frict}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\rho_{a}\sum_{b}\nu_{ab}\big(\delta\boldsymbol{u}\delta\boldsymbol{u}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}|\delta\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\big),

(E10)

and where superscript ’frict’ means frictional contributions due to
\delta\boldsymbol{u}
. The frictional contributions are only non-linear,
but we keep them to show that it is possible to take them into account. Using quasi-static approximation,
evolution equation (
C12
) can be simplified into

(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a})^{S}+\frac{\bar{\nu}_{a}}{\Omega_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}=-\frac{1}{\Omega_{a}}\big(p_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}^{q}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}^{\textrm{frict}}\big),

(E11)

where matrices
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}
and
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}^{q}
are given by (
C11
), (
C13
).
Equation (
E11
) can be directly solved. Nevertheless, the stress-tensor of Braginskii does not contain
heat flux contributions, or frictional contributions. To understand the solution of Braginskii more clearly, let us first solve the above equation
only with the matrix
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}
.

The simplest quasi-static
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}
is thus obtained by solving

(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a})^{S}+\frac{\bar{\nu}_{a}}{\Omega_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}=-\frac{p_{a}}{\Omega_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}.

(E12)

For any traceless and symmetric matrix
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}
, solution of (
E12
) reads (see details in Section
E.4
)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\eta_{0}^{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}-\eta_{1}^{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1}-\eta_{2}^{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{2}+\eta_{3}^{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{3}+\eta_{4}^{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{4};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\big)\Big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\Big);

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}+\frac{1}{2}\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\big)\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\big)^{S};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\big)^{S};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\big)^{S},

(E13)

with BGK viscosity coefficients

\displaystyle\eta_{0}^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p_{a}}{\bar{\nu}_{a}};\quad\eta_{1}^{a}=\frac{p_{a}\bar{\nu}_{a}}{4\Omega_{a}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{a}^{2}};\quad\eta_{2}^{a}=\frac{p_{a}\bar{\nu}_{a}}{\Omega_{a}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{a}^{2}};\quad\eta_{3}^{a}=\frac{2p_{a}\Omega_{a}}{4\Omega_{a}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{a}^{2}};\quad\eta_{4}^{a}=\frac{p_{a}\Omega_{a}}{\Omega_{a}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{a}^{2}}.

(E14)

Coefficient
\eta_{0}
is called the parallel viscosity,
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
perpendicular viscosities, and

\eta_{3},\eta_{4}
gyroviscosities. Importantly, the BGK solution (
E13
) is identical
to the form of
Braginskii 1965
viscosity-tensor, his equations (4.41)-(4.42), only his viscosities are different.
A comparison is presented in the next section. All four matrices
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0},\ldots\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{4}
are traceless and
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{2}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}
.

When magnetic field is zero, so
\Omega_{a}=0
and
\eta_{0}^{a}=\eta_{1}^{a}=\eta_{2}^{a}
,
the stress tensor (
E13
) simplifies into
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}=-\eta_{0}^{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}

and contributes to the momentum equations in a familiar form

\boldsymbol{B}=0:\qquad\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}=-\nabla\cdot(\eta_{0}^{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a})=-\eta_{0}^{a}\Big(\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\frac{1}{3}\nabla(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\Big)-(\nabla\eta_{0}^{a})\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a},

(E15)

analogously to the viscosity of Navier-Stokes equations (the last term can be neglected if
\eta_{0}^{a}
is spatially independent).

If the mean magnetic field is sufficiently strong so that its curvature can be neglected, (
E13
)
can be evaluated with respect to
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}_{0}=(0,0,1)
, yielding

\displaystyle\Pi_{xx}^{(2)a}=-\frac{\eta_{0}^{a}}{2}(W_{xx}^{a}+W_{yy}^{a})-\frac{\eta_{1}^{a}}{2}(W_{xx}^{a}-W_{yy}^{a})-\eta_{3}^{a}W_{xy}^{a};

\displaystyle\Pi_{xy}^{(2)a}=\frac{\eta_{3}^{a}}{2}(W_{xx}^{a}-W_{yy}^{a})-\eta_{1}^{a}W_{xy}^{a};

\displaystyle\Pi_{xz}^{(2)a}=-\eta_{4}^{a}W_{yz}^{a}-\eta_{2}^{a}W_{xz}^{a};

\displaystyle\Pi_{yy}^{(2)a}=-\frac{\eta_{0}^{a}}{2}(W_{xx}^{a}+W_{yy}^{a})+\frac{\eta_{1}^{a}}{2}(W_{xx}^{a}-W_{yy}^{a})+\eta_{3}^{a}W_{xy}^{a};

\displaystyle\Pi_{yz}^{(2)a}=\eta_{4}^{a}W_{xz}^{a}-\eta_{2}^{a}W_{yz}^{a};

\displaystyle\Pi_{zz}^{(2)a}=-\eta_{0}^{a}W_{zz}^{a},

(E16)

which is equation (2.21) of
Braginskii 1965
.
As a double check, adding
\Pi_{xx}^{(2)a}+\Pi_{yy}^{(2)a}+\Pi_{zz}^{(2)a}=-\eta_{0}^{a}(W_{xx}^{a}+W_{yy}^{a}+W_{zz}^{a})=0
, so the stress tensor is
indeed traceless (even though all the diagonal components are non-zero). For strong magnetic field
\Omega_{a}\gg\bar{\nu}_{a}
viscosities
(
E14
) simplify into

\eta_{0}^{a}=\frac{p_{a}}{\bar{\nu}_{a}};\quad\eta_{1}^{a}=\frac{1}{4}\frac{p_{a}\bar{\nu}_{a}}{\Omega_{a}^{2}};\quad\eta_{2}^{a}=4\eta_{1}^{a};\quad\eta_{3}^{a}=\frac{p_{a}}{2\Omega_{a}};\quad\eta_{4}^{a}=2\eta_{3}^{a}.

(E17)

Considering only self-collisions, the BGK viscosity coefficients (
E14
) were first recovered
by
Kaufman 1960
, even though he does not write them explicitly, and one needs
to get them from rearranging his equations (12)-(15) into form (
E16
). The same results for
\eta_{0}-\eta_{3}
can also be found for example
in
Helander & Sigmar 2002
(p. 86); see also
Zank 2014
(p. 164), however,
\eta_{4}
coefficient is erroneously related to
\eta_{3}=2\eta_{4}
,
which is a valid relation only in the limit when
x=\Omega_{a}/\bar{\nu}_{a}
is small (i.e. a weak magnetic field).
Correct relations are
\eta_{3}^{a}(x)=\eta_{4}^{a}(2x)
and
\eta_{1}^{a}(x)=\eta_{2}^{a}(2x)
, valid for both the BGK and Braginskii solutions.

Now one can consider more general (
E11
), with heat flux contributions
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}^{q}
and
frictional contributions
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}^{\textrm{frict}}
. Solution of (
E11
) is analogous to (
E13
)
because all matrices on the r.h.s. are traceless and symmetric. However, it is useful to
rewrite the solution into a different form by defining new matrix

\widetilde{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}}_{a}=(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})^{S}+\frac{2}{5p_{a}}(\nabla\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a})^{S},

(E18)

and the stress-tensor then reads

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\eta_{0}^{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}-\eta_{1}^{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1}-\eta_{2}^{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{2}+\eta_{3}^{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{3}+\eta_{4}^{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{4};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[\frac{3}{2}\big(\widetilde{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}}_{a}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\big)-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}-\frac{2}{5p_{a}}\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\Big]\Big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\Big)

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{2}\frac{\rho_{a}}{p_{a}}\Big[\sum_{b}\nu_{ab}\big(\delta u_{\parallel}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}|\delta\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\big)\Big]\Big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\Big);

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\cdot\widetilde{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}+\Big[\frac{1}{2}\big(\widetilde{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}}_{a}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\big)-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}-\frac{2}{5p_{a}}\nabla\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\Big]\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}

\displaystyle-\frac{\rho_{a}}{p_{a}}\sum_{b}\nu_{ab}\big(\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\perp}\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\perp}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}}{2}|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\perp}|^{2}\big);

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\cdot\widetilde{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}}_{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\big)^{S}-\frac{\rho_{a}}{p_{a}}\sum_{b}\nu_{ab}\big[\delta u_{\parallel}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\perp}\big]^{S};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\widetilde{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}}_{a}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\big)^{S}-\frac{\rho_{a}}{2p_{a}}\sum_{b}\nu_{ab}\big[(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\delta\boldsymbol{u})\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\perp}\big]^{S};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\widetilde{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}}_{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\big)^{S}-\frac{\rho_{a}}{p_{a}}\sum_{b}\nu_{ab}\big[(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\delta\boldsymbol{u})\delta u_{\parallel}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\big]^{S},

(E19)

with viscosities (
E14
). Prescribing
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}=0
and
\delta\boldsymbol{u}=0
of course recovers (
E13
).

E.2
Heat flux vector
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}

We consider the 13-moment model where evolution equation (
C19
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}}{dt}+\frac{7}{5}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\frac{7}{5}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\frac{2}{5}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\frac{5}{2}p_{a}\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)

\displaystyle+\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}+\frac{7}{2}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}\cdot\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a},

\displaystyle\qquad=\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(3)}_{a}\,{}^{\prime}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(3)}_{a}-\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a},

(E20)

and the BGK collisional contributions calculate

\frac{1}{2}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{(3)}_{ab}-\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}=-\nu_{ab}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\frac{\nu_{ab}}{2}\rho_{a}\delta\boldsymbol{u}|\delta\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}.

(E21)

In a quasi-static approximation (
E20
) can be simplified into

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\frac{\bar{\nu}_{a}}{\Omega_{a}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\,\frac{1}{\Omega_{a}}\Big[\frac{5}{2}p_{a}\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)+\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}+\frac{7}{2}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}\cdot\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a})\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}

(E22)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}-\sum_{b}\frac{\nu_{ab}}{2}\rho_{a}\delta\boldsymbol{u}|\delta\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\Big].

A general vector equation (where
\vec{\boldsymbol{a}}
is an unspecified vector, unrelated to the species index)

\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\frac{\bar{\nu}_{a}}{\Omega_{a}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}=-\frac{\vec{\boldsymbol{a}}}{\Omega_{a}},

(E23)

has the following exact solution
(split the equation to parallel and perpendicular parts

\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}=\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{\parallel a}+\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{\perp a}
&
\vec{\boldsymbol{a}}=\vec{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\parallel}+\vec{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\perp}

with
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{\parallel a}=0
; apply
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times
on the perpendicular part, use
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{\perp_{a}})=-\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{\perp a}
,
and solve the two coupled perpendicular equations by eliminating the
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{\perp a}
)

\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}=-\frac{1}{\bar{\nu}_{a}}(\vec{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\hat{b}})\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}+\frac{\Omega_{a}}{\Omega_{a}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{a}^{2}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{a}}-\frac{\bar{\nu}_{a}}{\Omega_{a}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{a}^{2}}\vec{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\perp}.

(E24)

Note that
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{a}}=\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\perp}
.
Result (
E24
) represents solution of equation (
E22
). For zero magnetic field
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}=-\vec{\boldsymbol{a}}/\bar{\nu}_{a}
.
The BGK frictional contributions due to
\delta\boldsymbol{u}
are only non-linear, in contrast,
the electron heat flux of Braginskii contains frictional
\delta\boldsymbol{u}
contributions that are linear. At the semi-linear level,
(
E22
) simplifies into

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\frac{\bar{\nu}_{a}}{\Omega_{a}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\,\frac{1}{\Omega_{a}}\Big[\frac{5}{2}p_{a}\nabla\Big(\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\Big)+\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}\Big],

(E25)

with a solution again given by (
E24
). The BGK operator can therefore account for linear (!)
contributions of the stress-tensor
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}
that enters the heat flux
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}
, similarly to the previous
result (
E19
) where the heat
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}
flux entered the stress-tensor
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}
.
Such a coupling is typically neglected with the Landau collisional operator.

The simplest BGK heat flux is a solution of equation

\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\frac{\bar{\nu}_{a}}{\Omega_{a}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}=-\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{\Omega_{a}m_{a}}\nabla T_{a},

(E26)

and the solution reads

\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}=-\kappa_{\parallel}^{a}\nabla_{\parallel}T_{a}-\kappa_{\perp}^{a}\nabla_{\perp}T_{a}+\kappa_{\times}^{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\nabla T_{a},

(E27)

with thermal conductivities

\kappa_{\parallel}^{a}=\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{\bar{\nu}_{a}m_{a}};\qquad\kappa_{\perp}^{a}=\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{m_{a}}\frac{\bar{\nu}_{a}}{(\Omega_{a}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{a}^{2})};\qquad\kappa_{\times}^{a}=\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{m_{a}}\frac{\Omega_{a}}{(\Omega_{a}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{a}^{2})}.\\

(E28)

We use the Braginskii notation with vector
\nabla_{\parallel}=\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\cdot\nabla
.
If magnetic field is zero, so that
\Omega_{a}=0
and
\kappa_{\parallel}^{a}=\kappa_{\perp}^{a}
,
the solution simplifies into
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}=-\kappa_{\parallel}^{a}\nabla T_{a}
.

E.3
BGK vs Braginskii comparison

Here we compare the BGK viscosities and heat conductivities with those of
Braginskii 1965
for a one ion-electron
plasma with ion charge
Z_{i}=1
. The BGK viscosities (
E14
) contain
\bar{\nu}_{a}=\sum_{b}\nu_{ab}
,
and in general should be added according to

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}_{i}=\nu_{ii}+\nu_{ie}=\nu_{ii}\Big(1+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{Z_{i}}\sqrt{\frac{m_{e}}{m_{i}}}\Big(\frac{T_{i}}{T_{e}}\Big)^{3/2}\Big);

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}_{e}=\nu_{ee}+\nu_{ei}=\nu_{ei}\Big(1+\frac{1}{Z_{i}\sqrt{2}}\Big).

(E29)

However, for the ion species Braginskii neglects ion-electron collisions and thus

\bar{\nu}_{i}=\nu_{ii}
and
\bar{\nu}_{e}=1.707\nu_{ei}
; see Section
VIII.2
. Using Braginskii
notation with one-index
\nu_{i}=\nu_{ii}
and
\nu_{e}=\nu_{ei}
then implies

\bar{\nu}_{a}=\alpha_{a}\nu_{a};\quad\textrm{where}\quad\alpha_{i}=1;\quad\alpha_{e}=1.707,

(E30)

and introducing quantity
x=\Omega_{a}/\nu_{a}
the BGK viscosities (
E14
) become

\displaystyle\eta_{0}^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p_{a}}{\alpha_{a}\nu_{a}};\quad\eta_{1}^{a}=\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{a}}\frac{\alpha_{a}}{4x^{2}+\alpha_{a}^{2}};\quad\eta_{2}^{a}=\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{a}}\frac{\alpha_{a}}{x^{2}+\alpha_{a}^{2}};\quad\eta_{3}^{a}=\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{a}}\frac{2x}{4x^{2}+\alpha_{a}^{2}};\quad\eta_{4}^{a}=\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{a}}\frac{x}{x^{2}+\alpha_{a}^{2}}.

(E31)

Note that
\eta_{1}^{a}(x)=\eta_{2}^{a}(2x)
and
\eta_{3}^{a}(x)=\eta_{4}^{a}(2x)
. Similarly, the BGK heat conductivities (
E28
) become

\displaystyle\kappa_{\parallel}^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{5}{2\alpha_{a}}\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{a}m_{a}};\qquad\kappa_{\perp}^{a}=\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{a}m_{a}}\frac{\alpha_{a}}{(x^{2}+\alpha_{a}^{2})};\qquad\kappa_{\times}^{a}=\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{\nu_{a}m_{a}}\frac{x}{(x^{2}+\alpha_{a}^{2})}.

(E32)

Viscosities and heat conductivities for Braginskii are given in the main text.
Ion viscosities are compared in Figure
3
, electron viscosities in Figure
4
and
heat conductivities in Figure
5
. A small value of
x
represents weak magnetic field and large value of
x
represents
strong magnetic field.

Figure 3:
Ion viscosities of the BGK model (red) and of the Braginskii model (black) normalized as
\hat{\eta}^{i}=\eta^{i}\nu_{ii}/p_{i}

vs. ratio
x=\Omega_{i}/\nu_{ii}
.
Left panel: perpendicular viscosities
\eta_{1}^{i},\eta_{2}^{i}
. Right panel: gyroviscosities
\eta_{3}^{i},\eta_{4}^{i}
.
For large values of
x
, the BGK asymptotic profiles
for
\hat{\eta}^{i}_{3}=1/(2x)
and
\hat{\eta}_{4}^{i}=1/x
become independent
of collisional frequencies and match the asymptotic profiles of Braginskii exactly.
BGK asymptotic profiles for
\hat{\eta}^{i}_{1}=1/(4x^{2})
and
\hat{\eta}^{i}_{2}=1/x^{2}
have correct functional
dependence, but differ from the Braginskii asymptotes by a proportionality constant.
The BGK operator reproduces the ion viscosity of Braginskii with surprisingly good accuracy.

Figure 4:
Electron viscosities normalized as
\hat{\eta}^{e}=\eta^{e}\nu_{ei}/p_{e}
vs. ratio
|x|=|\Omega_{e}|/\nu_{ei}
. Results are less
precise than for ions in Figure
3
, especially for small values of
x
, nevertheless, the same conclusions are obtained.

Figure 5:
Heat conductivities
\kappa^{a}_{\perp}
and
\kappa^{a}_{\times}
.
Left panel: ion species, normalized as
\kappa^{i}m_{i}\nu_{ii}/p_{i}
. Right panel: electron species, normalized as
\kappa^{e}m_{e}\nu_{ei}/p_{e}
.
For large values of
x
, the BGK asymptotic profiles
\kappa^{a}_{\times}
(dashed lines) match the Braginskii results exactly,
whereas for
\kappa_{\perp}^{a}
(solid lines) the results differ by a proportionality constant.

E.4
Nonlinear stress-tensor decomposition

Here we want to consider BGK equation for the stress-tensor (
E12
)

(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}})^{S}+\frac{\nu}{\Omega}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}=-\frac{p}{\Omega}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}},

(E33)

and clarify solution (
E13
). Species indices are dropped and both
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}
and
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}
are symmetric and traceless.
First we need to learn how to decompose any general matrix. It is useful for a moment to consider
undefined matrices
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}
and
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}
which are not necessarily symmetric nor traceless.

E.4.1
Decomposition of a matrix

We will work both in the reference frame of magnetic field lines (
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}_{0}=(0,0,1)
) which nicely guides and clarifies the calculations, and
also in a laboratory reference frame with general
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}
. In the reference frame of magnetic field lines one uses matrices

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right);\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}-\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&0\end{array}\right);\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}^{\times}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&-1&0\\
+1&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{array}\right),

where the last matrix is defined as
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}=(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}^{\times})\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}
. Then one takes a general matrix
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}
, and
starts multiplying it with matrices
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}
and
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}
from the left and right, yielding
a general decomposition

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}\,^{\prime}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1}\,^{\prime}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{2};

(E44)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}\,^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}=(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}})\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1}\,^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}+\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}=(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}})^{S}.

In the reference frame of magnetic field lines

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}\,^{\prime}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&W_{zz}\end{array}\right);\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1}\,^{\prime}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}W_{xx}&W_{xy}&0\\
W_{yx}&W_{yy}&0\\
0&0&0\end{array}\right);\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&W_{xz}\\
0&0&W_{yz}\\
W_{zx}&W_{zy}&0\end{array}\right),

and adding these matrices together obviously yields the full matrix
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}
. However, the decomposition (
E44
) also works in the
laboratory reference frame with general
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}
, as can be verified by adding the general matrices together.
It is possible to consider an alternative decomposition, according to

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{2};

(E55)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}})\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}+\frac{1}{2}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}:\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp})\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}-\frac{1}{2}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}:\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp})\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}})^{S},

where in the reference frame of magnetic field lines

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\frac{1}{2}(W_{xx}+W_{yy})&0&0\\
0&\frac{1}{2}(W_{xx}+W_{yy})&0\\
0&0&W_{zz}\end{array}\right);\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\frac{1}{2}(W_{xx}-W_{yy})&W_{xy}&0\\
W_{yx}&-\frac{1}{2}(W_{xx}-W_{yy})&0\\
0&0&0\end{array}\right).

Decomposition (
E55
) again works for general
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}
, and
in comparison to the previous decomposition
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}\,^{\prime}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1}\,^{\prime}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1}
. The advantage is that if
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}
is traceless then all 3 matrices are
traceless. It is useful to re-arrange
(
E55
) by separating the trace of
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}
with

(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}:\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp})\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}=(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}:\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}})\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}-(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}})\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}
, yielding decomposition

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{2};

(E63)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}})\Big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}:\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}})\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}+\frac{1}{2}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}})\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}-\frac{1}{2}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}:\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}})\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}})^{S}.

The same decomposition is used for the stress-tensor
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{0}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{1}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{2};

(E64)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}})\Big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}:\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}})\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}+\frac{1}{2}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}})\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}-\frac{1}{2}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}:\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}})\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}})^{S}.

Let us solve for
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{0}
. By applying
:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}
and
:\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}
at equation (
E33
) and using identities

(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}})^{S}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}=0;\qquad(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}})^{S}:\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}=0,

(E65)

yields

\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}=-\frac{p}{\nu}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}};\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}:\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}=-\frac{p}{\nu}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}:\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}},

(E66)

and plugging these results into (
E64
) yields the final solution for the parallel stress-tensor

\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{0}=-\frac{p}{\nu}\Big[\frac{3}{2}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}})\Big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}:\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}})\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\Big]=-\frac{p}{\nu}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}.

(E67)

The solution is valid for any general matrix
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}
(not necessarily symmetric or traceless).
If this result is compared with the expression (4.42) of
Braginskii 1965
given bellow by (
E70
), one notices

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}^{\textrm{BR}}=(\ref{eq:W0_Brag})=\frac{3}{2}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}})\Big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}:\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}})\Big(\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}-\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\Big)\neq\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0},

(E68)

and his result is valid only if
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}
is traceless (which it is).
The reason why Braginskii left his result in form (
E70
) and did not simplify it with
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}:\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}=0

is likely an alternative form (
E71
).

E.4.2
Symmetric and traceless matrices

We further consider only symmetric and traceless matrices
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}
and
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}
, so all previous expressions are simplified with

\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}:\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}=0
,
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}:\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}=0
and the BGK parallel stress-tensor
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{0}=-(p/\nu)\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}
. For clarity, it is useful to write several possible forms for

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}:\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}})\Big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\Big);

(E69)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}\Big[\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}:\Big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\Big)\Big]\Big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\Big);

(E70)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}\Big[(\nabla\boldsymbol{u})^{S}:\Big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\Big)\Big]\Big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\Big);

(E71)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3\Big[(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}):\Big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\Big)\Big]\Big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\Big);

(E72)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3\Big[\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\cdot(\nabla\boldsymbol{u})\cdot\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}-\frac{1}{3}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\Big]\Big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\Big).

(E73)

Braginskii uses (
E70
), for example
Fitzpatrick 2015
uses (
E72
), and we use (
E69
). In the reference frame of
magnetic field lines

\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}=\frac{3}{2}W_{zz}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}-1/3,&0,&0\\
0,&-1/3,&0\\
0,&0,&+2/3\end{array}\right);\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{0}=\frac{p}{\nu}W_{zz}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1/2,&0,&0\\
0,&1/2,&0\\
0,&0,&-1\end{array}\right).

(E74)

To solve equation (
E33
) it is
beneficial to introduce two other matrices
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{3}
,
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{4}
by decomposing

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}})^{S}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{3}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{4};

(E75)

\displaystyle 2\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp})^{S};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}})^{S},

where in the reference frame of magnetic field lines

\displaystyle 2\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{3}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}-2W_{xy}&W_{xx}-W_{yy}&0\\
W_{xx}-W_{yy}&2W_{xy}&0\\
0&0&0\end{array}\right);\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{4}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&-W_{yz}\\
0&0&W_{xz}\\
-W_{yz}&W_{xz}&0\end{array}\right).

Decomposition (
E75
) is again valid for general
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}
which is easily verified by using
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}+\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}
,
and the stress-tensor is decomposed in the same way

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}})^{S}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{3}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{4};

(E83)

\displaystyle 2\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp})^{S};

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}})^{S}.

Finally, by applying
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times
at matrices
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}\ldots\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{4}
yields the following identities

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0})^{S}=0;\qquad\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{3};\qquad(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{2})^{S}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{4};

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{3}=-\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1};\qquad(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{4})^{S}=-\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{2},

(E84)

which are easy to verify in a general reference frame with
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}
. The same identities hold for the stress-tensor

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{0})^{S}=0;\qquad\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{1}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{3};\qquad(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{2})^{S}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{4};

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{3}=-\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{1};\qquad(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{4})^{S}=-\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{2}.

(E85)

Final solution

Now we are ready to solve equation (
E33
), which is rewritten as

2\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{3}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{4}+\frac{\nu}{\Omega}\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{0}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{1}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{2}\big)=-\frac{p}{\Omega}\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{2}\big).

(E86)

One solution
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{0}=-(p/\nu)\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}
