has already been obtained, and can be eliminated from (
E86
).
For the rest of the equation, the most straightforward approach is to be guided by the reference frame of magnetic field lines,
which shows that the system (
E86
) can be directly split into two independent equations

\displaystyle 2\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{3}+\frac{\nu}{\Omega}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{1}=-\frac{p}{\Omega}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1};

(E87)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{4}+\frac{\nu}{\Omega}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{2}=-\frac{p}{\Omega}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{2}.

(E88)

In the general reference frame, the split can be achieved for example by applying
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\cdot
from left & right at (
E86
),
which by using identities
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{4}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}=0
;
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{2}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}=0
and
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{2}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}_{\perp}=0

yields (
E87
) and subsequently (
E88
). The split significantly simplifies the “inversion procedure”.

Furthermore, by applying
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times
at (
E87
), applying
\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times
together with symmetric operator at (
E88
),
and using identities (
E84
)-(
E85
)
then gives

\displaystyle-2\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{1}+\frac{\nu}{\Omega}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{3}=-\frac{p}{\Omega}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{3};

(E89)

\displaystyle-\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{2}+\frac{\nu}{\Omega}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{4}=-\frac{p}{\Omega}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{4}.

(E90)

Equations (
E87
), (
E89
) are coupled and can be treated as 2 equations in 2 unknowns, and similarly
equations (
E88
), (
E90
), finally yielding solutions

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{p\nu}{4\Omega^{2}+\nu^{2}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1}+\frac{2p\Omega}{4\Omega^{2}+\nu^{2}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{3};

(E91)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{p\nu}{\Omega^{2}+\nu^{2}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{2}+\frac{p\Omega}{\Omega^{2}+\nu^{2}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{4}.

(E92)

The entire solution for the stress-tensor
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{0}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{1}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{2}
thus reads

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}=-\frac{p}{\nu}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{0}-\frac{p\nu}{4\Omega^{2}+\nu^{2}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{1}-\frac{p\nu}{\Omega^{2}+\nu^{2}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{2}+\frac{2p\Omega}{4\Omega^{2}+\nu^{2}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{3}+\frac{p\Omega}{\Omega^{2}+\nu^{2}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{4}.

(E93)

E.5
BGK operator and electric field

The BGK operator is also an excellent tool to clarify various processes in fully ionized or partially ionized plasmas.
Here we want to clarify the Ohmic (magnetic) diffusion together with the ambipolar diffusion, both caused by the
momentum exchange rates

\boldsymbol{R}_{a}=\sum_{b\neq a}\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}(\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}).

(E94)

From the BGK perspective, one does not need to worry about complicated Landau and Boltzmann operators,
and simply “adopt” correct collisional frequencies; see for example Appendix C of
Schunk 1977
.
Momentum exhange rates (
E94
) are actually
the correct answer if relative drift velocities are small and one considers the 5-moment model (i.e. if
the heat flux is neglected).

We restrict our focus on spatial scales much longer than the Debye length.
The displacement current is neglected, the Gauss’s law
\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{E}=4\pi e\sum_{a}Z_{a}n_{a}
is replaced by
the charge neutrality and no condition is placed on
\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{E}
. The Maxwell’s equations then read

\displaystyle\sum_{a}Z_{a}n_{a}=0;\qquad\boldsymbol{j}=\sum_{a}eZ_{a}n_{a}\boldsymbol{u}_{a}=\frac{c}{4\pi}\nabla\times\boldsymbol{B};

(E95)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{B}}{\partial t}=-c\nabla\times\boldsymbol{E};\qquad\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{B}=0.

(E96)

By focusing on spatial and temporal scales of the ion and neutral species,
we do not need to resolve the electron motion. In the electron momentum equation the electron inertia
represented by
d_{e}\boldsymbol{u}_{e}/dt
is neglected (which does not mean that
m_{e}=0
, relations
\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}=\rho_{b}\nu_{ba}
still hold),
and the electric field is expressed as

\boldsymbol{E}=-\frac{1}{c}\boldsymbol{u}_{e}\times\boldsymbol{B}-\frac{1}{en_{e}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{e}+\frac{\boldsymbol{R}_{e}}{en_{e}}.

(E97)

Momentum equations for ions then become

\rho_{i}\frac{d_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}}{dt}+\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{i}+\frac{Z_{i}n_{i}}{n_{e}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{e}-\frac{eZ_{i}n_{i}}{c}(\boldsymbol{u}_{i}-\boldsymbol{u}_{e})\times\boldsymbol{B}=\boldsymbol{R}_{i}+\frac{Z_{i}n_{i}}{n_{e}}\boldsymbol{R}_{e}.

(E98)

Also, by using (
E95
), the electron density
n_{e}
and electron velocity
\boldsymbol{u}_{e}
is expressed as

n_{e}=\sum_{i}Z_{i}n_{i};\qquad\boldsymbol{u}_{e}=\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i}Z_{i}n_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}-\frac{\boldsymbol{j}}{en_{e}};\qquad\boldsymbol{j}=\frac{c}{4\pi}\nabla\times\boldsymbol{B},

(E99)

where the summations are over ion species. The electron density equation

\partial n_{e}/\partial t+\nabla\cdot(n_{e}\boldsymbol{u}_{e})=0
becomes redundant, because
multiplying all the density equations for charges (including electrons) by
Z_{a}
and summing them together yields a requirement

\nabla\cdot(\sum_{a}Z_{a}n_{a}\boldsymbol{u}_{a})=0
, which is satisfied by

\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{j}=0
in (
E99
) automatically. Expressions (
E99
) and (
E97
) then can
be substituted to all other equations (which is easy to do numerically), and the occurence of
\boldsymbol{E},\boldsymbol{u}_{e},n_{e}
in the
entire model is thus elliminated.

For a particular case of
\boldsymbol{R}_{e}
given by (
E94
), the electric field (
E97
) then becomes

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{cn_{e}}\big(\sum_{i}Z_{i}n_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\big)\times\boldsymbol{B}+\frac{\boldsymbol{j}\times\boldsymbol{B}}{cen_{e}}-\frac{1}{en_{e}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{e}+\frac{m_{e}}{e^{2}n_{e}}\boldsymbol{j}\sum_{a\neq e}\nu_{ea}

(E100)

\displaystyle+\frac{m_{e}}{e}\Big[\big(\sum_{a\neq e}\nu_{ea}\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\big)-\frac{1}{n_{e}}\big(\sum_{a\neq e}\nu_{ea}\big)\big(\sum_{i}Z_{i}n_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\big)\Big].

Summations over ’a’ include both ions and neutrals.
Terms on the r.h.s. can be called the convective term, the Hall term, the electron pressure term, the Ohmic term, and a mixed
collisional term due to ion and neutral velocities.
When (
E100
) is used in the induction equation, the Ohmic term (
\sim\boldsymbol{j}
) becomes directly diffusive through
identity
\nabla\times(\eta_{B}\nabla\times\boldsymbol{B})=-\eta_{B}\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{B}+\nabla(\eta_{B})\times(\nabla\times\boldsymbol{B})
,
where one defines a coefficient of magnetic diffusion
\eta_{B}=(\sum_{a\neq e}\nu_{ea})m_{e}c^{2}/(4\pi e^{2}n_{e})
. In contrast, no other term
in (
E100
) is directly diffusive in this sense. Nevertheless, the so-called ambipolar diffusion due to differences
in velocities
\boldsymbol{u}_{a}
between different species is still present implicitly, which can be
shown by solving dispersion relations. The explicit presence of ambipolar diffusion caused by
\sim-(\boldsymbol{j}\times\boldsymbol{B})\times\boldsymbol{B}=\boldsymbol{j}_{\perp}|\boldsymbol{B}|^{2}

is revealed by a construction of a single fluid model, formulated with respect to the center-of-mass velocity of all the species.
In general, ambipolar diffusion between two species with indices
(a,b)
exists if

\frac{Z_{a}}{m_{a}}\neq\frac{Z_{b}}{m_{b}},

(E101)

which is demonstrated in Section
E.6
.

In partially ionized solar plasmas one often focuses on a two-fluid model formulated with center-of-mass velocities
for the ion species
\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\rangle=(\sum_{i}\rho_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i})/\sum_{i}\rho_{i}
and for the neutral species

\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\rangle=(\sum_{n}\rho_{n}\boldsymbol{u}_{n})/\sum_{n}\rho_{n}
. Velocities for each species are thus decomposed into

\boldsymbol{u}_{i}=\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\rangle+\boldsymbol{w}_{i}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{n}=\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\rangle+\boldsymbol{w}_{n}
where
\boldsymbol{w}_{i},\boldsymbol{w}_{n}
represent drifts, and
because
\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\rangle
,
\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\rangle
can be pulled out in front of the summations
the electric field (
E100
) transforms into

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{c}\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\rangle\times\boldsymbol{B}-\frac{1}{cn_{e}}\big(\sum_{i}Z_{i}n_{i}\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\big)\times\boldsymbol{B}+\frac{\boldsymbol{j}\times\boldsymbol{B}}{cen_{e}}-\frac{1}{en_{e}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{e}+\frac{m_{e}}{e^{2}n_{e}}\boldsymbol{j}\sum_{a\neq e}\nu_{ea}

(E102)

\displaystyle+\big(\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\rangle-\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\rangle\big)\frac{m_{e}}{e}\sum_{n}\nu_{en}

\displaystyle+\frac{m_{e}}{e}\Big[\big(\sum_{a\neq e}\nu_{ea}\boldsymbol{w}_{a}\big)-\frac{1}{n_{e}}\big(\sum_{a\neq e}\nu_{ea}\big)\big(\sum_{i}Z_{i}n_{i}\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\big)\Big].

Electric field (
E102
) still represents multi-fluid electric field, where one considers
separate evolution equations for all the drifts
\boldsymbol{w}_{a}
. To obtain a two-fluid electric field these drifts have to be
somehow elliminated, which is of course not straightforward to justify.
In partially ionized solar plasmas the usual justification is that 1) one takes into account only the first
ionization degree, with all the ions having
Z_{i}=1
; 2) by precribing
that on average
\sum_{i}n_{i}\boldsymbol{w}_{i}=0
(which for example eliminates ambipolar diffusion between different ions) together
with
\sum_{n}n_{n}\boldsymbol{w}_{n}=0
;
3) that all the species have roughly the same temperature which by using collisional freqencies

\nu_{ei}=n_{i}f(T)/\sqrt{m_{e}}
yields
\sum_{i}\nu_{ei}\boldsymbol{w}_{i}=0
; 4) that all the neutrals have roughly same cross-sections
(radii
r_{n}
) which by using
\nu_{en}=n_{n}f(T)r_{n}^{2}/\sqrt{m_{e}}
yields
\sum_{n}\nu_{en}\boldsymbol{w}_{n}=0
.
The two-fluid electric field thus reads

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{c}\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\rangle\times\boldsymbol{B}+\frac{\boldsymbol{j}\times\boldsymbol{B}}{cen_{e}}-\frac{1}{en_{e}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{e}+\frac{m_{e}}{e^{2}n_{e}}\boldsymbol{j}\sum_{a\neq e}\nu_{ea}

(E103)

\displaystyle+\big(\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\rangle-\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\rangle\big)\frac{m_{e}}{e}\sum_{n}\nu_{en}.

The center-of-mass velocity for ions
\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\rangle
can be freely replaced by the center-of-mass velocity for all the charges

\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{c}\rangle
(which includes electrons).
Then electric field (
E103
) is almost identical to equation (115) of
Khomenko et al. 2014
, except that
the
\sum_{n}\nu_{en}
in the last term of (
E103
) is replaced by
(\sum_{n}\nu_{en})-(\sum_{i}\sum_{n}\nu_{in})
in that paper.
The difference arises from an alternative approach in that paper, where the electron inertia is not
neglected from the beginning, but instead the electric field is derived by first summing momentum equations for all the species together, and
prescribing quasi-static current
\boldsymbol{j}
. Then, subsequent expansion in mass-ratios retains contributions from
\boldsymbol{R}_{i}
. Nevertheless,
the missing contributions are small
\nu_{in}\ll\nu_{en}
, explaining the small difference between these two approaches.

For a particular case of only one ion species and one neutral species,
so that
n_{e}=Z_{i}n_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{e}=\boldsymbol{u}_{i}-\boldsymbol{j}/(en_{e})
,
the electric field (
E100
) simplifies into

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{c}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\times\boldsymbol{B}+\frac{\boldsymbol{j}\times\boldsymbol{B}}{cen_{e}}-\frac{1}{en_{e}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{e}+\frac{m_{e}}{e^{2}n_{e}}\boldsymbol{j}(\nu_{ei}+\nu_{en})

(E104)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{m_{e}}{e}\nu_{en}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}-\boldsymbol{u}_{i});

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{B}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla\times(\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\times\boldsymbol{B})-\nabla\times\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{j}}{en_{e}}\times\boldsymbol{B}\Big)+\frac{c}{e}\nabla\times\Big(\frac{1}{n_{e}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{e}\Big)

(E105)

\displaystyle\quad-\nabla\times\big(\eta_{B}\nabla\times\boldsymbol{B}\big)-\nabla\times\Big[\frac{cm_{e}}{e}\nu_{en}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}-\boldsymbol{u}_{i})\Big],

with the coefficient of magnetic diffusion
\eta_{B}=(\nu_{ei}+\nu_{en})m_{e}c^{2}/(4\pi e^{2}n_{e})
.

E.6
Ambipolar diffusion of two ion species

Here we consider a two-fluid model consisting of two different ion species with species indices
(i,j)
, so the charge neutrality reads
n_{e}=Z_{i}n_{i}+Z_{j}n_{j}
.
A particular case consisting of one ion and one neutral species can be obtained by prescribing
Z_{j}=0
and index
j=n
(or
Z_{i}=0
and
i=n
).
The momentum equations are

\displaystyle\rho_{i}\frac{d_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}}{dt}+\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{i}+\frac{Z_{i}n_{i}}{n_{e}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{e}-\frac{eZ_{i}n_{i}}{c}\frac{Z_{j}n_{j}}{n_{e}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{i}-\boldsymbol{u}_{j})\times\boldsymbol{B}-\frac{Z_{i}n_{i}}{cn_{e}}\boldsymbol{j}\times\boldsymbol{B}=\boldsymbol{R}_{i}+\frac{Z_{i}n_{i}}{n_{e}}\boldsymbol{R}_{e};

(E106)

\displaystyle\rho_{j}\frac{d_{j}\boldsymbol{u}_{j}}{dt}+\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{j}+\frac{Z_{j}n_{j}}{n_{e}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{e}+\frac{eZ_{i}n_{i}}{c}\frac{Z_{j}n_{j}}{n_{e}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{i}-\boldsymbol{u}_{j})\times\boldsymbol{B}-\frac{Z_{j}n_{j}}{cn_{e}}\boldsymbol{j}\times\boldsymbol{B}=\boldsymbol{R}_{j}+\frac{Z_{j}n_{j}}{n_{e}}\boldsymbol{R}_{e},

(E107)

with the collisional right hand sides

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{i}+\frac{Z_{i}n_{i}}{n_{e}}\boldsymbol{R}_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(\boldsymbol{u}_{i}-\boldsymbol{u}_{j})\Big[\rho_{i}\nu_{ij}+\rho_{e}\nu_{ei}\Big(\frac{Z_{j}n_{j}}{n_{e}}\Big)^{2}+\rho_{e}\nu_{ej}\Big(\frac{Z_{i}n_{i}}{n_{e}}\Big)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle-\boldsymbol{j}\frac{m_{e}}{en_{e}}(\nu_{ei}Z_{j}n_{j}-\nu_{ej}Z_{i}n_{i});

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{j}+\frac{Z_{j}n_{j}}{n_{e}}\boldsymbol{R}_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{u}_{i}-\boldsymbol{u}_{j})\Big[\rho_{i}\nu_{ij}+\rho_{e}\nu_{ei}\Big(\frac{Z_{j}n_{j}}{n_{e}}\Big)^{2}+\rho_{e}\nu_{ej}\Big(\frac{Z_{i}n_{i}}{n_{e}}\Big)^{2}\Big]

(E108)

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{j}\frac{m_{e}}{en_{e}}(\nu_{ei}Z_{j}n_{j}-\nu_{ej}Z_{i}n_{i}),

and the electric field (which determines the induction equation) reads

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{cn_{e}}\big(Z_{i}n_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}+Z_{j}n_{j}\boldsymbol{u}_{j}\big)\times\boldsymbol{B}+\frac{\boldsymbol{j}\times\boldsymbol{B}}{cen_{e}}-\frac{1}{en_{e}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{e}+\frac{m_{e}}{e^{2}n_{e}}\boldsymbol{j}(\nu_{ei}+\nu_{ej})

(E109)

\displaystyle+\frac{m_{e}}{en_{e}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{i}-\boldsymbol{u}_{j})\big(Z_{j}n_{j}\nu_{ei}-Z_{i}n_{i}\nu_{ej}\big).

The ambipolar diffusion term
-\boldsymbol{j}\times\boldsymbol{B}\times\boldsymbol{B}=\boldsymbol{j}_{\perp}|\boldsymbol{B}|^{2}
is not directly present in the
electric field, and the only term which directly causes magnetic diffusion in the induction equation is the Ohmic term
(\sim\boldsymbol{j})
.
Nevertheless, the ambipolar diffusion is still present implicitly, which can be shown by solving dispersion relations,
or by constructing a single-fluid model.

By using the same notation as
Zaqarashvili et al. 2011
and introducing center-of-mass velocity
\boldsymbol{V}=(\rho_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}+\rho_{j}\boldsymbol{u}_{j})/\rho
where
the total density
\rho=\rho_{i}+\rho_{j}
, and difference in velocities
\boldsymbol{w}=\boldsymbol{u}_{i}-\boldsymbol{u}_{j}
, so that
\boldsymbol{u}_{i}=\boldsymbol{V}+(\rho_{j}/\rho)\boldsymbol{w}
,

\boldsymbol{u}_{j}=\boldsymbol{V}-(\rho_{i}/\rho)\boldsymbol{w}
, yields momentum equations

\displaystyle\rho\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{V}}{\partial t}+\rho\boldsymbol{V}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{V}+\nabla\cdot(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{i}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{j}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{e})-\frac{1}{c}\boldsymbol{j}\times\boldsymbol{B}+\nabla\cdot\Big(\frac{\rho_{i}\rho_{j}}{\rho}\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{w}\Big)=0;

(E110)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{w}}{\partial t}+\boldsymbol{w}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{V}+\boldsymbol{V}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{w}+\frac{\rho_{j}}{\rho}\boldsymbol{w}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{w}-\boldsymbol{w}\cdot\nabla\big(\frac{\rho_{i}}{\rho}\boldsymbol{w}\big)-\frac{eZ_{i}n_{i}Z_{j}n_{j}}{cn_{e}}\frac{\rho}{\rho_{i}\rho_{j}}\boldsymbol{w}\times\boldsymbol{B}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{\rho_{i}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{i}-\frac{1}{\rho_{j}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{j}+\frac{1}{n_{e}}\Big(\frac{Z_{i}n_{i}}{\rho_{i}}-\frac{Z_{j}n_{j}}{\rho_{j}}\Big)\Big(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{e}-\frac{1}{c}\boldsymbol{j}\times\boldsymbol{B}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad=-\boldsymbol{w}\frac{\rho}{\rho_{i}\rho_{j}}\Big[\rho_{i}\nu_{ij}+\rho_{e}\nu_{ei}\Big(\frac{Z_{j}n_{j}}{n_{e}}\Big)^{2}+\rho_{e}\nu_{ej}\Big(\frac{Z_{i}n_{i}}{n_{e}}\Big)^{2}\Big]-\boldsymbol{j}\frac{\rho}{\rho_{i}\rho_{j}}\frac{m_{e}}{en_{e}}(\nu_{ei}Z_{j}n_{j}-\nu_{ej}Z_{i}n_{i}),

(E111)

with electric field

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{c}\boldsymbol{V}\times\boldsymbol{B}-\frac{1}{cn_{e}\rho}(Z_{i}n_{i}\rho_{j}-Z_{j}n_{j}\rho_{i})\boldsymbol{w}\times\boldsymbol{B}+\frac{\boldsymbol{j}\times\boldsymbol{B}}{cen_{e}}-\frac{1}{en_{e}}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{e}+\frac{m_{e}}{e^{2}n_{e}}\boldsymbol{j}(\nu_{ei}+\nu_{ej})

(E112)

\displaystyle+\frac{m_{e}}{en_{e}}\boldsymbol{w}\big(Z_{j}n_{j}\nu_{ei}-Z_{i}n_{i}\nu_{ej}\big).

System (
E110
)-(
E112
) is of course equivalent to (
E106
)-(
E109
).
However, in a particular case when the collisions are very frequent, the r.h.s. of (
E111
) becomes
very large, and by neglecting all the “inertial” terms in the first line of (
E111
) with
\boldsymbol{w}
, allows one to obtain
an explicit expression for the velocity difference

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{D}\Big[-\boldsymbol{j}\frac{m_{e}}{en_{e}}(\nu_{ei}Z_{j}n_{j}-\nu_{ej}Z_{i}n_{i})-\frac{\rho_{j}}{\rho}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{i}+\frac{\rho_{i}}{\rho}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{j}-\frac{1}{\rho n_{e}}\big(Z_{i}n_{i}\rho_{j}-Z_{j}n_{j}\rho_{i}\big)\Big(\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{e}-\frac{1}{c}\boldsymbol{j}\times\boldsymbol{B}\Big)\Big],

(E113)

where we defined denominator

D=\Big[\rho_{i}\nu_{ij}+\rho_{e}\nu_{ei}\Big(\frac{Z_{j}n_{j}}{n_{e}}\Big)^{2}+\rho_{e}\nu_{ej}\Big(\frac{Z_{i}n_{i}}{n_{e}}\Big)^{2}\Big].

(E114)

For frequent collisions only the first term in (
E113
)
\sim\boldsymbol{j}
is finite, and all other terms are small. Nevertheless, the sought-after term
is the last term in (
E113
)
\sim\boldsymbol{j}\times\boldsymbol{B}
, because when (
E113
) is used in (
E112
) it creates the ambipolar term

\sim-\boldsymbol{j}\times\boldsymbol{B}\times\boldsymbol{B}
. The single-fluid electric field reads

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{c}\boldsymbol{V}\times\boldsymbol{B}+\boldsymbol{j}\frac{m_{e}}{e^{2}n_{e}}\Big[\nu_{ei}+\nu_{ej}-\frac{m_{e}}{n_{e}D}(\nu_{ei}Z_{j}n_{j}-\nu_{ej}Z_{i}n_{i})^{2}\Big]

(E115)

\displaystyle+\frac{\boldsymbol{j}\times\boldsymbol{B}}{cen_{e}}\Big[1+\frac{2m_{e}}{n_{e}\rho D}(Z_{i}n_{i}\rho_{j}-Z_{j}n_{j}\rho_{i})(\nu_{ei}Z_{j}n_{j}-\nu_{ej}Z_{i}n_{i})\Big]

\displaystyle-\,\frac{\boldsymbol{j}\times\boldsymbol{B}\times\boldsymbol{B}}{c^{2}n_{e}^{2}\rho^{2}D}(Z_{i}n_{i}\rho_{j}-Z_{j}n_{j}\rho_{i})^{2}

\displaystyle-\frac{\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{e}}{en_{e}}\Big[1+\frac{m_{e}}{\rho n_{e}D}\big(Z_{j}n_{j}\nu_{ei}-Z_{i}n_{i}\nu_{ej}\big)\big(Z_{i}n_{i}\rho_{j}-Z_{j}n_{j}\rho_{i}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle+\frac{m_{e}}{en_{e}\rho D}\big(Z_{j}n_{j}\nu_{ei}-Z_{i}n_{i}\nu_{ej}\big)\Big[-\rho_{j}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{i}+\rho_{i}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{j}\Big]

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{cn_{e}\rho^{2}D}(Z_{i}n_{i}\rho_{j}-Z_{j}n_{j}\rho_{i})\Big[-\rho_{j}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{i}+\rho_{i}\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{j}-\frac{1}{n_{e}}\big(Z_{i}n_{i}\rho_{j}-Z_{j}n_{j}\rho_{i}\big)\nabla\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{e}\Big]\times\boldsymbol{B}.

Importantly, the sign in front of the ambipolar term is negative, and because

-\boldsymbol{j}\times\boldsymbol{B}\times\boldsymbol{B}=+\boldsymbol{j}_{\perp}|\boldsymbol{B}|^{2}
, the term indeed creates diffusion in the induction equation.
It is possible to define a coefficient of ambipolar diffusion

\eta_{A}=\frac{|\boldsymbol{B}|^{2}}{4\pi\rho}A=V_{A}^{2}A;\quad\textrm{where}\quad A=\frac{\big(Z_{i}n_{i}\rho_{j}-Z_{j}n_{j}\rho_{i}\big)^{2}}{n_{e}^{2}\rho D},

(E116)

and
V_{A}
is the Alfvén speed. As a double check, prescribing zero charge for one of the species, electric field (
E115
) identifies with equation (A.10) of

Zaqarashvili et al. 2011
(for example, our denominator simplifies to
D=\alpha_{in}+\alpha_{en}=\alpha_{n}
). Also,

\eta_{A}=|\boldsymbol{B}|^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}/(4\pi\rho^{2}(\rho_{i}\nu_{in}+\rho_{e}\nu_{en}))
identifies with the usual coefficient of ambipolar diffusion; see for
example equation (20) in
Khomenko & Collados 2012
(after switching to cgs units with
\mu_{0}\to 4\pi
).
The ambipolar diffusion exists if

\frac{Z_{i}}{m_{i}}\neq\frac{Z_{j}}{m_{j}}.

(E117)

It is important to emphasize that the reduction to a single-fluid model was obtained by assuming that collisions
are sufficiently frequent, and the ambipolar diffusion (as well as other terms) now contain a denominator
D
, which can be simplified into
D=\rho_{i}\nu_{ij}
.
So when collisional frequencies
\nu_{ij}
become small, leads to an artificial “explosion” of the ambipolar diffusion.
This is nicely demonstrated in the figures of
Zaqarashvili et al. 2011
plotted with respect to a wavenumber

\bar{k}\sim k/\nu
, where it is shown that for a single-fluid description, the ambipolar diffusion in a collisionless regime (when
\bar{k}
becomes large)
yields cut-off frequencies for waves. The mechanism is completely analogous to the “explosion” of the Braginskii stress-tensor or the heat
flux vector in a collisionless regime. In contrast, as they show in their two-fluid figures,
no “explosion” of the ambipolar diffusion is present. The effect is further discussed in
Zaqarashvili et al. 2012
.

E.6.1
Damping of Alfvén waves

For example, considering Alfvén waves at long-wavelengths and focusing only on the ambipolar diffusion
(with the Hall-term, Ohmic terms and pressure terms neglected), the induction equation reads

\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{B}}{\partial t}=\nabla\times(\boldsymbol{V}\times\boldsymbol{B})+\nabla\times\Big[\eta_{A}(\nabla\times\boldsymbol{B})_{\perp}\Big],

(E118)

with the coefficient of ambipolar diffusion (
E116
).
This yields the following dispersion relation for Alfvén waves

\omega^{2}+i\omega V_{a}^{2}k_{\parallel}^{2}A-V_{A}^{2}k_{\parallel}^{2}=0,

(E119)

with solutions

\omega=\pm V_{A}|k_{\parallel}|\sqrt{1-\Big(\frac{V_{A}k_{\parallel}A}{2}\Big)^{2}}-i\frac{V_{A}^{2}k_{\parallel}^{2}A}{2}.

(E120)

Obviously, the Alfvén waves are damped and for wavenumbers
k_{\parallel}\geq 2/(V_{A}A)

the real part of the frequency even becomes zero, so the wave stops existing (i.e. cut-off wavenumber). For the particular case
of one specie being neutral, the quantity
A=\rho_{n}^{2}/(\rho\alpha_{n})
, which can be approximated as

A=\rho_{n}^{2}/(\rho\alpha_{in})=\zeta_{n}^{2}/(\zeta_{i}\nu_{in})
. Then
expressions (
E119
), (
E120
) identify with equations (44)-(47) of
Zaqarashvili et al. 2011
, however,
one needs to use their definition
\nu_{in}=\alpha_{in}/\rho
instead of the more logical (and correct)
\nu_{in}=\alpha_{in}/\rho_{i}
.

Appendix F
General Fokker-Planck collisional operator

For Coulomb collisions, the Boltzmann collisional operator can be approximated by a general Fokker-Planck type of collisional operator,

C_{ab}(f_{a},f_{b})=-\nabla_{v}\cdot\Big[\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}f_{a}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{v}\cdot(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}f_{a})\Big],

(F1)

where higher-order derivatives in velocity space are neglected, and where
\boldsymbol{A}
is called a dynamical friction vector
and
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}
is called a diffusion tensor. In space physics and astrophysics, various
approximations for
\boldsymbol{A}
and
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}
are used, and if a collisional operator has form (
F1
),
then equation (
A1
) is summarily called the Fokker-Planck equation.
Summation over all the species (including self-collisions) then defines the full operator
C(f_{a})=\sum_{b}C_{ab}(f_{a},f_{b})
which can be also
written as
C(f_{a})=-\nabla_{v}\cdot[\boldsymbol{A}_{a}f_{a}-(1/2)\nabla_{v}\cdot(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{a}f_{a})]
, where one
defines
\boldsymbol{A}_{a}=\sum_{b}\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}
&
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{a}=\sum_{b}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}
.
The Fokker-Planck operators work extremely well for any collisional
process where collisions with a small scattering angle dominate, and where a lot of subsequent collisions gradually yield (in a sense of
a random walk) a significant deviation from a particle original velocity direction. This is exactly the case for scattering by the electrostatic
Coulomb force, where the Rutherford scattering cross-section is proportional to
1/\sin^{4}(\chi/2)
, and heavily dominated by events with a
small scattering angle
\chi
.

For any tensor
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}
, a general Fokker-Planck operator can be integrated according to

\int\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}C_{ab}(f_{a},f_{b})d^{3}v=\int f_{a}\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}\cdot\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}d^{3}v+\frac{1}{2}\int f_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}:\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}d^{3}v,

(F2)

and for clarity explicitly in the index notation

\int\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}C_{ab}(f_{a},f_{b})d^{3}v=\int f_{a}A^{ab}_{i}\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}}{\partial v_{i}}d^{3}v+\frac{1}{2}\int f_{a}D^{ab}_{ij}\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{i}}\frac{\partial\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}}}{\partial v_{j}}d^{3}v.

(F3)

Useful identities are

\displaystyle\frac{\partial|\boldsymbol{v}|}{\partial v_{i}}=\frac{v_{i}}{|\boldsymbol{v}|};\quad\frac{\partial|\boldsymbol{c}|}{\partial v_{i}}=\frac{c_{i}}{|\boldsymbol{c}|};\quad\frac{\partial|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}}{\partial v_{i}}=2v_{i};\quad\frac{\partial|\boldsymbol{c}|^{2}}{\partial v_{i}}=2c_{i},

(F4)

and the tensorial collisional contributions defined in (
5
) can be calculated according to

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{a}\int f_{a}\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}d^{3}v;

(F5)

\displaystyle Q_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{a}\int f_{a}\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}\cdot\boldsymbol{c}_{a}d^{3}v+\frac{m_{a}}{2}\int f_{a}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}d^{3}v;

(F6)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{ab}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{a}\int f_{a}\big[\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\big]^{S}d^{3}v+\frac{m_{a}}{2}\int f_{a}\big[\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}\big]^{S}d^{3}v;

(F7)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{ab}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{a}\int f_{a}\big[\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\big]^{S}d^{3}v+\frac{m_{a}}{2}\int f_{a}\big[\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}^{S}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\big]^{S}d^{3}v.

(F8)

If the diffusion tensor is symmetric then
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{S}_{ab}=2\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}

(For clarity, the symmetric operator does not act on species indices and in general
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}\neq\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ba}
similarly to
\nu_{ab}\neq\nu_{ba}
,
the symmetric operator acts as
(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab}_{ij})^{S}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab}_{ij}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab}_{ji}
).
The 4th & 5th-order collisional contributions are

\displaystyle\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{ab}^{(4)}\big)_{ijkl}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{a}\int f_{a}\big[\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\big]^{S}_{ijkl}d^{3}v+\frac{m_{a}}{2}\int f_{a}\Big[\big[\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}^{S}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\big]^{S}_{ijkl}+(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab})^{S}_{ik}c_{j}^{a}c_{l}^{a}+(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab})^{S}_{jl}c_{i}^{a}c_{k}^{a}\Big]d^{3}v;

(F9)

\displaystyle\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{ab}^{(5)}\big)_{ijklm}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{a}\int f_{a}\big[\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\big]^{S}_{ijklm}d^{3}v+\frac{m_{a}}{2}\int f_{a}\Big[\big[\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}^{S}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\big]^{S}_{ijklm}+(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab})^{S}_{ik}c_{j}^{a}c_{l}^{a}c_{m}^{a}

(F10)

\displaystyle+(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab})^{S}_{il}c_{j}^{a}c_{k}^{a}c_{m}^{a}+(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab})^{S}_{jl}c_{i}^{a}c_{k}^{a}c_{m}^{a}+(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab})^{S}_{jm}c_{i}^{a}c_{k}^{a}c_{l}^{a}+(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab})^{S}_{km}c_{i}^{a}c_{j}^{a}c_{l}^{a}\Big]d^{3}v.

The first integral in (
F9
) proportional to
\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}
contains 4 terms, and the second integral in
(
F9
) proportional to
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab}
contains 12 terms. Similarly, the first integral in (
F10
) contains 5 terms
and the second integral in (
F10
) contains 20 terms. The second integrals in (
F9
)-(
F10
) can be written simply by
picking two indices for
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab}
and giving the rest of indices to
\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}
&
\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}
.
Generalization to an n-th order collisional contributions defined in (
A10
)
is done naturally by introducing a set of indices
R=\{r_{1}\ldots r_{n}\}
together with an ordered set
(s_{1},s_{2})
, and writing

\displaystyle\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{ab}^{(n)}\big)_{r_{1}r_{2}\ldots r_{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{a}\int f_{a}\big[A^{ab}_{r_{1}}c^{a}_{r_{2}}\ldots c^{a}_{r_{n}}\big]^{S}d^{3}v+\frac{m_{a}}{2}\int f_{a}\Big[D^{ab}_{s_{1}s_{2}}c_{s_{3}}\ldots c_{s_{n}}\Big]d^{3}v;

(F11)

\displaystyle\textrm{where}\quad(s_{1},s_{2})\in R=\{r_{1}\ldots r_{n}\};\quad\textrm{and}\quad s_{3}\ldots s_{n}\in R\setminus\{s_{1},s_{2}\},

so that the first integral contains (n) terms, and the second integral contains
2\binom{n}{2}=n(n-1)
terms. Alternatively,
one can replace the ordered set
(s_{1},s_{2})
by a non-ordered set
\{s_{1},s_{2}\}
and include the symmetric operator on
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab}
.

It is useful to write collisional contributions for contracted vectors, matrices and
scalars, by assuming symmetric
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab}
. We use definitions from Section
VIII.3
, see equation (
188
),
which were also used in Appendix
D
; see equation (
D4
) & (
D5
). This yields collisional contributions
for vectors

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}}^{(2n+1)}_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{a}\int\Big[(2n)(\boldsymbol{A}^{ab}\cdot\boldsymbol{c}_{a})\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2n-2}+\boldsymbol{A}^{ab}|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2n}\Big]f_{a}d^{3}v

(F12)

\displaystyle+m_{a}\int\Big[(2n)(n-1)(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab}:\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a})\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2n-4}+(n)(\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab})\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2n-2}

\displaystyle\qquad+(2n)(\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab})|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2n-2}\Big]f_{a}d^{3}v;

matrices

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}}}^{(2n)}_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{a}\int\Big[(\boldsymbol{A}^{ab}\boldsymbol{c}_{a})^{S}|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2n-2}+(2n-2)(\boldsymbol{A}^{ab}\cdot\boldsymbol{c}_{a})\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2n-4}\Big]f_{a}d^{3}v

(F13)

\displaystyle+m_{a}\int\Big[\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab}|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2n-2}+(2n-2)(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab}\cdot\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a})^{S}|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2n-4}

\displaystyle+(n-1)(\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab})\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2n-4}+(n-1)(2n-4)(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab}:\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a})\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2n-6}\Big]f_{a}d^{3}v;

and scalars

\displaystyle Q^{(2n)}_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{a}\int\Big[(2n)(\boldsymbol{A}^{ab}\cdot\boldsymbol{c}_{a})|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2n-2}+(n)(\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab})|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2n-2}

(F14)

\displaystyle+(2n)(n-1)(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{ab}:\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\boldsymbol{c}_{a})|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2n-4}\Big]f_{a}d^{3}v,

all valid for
n\geq 1
. Applying trace at (
F13
) recovers (
F14
).

Appendix G
Landau collisional operator (5-moment model)

For Coulomb collisions, a very accurate collisional operator was obtained by
Landau 1936
;
Landau 1937
in the
following form (see for example equation (1.2) in
Braginskii 1958
)

C_{ab}(f_{a},f_{b})=-\,\frac{2\pi e^{4}Z_{a}^{2}Z_{b}^{2}\ln\Lambda}{m_{a}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot\int\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{V}}}\cdot\Big[\frac{f_{a}(\boldsymbol{v})}{m_{b}}\frac{\partial f_{b}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime})}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}}-\frac{f_{b}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime})}{m_{a}}\frac{\partial f_{a}(\boldsymbol{v})}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}\Big]d^{3}v^{\prime};

(G1)

\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{V}}}=\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{|\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}|}-\frac{(\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime})(\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime})}{|\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}|^{3}}.

(G2)

With this collisional operator, equation (
A1
) is known as the Landau equation.
The Landau collisional operator is sometimes called
the Landau collisional integral because (
G1
) contains integral over
d^{3}v^{\prime}
(i.e. it is an integro-differential operator).
The operator can be rewritten
into the general Fokker-Planck form (
F1
) by introducing Rosenbluth potentials

H_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})=\int\frac{f_{b}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime})}{|\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}|}d^{3}v^{\prime};\quad\textrm{and}\quad G_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})=\int f_{b}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime})|\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}|d^{3}v^{\prime},

(G3)

yielding (see for example equations (7)-(8) of
Hinton 1983
)

\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}=2\frac{c_{ab}}{m_{a}^{2}}\big(1+\frac{m_{a}}{m_{b}}\big)\frac{\partial H_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}};\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}=2\frac{c_{ab}}{m_{a}^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}G_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}\partial\boldsymbol{v}};\qquad c_{ab}=2\pi e^{4}Z_{a}^{2}Z_{b}^{2}\ln\Lambda.

(G4)

Useful identities are

\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{V}}}=-2\frac{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}}{|\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}|^{3}}=-\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{V}}};\qquad\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}\partial\boldsymbol{v}}|\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}|=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{V}}},

(G5)

and it is easy to verify that (
F1
), (
G4
) recovers the Landau operator (
G1
)
(after one uses the Gauss-Ostrogradsky divergence theorem in velocity
d^{3}v^{\prime}
, which makes the associated integral to vanish).
By using Laplacian
\nabla^{2}_{v}=\nabla_{v}\cdot\nabla_{v}
, the following identity implies

\nabla^{2}_{v}\frac{1}{|\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}|}=-4\pi\delta(\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime});\quad=>\quad\nabla^{2}_{v}H_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})=-4\pi f_{b}(\boldsymbol{v}).

(G6)

The Rosenbluth potential
H_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})
is thus completely analogous to the electrostatic potential
\Phi(\boldsymbol{x})

(with a Poisson equation
\nabla^{2}\Phi(\boldsymbol{x})=-4\pi\rho_{c}(\boldsymbol{x})
, where
\rho_{c}(\boldsymbol{x})
is the charge spatial distribution),
here just used in velocity space. Also, because of identity
\nabla^{2}_{v}|\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}|=2/|\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}|
, the Rosenbluth potentials are related by

H_{b}=\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}_{v}G_{b};\quad=>\quad\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}=\frac{1}{2}\big(1+\frac{m_{a}}{m_{b}}\big)\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}.

(G7)

However, the structure of Rosenbluth potentials implies that the Landau operator is quite complicated. Already in the simplest example when
prescribing Maxwellian
f_{b}=n_{b}/(\pi^{3/2}v_{\textrm{th}b}^{3})\exp(-y^{2})
with the (vector) variable
\boldsymbol{y}=(\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{u}_{b})/v_{\textrm{th}b}
and scalar
y=|\boldsymbol{y}|
, yields
Rosenbluth potentials

\displaystyle H_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n_{b}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}}\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y};

(G8)

\displaystyle G_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n_{b}v_{\textrm{th}b}\Big[\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-y^{2}}+\big(\frac{1}{2y}+y\big)\textrm{erf}(y)\Big],

(G9)

where the error function
\textrm{erf}(y)=(2/\sqrt{\pi})\int_{0}^{y}e^{-z^{2}}dz
is present.
These Rosenbluth potentials make collisional contributions (
F5
), (
F6
) difficult to calculate.

For clarity on how the
H_{b}
is obtained, it is useful to introduce (vector) variable
\boldsymbol{x}=(\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{v})/v_{\textrm{th}b}
, and scalar
x=|\boldsymbol{x}|

and change the integration into
d^{3}v^{\prime}=v_{\textrm{th}b}^{3}d^{3}x
, so that

H_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})=\frac{n_{b}}{\pi^{3/2}v_{\textrm{th}b}^{3}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-\frac{|\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{u}_{b}|^{2}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}}}{|\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{v}|}d^{3}v^{\prime}=\frac{n_{b}}{\pi^{3/2}v_{\textrm{th}b}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}}{x}d^{3}x.

(G10)

In the last integral the variable
\boldsymbol{y}
is a constant (because
\boldsymbol{v}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{b}
are constants).
One introduces spherical co-ordinates in the
\boldsymbol{x}
-space with orthogonal unit vectors
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1},\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2},\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{3}
,
where the direction of vector
\boldsymbol{y}
forms axis
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{3}=\boldsymbol{y}/y
, so that the vector

\boldsymbol{x}=x\sin\theta\cos\phi\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}+x\sin\theta\sin\phi\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2}+x\cos\theta\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{3}.

(G11)

In this reference frame
\boldsymbol{y}=(0,0,y)
and so
|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}=x^{2}+y^{2}+2xy\cos\theta
. Then one
can calculate the integral in spherical co-ordinates
d^{3}x=x^{2}\sin\theta dxd\theta d\phi
, yielding

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}}{x}d^{3}x=2\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\pi}xe^{-(x^{2}+y^{2})}\sin\theta e^{-2xy\cos\theta}d\theta dx

\displaystyle=2\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}xe^{-(x^{2}+y^{2})}\frac{1}{2xy}\Big(e^{+2xy}-e^{-2xy}\Big)dx=\frac{\pi}{y}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(e^{-(x-y)^{2}}-e^{-(x+y)^{2}}\Big)dx

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{y}\Big(\int_{-y}^{\infty}e^{-z^{2}}dz-\int_{y}^{\infty}e^{-z^{2}}dz\Big)=\frac{\pi}{y}\int_{-y}^{y}e^{-z^{2}}dz=\frac{2\pi}{y}\int_{0}^{y}e^{-z^{2}}dz=\frac{\pi^{3/2}}{y}\textrm{erf}(y),

(G12)

recovering (
G8
). The result can be verified by calculating (
G6
).
Similarly, the potential
G_{b}
can be obtained by calculating

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}xe^{-|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}d^{3}x=\frac{\pi}{y}\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{2}\Big(e^{-(x-y)^{2}}-e^{-(x+y)^{2}}\Big)dx=\frac{\pi}{y}\Big(\int_{-y}^{\infty}(z+y)^{2}e^{-z^{2}}dz-\int_{y}^{\infty}(z-y)^{2}e^{-z^{2}}dz\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{y}\Big(2\int_{0}^{y}z^{2}e^{-z^{2}}dz+4y\int_{y}^{\infty}ze^{-z^{2}}dz+2y^{2}\int_{0}^{y}e^{-z^{2}}dz\Big)=\pi^{3/2}\big(y+\frac{1}{2y}\big)\textrm{erf}(y)+\pi e^{-y^{2}},

(G13)

recovering (
G9
), and which can be verified to satisfy (
G7
).

Note that because
\textrm{erf}(0)=0
, the error function can be actually defined as an indefinite integral

\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int e^{-x^{2}}dx=\textrm{erf}(x);\qquad\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int e^{-\frac{(x+a)^{2}}{b^{2}}}dx=\frac{\textrm{erf}(x+a)}{b^{2}}.

Useful relations are
\textrm{erf}(-x)=-\textrm{erf}(x)
and
\textrm{erf}(\infty)=1
. Then the calculations above can be done more elegantly, for example

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(x-y)^{2}}dx=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}\textrm{erf}(x-y)\Big|_{x=0}^{x=\infty}=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}\big(1+\textrm{erf}(y)\big).

G.1
Momentum exchange rates
\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}

To obtain the momentum exchange rates
\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}
, one needs to calculate

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{a}\int f_{a}\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}d^{3}v=2\frac{c_{ab}}{m_{a}}\big(1+\frac{m_{a}}{m_{b}}\big)\int f_{a}\frac{\partial H_{b}}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}d^{3}v;

(G14)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\frac{c_{ab}}{m_{a}}\big(1+\frac{m_{a}}{m_{b}}\big)\int H_{b}\frac{\partial f_{a}}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}d^{3}v.

Prescribing Maxwellian
f_{a}=(n_{a}/(\pi^{3/2}v_{\textrm{th}a}^{3}))\exp(-|\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|^{2}/v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2})
with general velocity
\boldsymbol{u}_{a}

leads to the “runaway” effect addressed below in Section
G.3
. It is useful to first consider simplified situation
where the differences between drift velocities
\boldsymbol{u}_{a}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{b}
are small.
The Maxwellian
f_{a}
is rewritten with
the variable
\boldsymbol{y}
and variable
\boldsymbol{u}=(\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a})/v_{\textrm{th}a}
, and expanded by assuming that
|\boldsymbol{u}|\ll 1
, so that

f_{a}=\frac{n_{a}}{\pi^{3/2}v_{\textrm{th}a}^{3}}e^{-|\boldsymbol{y}\alpha+\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}}\simeq\frac{n_{a}}{\pi^{3/2}v_{\textrm{th}a}^{3}}e^{-y^{2}\alpha^{2}}\big(1-2\alpha\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\big),

(G15)

where
\alpha=v_{\textrm{th}b}/v_{\textrm{th}a}
. Then the derivative

\frac{\partial f_{a}}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}=-\frac{2n_{a}}{\pi^{3/2}v_{\textrm{th}a}^{4}}e^{-y^{2}\alpha^{2}}\Big[\boldsymbol{u}+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}-2\alpha^{2}\boldsymbol{y}(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})\Big],

(G16)

and one needs to calculate

\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}=\frac{4c_{ab}}{m_{a}}\big(1+\frac{m_{a}}{m_{b}}\big)\frac{n_{a}n_{b}}{\pi^{3/2}v_{\textrm{th}a}^{4}v_{\textrm{th}b}}\underbrace{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y}e^{-y^{2}\alpha^{2}}\Big[\boldsymbol{u}+\alpha\boldsymbol{y}-2\alpha^{2}\boldsymbol{y}(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})\Big]d^{3}v}_{\bigcirc{}\!\!\!\!\mbox{\small 1}+\bigcirc{}\!\!\!\!\mbox{\small 2}+\bigcirc{}\!\!\!\!\mbox{\small 3}},

(G17)

where we split the integral into three parts. The integration over
d^{3}v
can be changed to
v_{\textrm{th}b}^{3}d^{3}y
.
We will use

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-y^{2}\alpha^{2}}y\,\textrm{erf}(y)dy=\frac{1}{2\alpha^{2}\sqrt{1+\alpha^{2}}};

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-y^{2}\alpha^{2}}y^{3}\,\textrm{erf}(y)dy=\frac{3\alpha^{2}+2}{4\alpha^{4}(1+\alpha^{2})^{3/2}}.

(G18)

The three integrals are then evaluated according to

\displaystyle\bigcirc{}\!\!\!\!\mbox{\small 1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y}e^{-y^{2}\alpha^{2}}d^{3}v=\boldsymbol{u}v_{\textrm{th}b}^{3}4\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}y\,\textrm{erf}(y)e^{-y^{2}\alpha^{2}}dy=\boldsymbol{u}v_{\textrm{th}b}^{3}\frac{2\pi}{\alpha^{2}\sqrt{1+\alpha^{2}}};

\displaystyle\bigcirc{}\!\!\!\!\mbox{\small 2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{y}\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y}e^{-y^{2}\alpha^{2}}d^{3}v=0;

\displaystyle\bigcirc{}\!\!\!\!\mbox{\small 3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\alpha^{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{y}(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y}e^{-y^{2}\alpha^{2}}d^{3}v=-\frac{2\alpha^{2}}{3}\boldsymbol{u}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}y\,\textrm{erf}(y)e^{-y^{2}\alpha^{2}}d^{3}v

(G19)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{8\pi}{3}\alpha^{2}v_{\textrm{th}b}^{3}\boldsymbol{u}\int_{0}^{\infty}y^{3}\,\textrm{erf}(y)e^{-y^{2}\alpha^{2}}dy=-\frac{8\pi}{3}\alpha^{2}v_{\textrm{th}b}^{3}\boldsymbol{u}\frac{3\alpha^{2}+2}{4\alpha^{4}(1+\alpha^{2})^{3/2}},

and so

\bigcirc{}\!\!\!\!\mbox{\small 1}+\bigcirc{}\!\!\!\!\mbox{\small 3}=\boldsymbol{u}v_{\textrm{th}b}^{3}\frac{2\pi}{3\alpha^{2}(1+\alpha^{2})^{3/2}}=\boldsymbol{u}\frac{2\pi}{3}\frac{v_{\textrm{th}a}^{5}v_{\textrm{th}b}}{(v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}+v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2})^{3/2}}.

(G20)

The entire result (
G17
) then can be written as (see for example equations (46)-(47) of
Hinton 1983
)

\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}=\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}(\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}),

(G21)

where the collisional frequency

\nu_{ab}=\tau^{-1}_{ab}=\frac{16}{3}\sqrt{\pi}\frac{n_{b}e^{4}Z_{a}^{2}Z_{b}^{2}\ln\Lambda}{m_{a}^{2}(v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}+v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2})^{3/2}}\Big(1+\frac{m_{a}}{m_{b}}\Big),

(G22)

and the thermal speeds
v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}=2T_{a}/m_{a}
. Note that
m_{a}n_{a}\nu_{ab}=m_{b}n_{b}\nu_{ba}
holds.
Collisional frequency (
G22
) is identical to equation (C2) of
Schunk 1977
; see equation (
179
).

It is useful to clarify the physical meaning of the collisional frequencies. Considering momentum equations for two species where
all the spatial gradients are neglected, so that
\partial\boldsymbol{u}_{a}/\partial t-(eZ_{a}/m_{a})\boldsymbol{E}=\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}/\rho_{a}
and

\partial\boldsymbol{u}_{b}/\partial t-(eZ_{b}/m_{b})\boldsymbol{E}=\boldsymbol{R}_{ba}/\rho_{b}
, subtracting them and defining difference
\delta\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}
yields an
evolution equation

\frac{\partial\delta\boldsymbol{u}}{\partial t}+\nu\delta\boldsymbol{u}=e\boldsymbol{E}\Big(\frac{Z_{b}}{m_{b}}-\frac{Z_{a}}{m_{a}}\Big);\qquad\nu=\nu_{ab}+\nu_{ba}.

(G23)

With no use of Maxwell’s equations and instead assuming an applied (external) constant electric field and also constant collisional frequencies,
an initial velocity difference
\delta\boldsymbol{u}(0)
evolves according to

\delta\boldsymbol{u}(t)=\delta\boldsymbol{u}(0)e^{-\nu t}+(1-e^{-\nu t})\frac{e\boldsymbol{E}}{\nu}\Big(\frac{Z_{b}}{m_{b}}-\frac{Z_{a}}{m_{a}}\Big).

(G24)

Approximately after time
\tau=1/\nu
(which represents many small-angle collisions) the dependence on the initial
condition disappears and the difference between velocities reaches a constant value

\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}=\frac{e\boldsymbol{E}}{\nu_{ab}+\nu_{ba}}\Big(\frac{Z_{b}}{m_{b}}-\frac{Z_{a}}{m_{a}}\Big)=\textrm{const.}

(G25)

Provided that
Z_{a}/m_{a}\neq Z_{b}/m_{b}
, the collisional time
\tau=1/(\nu_{ab}+\nu_{ba})
then can be interpreted as an average time that
is required for particles “a” and “b” to experience (many small-angle) collisions, so that the difference between their average fluid velocities
reaches a constant value proportional to the value of the applied (external) electric field
\boldsymbol{E}
. For the particular case of

Z_{a}/m_{a}=Z_{b}/m_{b}
, the velocities become equal regardless of the value of applied
\boldsymbol{E}
.

For a particular case of a one ion-electron plasma
\boldsymbol{u}_{e}-\boldsymbol{u}_{i}=-e\boldsymbol{E}/(\nu_{ei}m_{e})
, which can be also directly obtained from the quasi-static electron
or ion momentum equations. Prescribing charge neutrality
n_{e}=Z_{i}n_{i}
so that the current
\boldsymbol{j}=-en_{e}(\boldsymbol{u}_{e}-\boldsymbol{u}_{i})
then yields
relation
\boldsymbol{j}=\sigma\boldsymbol{E}
with the usual electrical conductivity
\sigma=1/\eta=e^{2}n_{e}/(\nu_{ei}m_{e})
, where
\sigma
does not depend on the value
of current
j
(because
j
is assumed to be small).

G.2
Energy exchange rates
Q_{ab}

Similar calculations are used to obtain the energy exchange rates
Q_{ab}
, according to (
F8
).
It is beneficial to notice that
\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}=(4c_{ab}/m_{a}^{2})H_{b}
and so

Q_{ab}=\frac{2c_{ab}}{m_{a}}\big(1+\frac{m_{a}}{m_{b}}\big)\int f_{a}\frac{\partial H_{b}}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot\boldsymbol{c}_{a}d^{3}v+\frac{2c_{ab}}{m_{a}}\int f_{a}H_{b}d^{3}v;

(G26)

\frac{\partial H_{b}}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}=\frac{n_{b}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}\boldsymbol{y}\Big(\frac{1}{y^{2}}\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-y^{2}}-\frac{1}{y^{3}}\textrm{erf}(y)\Big),

(G27)

and because
\boldsymbol{c}_{a}=\boldsymbol{y}v_{\textrm{th}b}+\boldsymbol{u}v_{\textrm{th}a}
then

\frac{\partial H_{b}}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot\boldsymbol{c}_{a}=\frac{n_{b}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}\Big(y^{2}v_{\textrm{th}b}+(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{y})v_{\textrm{th}a}\Big)\Big(\frac{1}{y^{2}}\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-y^{2}}-\frac{1}{y^{3}}\textrm{erf}(y)\Big).

(G28)

Importantly, to correctly account for
|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}
contributions, the
f_{a}
has to be expanded further

f_{a}=\frac{n_{a}}{\pi^{3/2}v_{\textrm{th}a}^{3}}e^{-|\boldsymbol{y}\alpha+\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}}\simeq\frac{n_{a}}{\pi^{3/2}v_{\textrm{th}a}^{3}}e^{-y^{2}\alpha^{2}}\Big(1-(2\alpha\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}+|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2})+2\alpha^{2}(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})^{2}\Big),

(G29)

where
\alpha=v_{\textrm{th}b}/v_{\textrm{th}a}
. This distribution function yields

\displaystyle\int f_{a}H_{b}d^{3}v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2n_{a}n_{b}}{\sqrt{\pi}v_{\textrm{th}a}}\Big[\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\alpha^{2}}}-\frac{|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}}{3(1+\alpha^{2})^{3/2}}\Big];

\displaystyle\int f_{a}\frac{\partial H_{b}}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot\boldsymbol{c}_{a}d^{3}v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2n_{a}n_{b}}{\sqrt{\pi}v_{\textrm{th}a}}\Big[-\frac{1}{(1+\alpha^{2})^{3/2}}+\frac{|\boldsymbol{u}^{2}|}{(1+\alpha^{2})^{5/2}}\Big],

(G30)

and the final result reads

Q_{ab}=3\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}\frac{T_{b}-T_{a}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}+\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}\frac{3}{2}\Big(\frac{m_{b}T_{a}}{m_{b}T_{a}+m_{a}T_{b}}-\frac{1}{3}\frac{m_{b}}{m_{b}+m_{a}}\Big)|\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|^{2},

(G31)

or equivalently

Q_{ab}=3\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}\frac{T_{b}-T_{a}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}+\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}\frac{m_{b}(3T_{a}m_{a}+2T_{a}m_{b}-T_{b}m_{a})}{2(T_{b}m_{a}+T_{a}m_{b})(m_{b}+m_{a})}|\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|^{2}.

(G32)

Hinton 1983
calculates only the first term, the thermal exchange rate (his equation (52); see also
Landau 1936
for an ion-electron plasma).
Calculating
Q_{ab}+Q_{ba}=\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}|\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|^{2}=(\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\cdot\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}
yields energy conservation and the result
(
G31
) is well-defined. (Re-calculating
\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}
with the further expanded
f_{0}
(
G29
) yields unchanged
result
\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}=\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}(\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a})
).
As a double check, expanding the more general expression for unrestricted drifts (
G64
) (by expansion
\Psi_{ab}=1-\epsilon^{2}
)
yields

Q_{ab}=3\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}\frac{T_{b}-T_{a}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}\Big(1-\frac{|\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|^{2}}{\frac{2T_{a}}{m_{a}}+\frac{2T_{b}}{m_{b}}}\Big)+\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}\frac{m_{b}}{m_{b}+m_{a}}|\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|^{2}.

(G33)

Results (
G33
) and (
G31
) are equivalent, and valid for an unrestricted difference in temperature.
After prescribing that the difference in temperatures is small simplifies the frictional part into

Q_{ab}=3\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}\frac{T_{b}-T_{a}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}+\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}\frac{m_{b}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}|\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|^{2}.

(G34)

This frictional part is derived elegantly in the Appendix of
Braginskii 1965
.

G.3
R_{ab}
and
Q_{ab}
for unrestricted drifts
\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}
(runaway effect)

Here we want to calculate
\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}
for a general Maxwellian distributions
f_{a},f_{b}
, with no restriction
for the value of difference
\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}
. We follow
Burgers 1969
and
Tanenbaum 1967
.
Instead of using the Rosenbluth potential
H_{b}
and calculating (
G14
), it is easier to consider

\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}=2\frac{c_{ab}}{m_{a}}\big(1+\frac{m_{a}}{m_{b}}\big)\int\int f_{a}(\boldsymbol{v})f_{b}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime})\frac{\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{v}}{|\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{v}|^{3}}d^{3}vd^{3}v^{\prime}.

(G35)

Additionally, instead of
\boldsymbol{v}
and
\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}
, it feels more natural to use
\boldsymbol{v}_{a}=\boldsymbol{v}
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{b}=\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}
.
It is useful to introduce vectors
\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{v}_{b}-\boldsymbol{v}_{a}
and
\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}
. The integral is then calculated by introducing “center-of-mass” velocity

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}=\frac{m_{a}\boldsymbol{v}_{a}+m_{b}\boldsymbol{v}_{b}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}-\frac{m_{a}\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+m_{b}\boldsymbol{u}_{b}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}+\frac{m_{a}m_{b}}{(m_{a}+m_{b})}\,\frac{T_{b}-T_{a}}{(m_{b}T_{a}+m_{a}T_{b})}(\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{x}),

(G36)

which transforms

f_{a}f_{b}=\frac{n_{a}n_{b}}{\pi^{3}v_{\textrm{th}a}^{3}v_{\textrm{th}b}^{3}}\exp\Big(-\frac{|\boldsymbol{v}_{a}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|^{2}}{v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}}-\frac{|\boldsymbol{v}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{b}|^{2}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}\Big),

(G37)

into

\displaystyle f_{a}f_{b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n_{a}n_{b}}{\pi^{3}\tilde{\alpha}^{3}\beta^{3}}\exp\Big(-\frac{|\boldsymbol{C}|^{2}}{\tilde{\alpha}^{2}}-\frac{|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\Big),

(G38)

with new thermal speeds

\tilde{\alpha}^{2}=\frac{2T_{a}T_{b}}{m_{b}T_{a}+m_{a}T_{b}};\qquad\beta^{2}=v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}+v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}.

(G39)

Importantly,
d^{3}v_{a}d^{3}v_{b}=d^{3}Cd^{3}x
(by calculating Jacobian).
For later calculations of more complicated integrals than (
G35
), useful transformations are

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{c}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}-\frac{m_{b}T_{a}}{m_{b}T_{a}+m_{a}T_{b}}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{u});

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{c}_{b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}+\frac{m_{a}T_{b}}{m_{b}T_{a}+m_{a}T_{b}}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{u}).

(G40)

The integral (
G35
) thus transforms into

\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}=2\frac{c_{ab}}{m_{a}}\big(1+\frac{m_{a}}{m_{b}}\big)\frac{n_{a}n_{b}}{\pi^{3/2}\beta^{3}}\int\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{x^{3}}e^{-\frac{|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}}{\beta^{2}}}d^{3}x,

(G41)

where we have already integrated over
d^{3}C
. One introduces reference frame in the
\boldsymbol{x}
-space with
unit vectors
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1},\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2},\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{3}
,
where the direction of vector

\boldsymbol{u}
defines the axis
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{3}=\boldsymbol{u}/u
, so that

\boldsymbol{x}=x\sin\theta\cos\phi\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}+x\sin\theta\sin\phi\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2}+x\cos\theta\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{3}.

(G42)

For example, integration of (
G42
) over
\phi
yields
\int_{0}^{2\pi}\boldsymbol{x}d\phi=2\pi x\cos\theta\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{3}
,
i.e. the result is in the direction of
\boldsymbol{u}
. Furthermore, because
|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}=x^{2}+u^{2}-2xu\cos\theta
, the integration of (
G41
)
over
\phi
can be carried out, yielding

\displaystyle\int\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{x^{3}}e^{-\frac{|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}}{\beta^{2}}}d^{3}x=\frac{\boldsymbol{u}}{u}2\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\pi}e^{-\frac{|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}}{\beta^{2}}}\cos\theta\sin\theta dxd\theta.

(G43)

To calculate that integral, it is useful to introduce (constant)
\epsilon=u/\beta
, and change the integration into variables

z=\frac{x}{\beta}-s;\qquad s=\epsilon\cos\theta,

(G44)

so that
|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}/\beta^{2}=z^{2}-s^{2}+\epsilon^{2}
, yielding

\displaystyle\int\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{x^{3}}e^{-\frac{|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}}{\beta^{2}}}d^{3}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}2\pi\frac{e^{-\epsilon^{2}}}{\epsilon^{3}}\int_{-s}^{\infty}\int_{-\epsilon}^{\epsilon}se^{-z^{2}+s^{2}}dzds

(G45)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}\pi^{3/2}\Big(\frac{\textrm{erf}(\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{3}}-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-\epsilon^{2}}}{\epsilon^{2}}\Big).

In the last integral, it is necessary to first integrate over
dz
and then over
ds
, by using

\displaystyle\int_{-s}^{\infty}e^{-z^{2}}dz

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}\big(1+\textrm{erf}(s)\big);

\displaystyle\int_{-\epsilon}^{\epsilon}se^{s^{2}}\textrm{erf}(s)ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\epsilon^{2}}\textrm{erf}(\epsilon)-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\epsilon.

(G46)

The final result then reads

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}(\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\Phi_{ab};

(G47)

\displaystyle\Phi_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{3}{4}\sqrt{\pi}\frac{\textrm{erf}(\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{3}}-\frac{3}{2}\frac{e^{-\epsilon^{2}}}{\epsilon^{2}}\Big);\qquad\epsilon=\frac{|\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|}{\sqrt{v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}+v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}},

(G48)

recovering equation (26.4) of
Burgers 1969
and equation (25b) of
Schunk 1977
.
For small values
\epsilon\to 0
, the contribution
\Phi\to 1
(more precisely
\Phi_{ab}=1-(3/5)\epsilon^{2}
), recovering the previous
result (
G21
) with small drifts. However, for large values
\epsilon\gg 1
, the contribution
\Phi_{ab}
decreases to zero
as
\Phi_{ab}=3\sqrt{\pi}/(4\epsilon^{3})
and thus for large differences in drifts
|\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|
,
momentum exchange rates
\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}
disappear for Coulomb collisions. The phenomenon is known as
the “runaway effect”
(
Dreicer 1959
)
. It is also possible to write

\Phi_{ab}=\frac{3\sqrt{\pi}}{2\epsilon}\widetilde{G}_{ab}(\epsilon);\quad\textrm{where}\quad\widetilde{G}_{ab}(\epsilon)=\frac{\textrm{erf}(\epsilon)}{2\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{e^{-\epsilon^{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}\epsilon}=\frac{\textrm{erf}(\epsilon)-\epsilon\textrm{erf}\,^{\prime}(\epsilon)}{2\epsilon^{2}},

(G49)

where
\widetilde{G}_{ab}(\epsilon)
is called the Chandrasekhar function (we use tilde to differentiate it from the
Rosenbluth potential
G_{b}
), and (
G47
) then becomes

\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}=\frac{3}{2}\sqrt{\pi}\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}(v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}+v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}}{|\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|}\widetilde{G}_{ab}(\epsilon).

(G50)

In plasma books (e.g.
Helander & Sigmar 2002
), the Chandrasekhar function is typically introduced in velocity space as

\widetilde{G}(v/v_{\textrm{th}b})
, i.e. without drifts and before integration over
d^{3}v
.
The runaway effect is then explained on a population of electron species, which gets accelerated
by applied external electric field. Because for large
velocities
v
frictional forces (collisions) decrease as
\widetilde{G}\sim v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}/(2v^{2})
,
the tail of the distribution function might depart and run away. In this sense, the runaway effect could be viewed as
a purely kinetic effect. Nevertheless, obviously analogous runaway effect exists in a fluid
description (i.e. after integration over
d^{3}v
), it is just represented through difference in drifts
\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}

(which form a current
\boldsymbol{j}
). For example, considering a one ion-electron plasma with an electric current
\boldsymbol{j}=-en_{e}(\boldsymbol{u}_{e}-\boldsymbol{u}_{i})
,
taking the electron momentum equation and neglecting for simplicity all the terms except of the external
\boldsymbol{E}
and
\boldsymbol{R}_{ei}

(including
\partial\boldsymbol{u}_{e}/\partial t
which neglects acceleration) yields a relation

\boldsymbol{E}=\frac{\boldsymbol{R}_{ei}}{en_{e}}=\eta\boldsymbol{j};\qquad\eta=\frac{1}{\sigma}=\frac{\rho_{e}\nu_{ei}}{e^{2}n_{e}^{2}}\Phi_{ei},

(G51)

which agrees with equation (33.6) of
Burgers 1969
.
The electrical resistivity
\eta
now contains
\Phi_{ei}
given by (
G48
) with
\epsilon=j/(en_{e}v_{\textrm{th}e})
.
For small values of current
j
, the
\eta
is independent of
j
.
The runaway effect means that with increasing current
j
the electrical resistivity
\eta
decreases, and for large current
j
it becomes

\eta=(3\sqrt{\pi}/4)en_{e}\rho_{e}\nu_{ei}v_{\textrm{th}e}^{3}/j^{3}
. In reality the problem is much more complex when the acceleration
is considered, because subtracting two momentum equations, a general difference in velocities
\delta\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}
now
evolves according to a non-linear differential equation

\frac{\partial\delta\boldsymbol{u}}{\partial t}+\nu\Phi_{ab}(\epsilon)\delta\boldsymbol{u}=e\boldsymbol{E}\Big(\frac{Z_{b}}{m_{b}}-\frac{Z_{a}}{m_{a}}\Big);\qquad\nu=\nu_{ab}+\nu_{ba},

(G52)

which does not seem to be solvable analytically. Nevertheless (after studying the solutions for some time), it is possible to
conclude that there exist two distinct classes of solutions, that are typically separated by the value of applied
constant electric field
E
with respect to a critical value
E_{\textrm{crit}}
, where the maximal frictional forces balance the electric forces.
For
E<E_{\textrm{crit}}
the solutions converge in time towards
a situation where
\Phi_{ab}=1
, and one recovers
evolution equation (
G23
) with static solution (
G25
). In contrast, for
E>E_{\textrm{crit}}
, the solutions evolve in time
towards a situation with
\Phi_{ab}=0
, which can be shown for example by considering solutions where
\Phi_{ab}(\epsilon)
is approximated with
its asymptotic expansion. For very large values of
E
one can
straightforwardly prescribe
\Phi_{ab}=0
, yielding a (collisionless) solution

\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}=e\boldsymbol{E}\Big(\frac{Z_{b}}{m_{b}}-\frac{Z_{a}}{m_{a}}\Big)\,t.

(G53)

Thus, provided that
Z_{a}/m_{a}\neq Z_{b}/m_{b}
is true, a stationary solution does not
exist and the difference in velocities grows in time without bounds, before beam/stream plasma instabilities with associated development
of turbulence (and in extreme cases eventually relativistic effects) restrict its further growth.
For the particular case
Z_{a}/m_{a}=Z_{b}/m_{b}
the runaway
effect does not exist, and difference in velocities will converge to zero according to (
G52
).
The frictional forces
\epsilon\Phi_{ab}(\epsilon)
are plotted as a red curve in the right panel of Figure
6
.
They reach its maximum value
[\epsilon\Phi_{ab}(\epsilon)]_{\textrm{max}}=0.57
at
\epsilon=0.97
(often rounded as
\epsilon=1
).
The critical electric field
\boldsymbol{E}_{\textrm{crit}}
is determined by making the maximum frictional forces equal
to the electric forces, so that (
G52
) becomes
\partial\delta\boldsymbol{u}/\partial t=0
, yielding

\boldsymbol{E}_{\textrm{crit}}=\underbrace{[\epsilon\Phi_{ab}(\epsilon)]_{\textrm{max}}}_{0.57}\sqrt{v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}+v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}\,\frac{(\nu_{ab}+\nu_{ba})}{e}\frac{m_{a}m_{b}}{(Z_{b}m_{a}-Z_{a}m_{b})}\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{u}}{|\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|}.

(G54)

Alternatively, one might use the Chandrasekhar function where
[\epsilon\Phi_{ab}]_{\textrm{max}}=(3/2)\sqrt{\pi}[\widetilde{G}_{ab}]_{\textrm{max}}
,
and
[\widetilde{G}_{ab}]_{\textrm{max}}=0.214
.
The runaway effect thus exist for

\displaystyle E>E_{\textrm{crit}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underbrace{[\widetilde{G}_{ab}(\epsilon)]_{\textrm{max}}}_{0.214}\hat{E}_{D};

(G55)

\displaystyle\hat{E}_{D}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3\sqrt{\pi}}{2}\sqrt{v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}+v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}\frac{(\nu_{ab}+\nu_{ba})}{e}\frac{m_{a}m_{b}}{|Z_{b}m_{a}-Z_{a}m_{b}|},

(G56)

where
\hat{E}_{D}
can be viewed as a generalized Dreicer electric field for two species with arbitrary masses, charges and temperatures.
By further substituting for the collisional frequencies (we take
\ln\lambda
to be constant)

\hat{E}_{D}=8\pi\frac{(m_{a}+m_{b})}{m_{a}m_{b}}\frac{(\rho_{a}+\rho_{b})}{|Z_{b}m_{a}-Z_{a}m_{b}|}\frac{e^{3}Z_{a}^{2}Z_{b}^{2}\ln\lambda}{(v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}+v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2})},

(G57)

which for an ion-electron plasma yields the usual Dreicer electric field

E_{D}=\frac{4\pi n_{i}e^{3}Z_{i}^{2}\ln\lambda}{T_{e}}.

(G58)

In the paper of
Dreicer 1959
his reference field is defined as
E_{c}=E_{D}/2
, so in his notation the runaway effect exists for
E>0.43E_{c}
instead
of
E>0.214E_{D}
. In the most of recent literature definition (
G58
) is used. It is sometimes incorrectly stated that the runaway effect
exists for
E
exceeding
E_{D}
, whereas the correct value as calculated by Dreicer is almost 5 times smaller.
Note the dependence of (
G58
) on
T_{e}
, meaning that for
any given value of electric field the runaway effect will appear if the temperatures are sufficiently high.
For
Z_{a}/m_{a}=Z_{b}/m_{b}
the
\hat{E}_{D}
becomes infinitely large and the runaway effect between these species is not present.
For an ion-electron plasma the Dreicer electric field is also discussed for example by
Tanenbaum 1967
(p. 258) and
Balescu 1988
(p. 775).
We found it useful to consider the situation for two arbitrary (charged) species.

Similarly to
\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}
, the
Q_{ab}
is obtained by calculating two integrals in (
G26
), and the first integral yields

\displaystyle\int f_{a}\frac{\partial H_{b}}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}_{a}}\cdot\boldsymbol{c}_{a}d^{3}v_{a}=\int\int f_{a}f_{b}\frac{\boldsymbol{v}_{b}-\boldsymbol{v}_{a}}{|\boldsymbol{v}_{b}-\boldsymbol{v}_{a}|^{3}}\cdot\boldsymbol{c}_{a}d^{3}v_{a}d^{3}v_{b}

\displaystyle=-\,\frac{n_{a}n_{b}}{\pi^{3/2}\beta^{3}}\frac{m_{b}T_{a}}{m_{b}T_{a}+m_{a}T_{b}}\int\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{x^{3}}\cdot(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{u})e^{-\frac{|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}}{\beta^{2}}}d^{3}x

\displaystyle=-\,\frac{2n_{a}n_{b}}{\sqrt{\pi}\beta^{3}}v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}e^{-\epsilon^{2}},

(G59)

where we have used

\displaystyle\int\frac{1}{x}e^{-\frac{|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}}{\beta^{2}}}d^{3}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi^{3/2}\beta^{2}\frac{\textrm{erf}(\epsilon)}{\epsilon};

(G60)

\displaystyle\int\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{x}\cdot(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{u})e^{-\frac{|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}}{\beta^{2}}}d^{3}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi\beta^{2}e^{-\epsilon^{2}}.

(G61)

The second integral in (
G26
) yields

\displaystyle\int f_{a}H_{b}d^{3}v_{a}=\int\int f_{a}f_{b}\frac{1}{|\boldsymbol{v}_{b}-\boldsymbol{v}_{a}|}d^{3}v_{a}d^{3}v_{b}=\frac{n_{a}n_{b}}{\beta}\frac{\textrm{erf}(\epsilon)}{\epsilon}=\frac{n_{a}n_{b}}{\beta^{3}}\frac{\textrm{erf}(\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{3}}|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}.

(G62)

The entire equation (
G26
) then becomes

Q_{ab}=\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}\Big[-\frac{3T_{a}}{m_{a}}e^{-\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{3}{4}\sqrt{\pi}\frac{\textrm{erf}(\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{3}}\frac{m_{b}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}|\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|^{2}\Big],

(G63)

and the difference in temperatures
T_{b}-T_{a}
is not directly visible. Nevertheless, the solution can be rewritten into

\displaystyle Q_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}\Big[3\frac{T_{b}-T_{a}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}\Psi_{ab}+\frac{m_{b}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}|\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|^{2}\Phi_{ab}\Big];

(G64)

\displaystyle\Psi_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-\epsilon^{2}};\qquad\Phi_{ab}=\Big(\frac{3}{4}\sqrt{\pi}\frac{\textrm{erf}(\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{3}}-\frac{3}{2}\frac{e^{-\epsilon^{2}}}{\epsilon^{2}}\Big);\qquad\epsilon=\frac{|\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|}{\sqrt{v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}+v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}},

recovering equation (26.8) of
Burgers 1969
and equation (25c) of
Schunk 1977
. Similarly to
R_{ab}
, for large differences in drifts, the
Q_{ab}
disappears.

It is of interest to explore the validity of results with small drifts, obtained in Sections
G.1
and
G.2
.
The functions
\Phi_{ab}
and
\Psi_{ab}
are plotted in the left panel of Figure
6
. Both functions are decreasing,
and thus in fluid models with the small drift approximation the effects of collisions are overestimated.
We fix the temperature (so that
\nu_{ab}=\textrm{const.}
), and in the right panel of Figure
6
we
plot function
\epsilon\Phi_{ab}
which corresponds to
R_{ab}
(red line), and function
\epsilon^{2}\Phi_{ab}
which corresponds to
Q_{ab}
(blue line).
For large drifts
\epsilon\gg 1
functions
\epsilon\Phi_{ab}\sim 3\sqrt{\pi}/(4\epsilon^{2})
and

\epsilon^{2}\Phi_{ab}\sim 3\sqrt{\pi}/(4\epsilon)
.

Figure 6:
Left panel: functions
\Phi_{ab}
(red line) and
\Psi_{ab}
(blue line), with respect to
\epsilon
defined in equation (
G64
).
Right panel: functions
\epsilon\Phi_{ab}\sim R_{ab}
(red line) and
\epsilon^{2}\Phi_{ab}\sim Q_{ab}
(blue line), where temperature is fixed.
Corresponding approximations for small drifts with
\Phi_{ab}=1
are also plotted (dotted lines).
Function
\epsilon\Phi_{ab}
reaches maximum 0.57 at
\epsilon=0.97
, and function
\epsilon^{2}\Phi_{ab}
reaches maximum 0.70 at
\epsilon=1.51
.
It is possible to conclude that the small drift approximation is reasonably accurate up to
\epsilon=0.5
, and
that very small values
\epsilon\ll 1
are actually not required. Even though we did not calculate the runaway effect for higher order moments,
out of curiosity we include a function
\epsilon^{3}\Phi_{ab}
(black dashed line), which does not decrease to zero for large drifts but instead
converges to a constant value 1.33.

G.4
Difficulties with Rosenbluth potentials

It is interesting to analyze, why it seems impossible to calculate the run-away effect for
\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}

through the Rosenbluth potentials, and why one needs to use the “center-of-mass” transformation instead.
An attempt to calculate the run-away effect yields

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{a}\int f_{a}\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}d^{3}v

(G65)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4\frac{c_{ab}}{m_{a}}\big(1+\frac{m_{a}}{m_{b}}\big)\frac{n_{a}n_{b}v_{\textrm{th}b}}{\pi^{3/2}v_{\textrm{th}a}^{3}}\int e^{-|\alpha\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}}\frac{\boldsymbol{y}}{y}\underbrace{\Big(\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{2y^{2}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{y}\Big)}_{\widetilde{G}_{ab}(y)}d^{3}y,

where
\alpha=v_{\textrm{th}b}/v_{\textrm{th}a}
and
\boldsymbol{u}=(\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a})/v_{\textrm{th}a}
, and we have
also identified the Chandrasekhar function.
First integrating over
d\phi
where the direction of
\boldsymbol{u}
forms the axis
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{3}=\boldsymbol{u}/u
yields

\displaystyle\int e^{-|\alpha\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}}\frac{\boldsymbol{y}}{y}\Big(\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{2y^{2}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{y}\Big)d^{3}y

\displaystyle=2\pi\frac{\boldsymbol{u}}{u}e^{-u^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\pi}e^{-\alpha^{2}y^{2}}e^{-2\alpha yu\cos\theta}\cos\theta\sin\theta\Big(\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{2}-\frac{y}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-y^{2}}\Big)dyd\theta.

(G66)

Then one can perform integration over
d\theta
, however, subsequent integration over
dy
does not seem possible.
Or by attempting first integration over
dy
, by using substitutions
s=u\cos\theta
;
z=\alpha y+s
; so that
|\alpha\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}=z^{2}-s^{2}+u^{2}
yields

(\ref{eq:Cudo})=2\pi\frac{\boldsymbol{u}}{u^{3}}e^{-u^{2}}\int_{s}^{\infty}\int_{-u}^{u}se^{+s^{2}}e^{-z^{2}}\Big[\frac{1}{2}\textrm{erf}\big(\frac{z-s}{\alpha}\big)-\frac{z-s}{\alpha\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-\frac{(z-s)^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}}\Big]dzds,

(G67)

and the 1D integrals over
dz
again appear impossible to calculate. The problem is the “drift” “s”, and also constants
\alpha
.
For example, the following indefinite integral is easily calculated by parts

\int e^{-(az+b)^{2}}\textrm{erf}(az+b)dz=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{4a}\textrm{erf}^{2}(az+b),

(G68)

but the result is not useful. Obviously, a different approach has to be used
to integrate over the Chandrasekhar function if
f_{a}^{(0)}
is a Maxwellian with unrestricted drifts.

Importantly, from Section
G.3
where the “center-of-mass” transformation is used,
we know that the correct answer has to be

\int f_{a}^{(0)}\frac{\boldsymbol{y}}{y}\widetilde{G}_{ab}(y)d^{3}v\overset{!}{=}-n_{a}\frac{v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}{v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}+v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}}{|\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|}\widetilde{G}_{ab}(\epsilon),

(G69)

where
\boldsymbol{y}=(\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{u}_{b})/v_{\textrm{th}b}
;
\epsilon=|\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|/\sqrt{v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}+v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}
;
d^{3}v=v_{\textrm{th}b}^{3}d^{3}y
;
or written in a full form

\displaystyle\frac{n_{a}}{\pi^{3/2}v_{\textrm{th}a}^{3}}\int e^{-\frac{|\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|^{2}}{v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}}}\frac{\boldsymbol{y}}{y}\Big(\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{2y^{2}}-\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}y}\Big)d^{3}v

\displaystyle\overset{!}{=}-n_{a}\frac{v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}{v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}+v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}}{|\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|}\Big(\frac{\textrm{erf}(\epsilon)}{2\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{e^{-\epsilon^{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}\epsilon}\Big).

(G70)

Finally, written in perhaps the prettiest form when not referring to any physical quantities
(i.e. a form suitable for integral tables)

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-|\alpha\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}}\frac{\boldsymbol{y}}{y}\Big(\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{2y^{2}}-\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}y}\Big)d^{3}y

\displaystyle\overset{!}{=}-\,\frac{\pi^{3/2}}{\alpha(1+\alpha^{2})}\frac{\boldsymbol{u}}{u}\Big(\frac{\textrm{erf}(\epsilon)}{2\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{e^{-\epsilon^{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}\epsilon}\Big);\quad\textrm{where}\quad\epsilon=\frac{u}{\sqrt{1+\alpha^{2}}};\quad\alpha>0.

(G71)

It is remarkable that the integral has such a striking symmetry, even though the integral seems impossible
to calculate directly,
i.e. the integral “transfers” a Chandrasekar function in y-variable to a
Chandrasekar function in
\epsilon
-variable. The result seems well-defined
even for
\alpha<0
, so the restriction is
\alpha\neq 0
and real (the integral is divergent for
\alpha=0
). Limit
u\to 0
yields zero.
The “proof” of (
G71
) can be viewed as analogous when evaluating the
1D Gaussian integral
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-x^{2}}dx
through
\int\int e^{-(x^{2}+y^{2})}dxdy
in polar co-ordinates,
where here instead of integrating over
d^{3}v
, a trick is used to integrate over
d^{3}vd^{3}v^{\prime}
.

Appendix H
8-moment model (heat flux and thermal force)

To obtain collisional contributions with the heat flux, one uses the following 8-moment distribution function of Grad

f_{b}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime})=\frac{n_{b}}{\pi^{3/2}v_{\textrm{th}b}^{3}}e^{-\frac{|\boldsymbol{c}_{b}|^{2}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}}\Big[1-\frac{m_{b}}{T_{b}p_{b}}\Big(1-\frac{m_{b}|\boldsymbol{c}_{b}|^{2}}{5T_{b}}\Big)\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{c}_{b}\Big].

(H1)

Calculations done by
Burgers 1969
;
Schunk 1977
;
Killie et al. 2004
were performed by using the “center-of-mass” transformation described in Section
G.3
.
Here, to do something slightly different, we verify the calculations by using the Rosenbluth potentials.
The route through Rosenbluth potentials has a great dis-advantage, that error functions are encountered even if we are interested
only in expressions with small drift velocities (with respect to thermal velocities). This is because the Rosenbluth
potentials have to be derived with exact (
H1
), and not expanded for small drifts from the beginning.
Nevertheless, the route has an advantage that it is possible to do a double-check in the middle of calculations, because there are identities that
the Rosenbluth potentials must satisfy.

H.1
Rosenbluth potentials

By using the same variables
\boldsymbol{x}=(\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{v})/v_{\textrm{th}b}
and
\boldsymbol{y}=(\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{u}_{b})/v_{\textrm{th}b}
as before, so that

\boldsymbol{c}_{b}=(\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y})v_{\textrm{th}b}
, we need to obtain Rosenbluth potentials

\displaystyle H_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{f_{b}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime})}{|\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{v}|}d^{3}v^{\prime}

(H2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n_{b}}{\pi^{3/2}v_{\textrm{th}b}}\int\frac{e^{-|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}}{x}\Big[1-\frac{m_{b}v_{\textrm{th}b}}{T_{b}p_{b}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\cdot(\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y})\Big(1-\frac{2}{5}|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}\Big)\Big]d^{3}x;

\displaystyle G_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int|\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{v}|f_{b}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime})d^{3}v^{\prime}

(H3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n_{b}v_{\textrm{th}b}}{\pi^{3/2}}\int xe^{-|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}\Big[1-\frac{m_{b}v_{\textrm{th}b}}{T_{b}p_{b}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\cdot(\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y})\Big(1-\frac{2}{5}|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}\Big)\Big]d^{3}x.

It is possible to calculate the following integrals (directly obtainable with Maple in spherical geometry,
after the vector integrals containing
\boldsymbol{x}
are first integrated by hand over
d\phi
)

\displaystyle\int\frac{1}{x}e^{-|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}d^{3}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi^{3/2}\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y};

(H4)

\displaystyle\int\frac{1}{x}e^{-|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}\big(1-\frac{2}{5}|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}\big)d^{3}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{5}\pi^{3/2}\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y}+\frac{2}{5}\pi e^{-y^{2}};

(H5)

\displaystyle\int\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{x}e^{-|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}\big(1-\frac{2}{5}|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}\big)d^{3}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{5}\pi^{3/2}\boldsymbol{y}\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y},

(H6)

and similarly

\displaystyle\int xe^{-|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}d^{3}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi^{3/2}\Big(y+\frac{1}{2y}\Big)\textrm{erf}(y)+\pi e^{-y^{2}};

(H7)

\displaystyle\int xe^{-|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}\big(1-\frac{2}{5}|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}\big)d^{3}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{5}\pi^{3/2}y\textrm{erf}(y)+\frac{2}{5}\pi e^{-y^{2}};

(H8)

\displaystyle\int x\boldsymbol{x}e^{-|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}\big(1-\frac{2}{5}|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}\big)d^{3}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{5}\boldsymbol{y}\Big[\pi^{3/2}\Big(y+\frac{1}{4y^{3}}\Big)\textrm{erf}(y)+\pi\Big(1-\frac{1}{2y^{2}}\Big)e^{-y^{2}}\Big].

(H9)

This yields the final Rosenbluth potentials for the 8-moment model, in the following form

\displaystyle H_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n_{b}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}}\Big[\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y}-\frac{2}{5}\frac{m_{b}v_{\textrm{th}b}}{T_{b}p_{b}}(\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{y})\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-y^{2}}\Big];

(H10)

\displaystyle G_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n_{b}v_{\textrm{th}b}\Big[\Big(y+\frac{1}{2y}\Big)\textrm{erf}(y)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-y^{2}}\Big]

(H11)

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{5}\frac{n_{b}}{p_{b}}(\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{y})\Big[\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{2y^{3}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{y^{2}}\Big].

We will need a vector

\displaystyle\frac{\partial H_{b}}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}

\displaystyle=
