\displaystyle\frac{n_{b}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}\Big[\boldsymbol{y}\Big(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{y^{2}}-\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y^{3}}\Big)-\frac{2}{5}\frac{m_{b}v_{\textrm{th}b}}{T_{b}p_{b}}\Big(\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}-2\boldsymbol{y}(\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{y})\Big)\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-y^{2}}\Big],

(H12)

and a matrix

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}G_{b}}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}\partial\boldsymbol{v}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n_{b}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}}\Big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}-\frac{\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}}{y^{2}}\Big)\Big[\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{y^{2}}+\big(\frac{1}{y}-\frac{1}{2y^{3}}\big)\textrm{erf}(y)\Big]+\frac{n_{b}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}}\frac{\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}}{y^{2}}\Big[\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y^{3}}-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{y^{2}}\Big]

(H13)

\displaystyle+\frac{n_{b}m_{b}}{5T_{b}p_{b}}\Big\{\Big[\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}+(\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{y})\Big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}-\frac{\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}}{y^{2}}\Big)\Big]\Big[-\frac{3}{2}\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y^{5}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\big(\frac{2}{y^{2}}+\frac{3}{y^{4}}\big)e^{-y^{2}}\Big]

\displaystyle+(\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{y})\frac{\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}}{y^{2}}\Big[\frac{6}{y^{5}}\textrm{erf}(y)-\frac{4}{\sqrt{\pi}}\big(1+\frac{2}{y^{2}}+\frac{3}{y^{4}}\big)e^{-y^{2}}\Big]\Big\}.

As a double check, applying
(\partial/\partial\boldsymbol{v})\cdot
on (
H12
) recovers
-4\pi f_{b}
, and applying

(1/2)\textrm{Tr}
on (
H13
) recovers
H_{b}
.
The dynamical friction vector then reads

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}=2\frac{c_{ab}}{m_{a}^{2}}\big(1+\frac{m_{a}}{m_{b}}\big)\frac{n_{b}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}\Big[\boldsymbol{y}\Big(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{y^{2}}-\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y^{3}}\Big)-\frac{2}{5}\frac{m_{b}v_{\textrm{th}b}}{T_{b}p_{b}}\Big(\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}-2\boldsymbol{y}(\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{y})\Big)\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-y^{2}}\Big],

(H14)

and after slight re-arrangement the diffusion tensor becomes

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\frac{c_{ab}}{m_{a}^{2}}\Big\{\frac{n_{b}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\Big[\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{y^{2}}+\big(\frac{1}{y}-\frac{1}{2y^{3}}\big)\textrm{erf}(y)\Big]+\frac{n_{b}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}}\frac{\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}}{y^{2}}\Big[\big(\frac{3}{2y^{3}}-\frac{1}{y}\big)\textrm{erf}(y)-\frac{3}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{y^{2}}\Big]

(H15)

\displaystyle+\frac{n_{b}m_{b}}{5T_{b}p_{b}}\Big[\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\boldsymbol{y}+\boldsymbol{y}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}+(\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{y})\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\Big]\Big[-\frac{3}{2}\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y^{5}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\big(\frac{2}{y^{2}}+\frac{3}{y^{4}}\big)e^{-y^{2}}\Big]

\displaystyle+\frac{n_{b}m_{b}}{5T_{b}p_{b}}(\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{y})\frac{\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}}{y^{2}}\Big[\frac{15}{2}\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y^{5}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\big(4+\frac{10}{y^{2}}+\frac{15}{y^{4}}\big)e^{-y^{2}}\Big]\Big\}.

H.2
Momentum exchange rates
\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}

Then similarly to
f_{b}
according to (
H1
), one prescribes for species ’a’

f_{a}(\boldsymbol{v})=\frac{n_{a}}{\pi^{3/2}v_{\textrm{th}a}^{3}}e^{-\frac{|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2}}{v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}}}\Big[1-\frac{m_{a}}{T_{a}p_{a}}\Big(1-\frac{m_{a}|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2}}{5T_{a}}\Big)\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\Big],

(H16)

and introduces variable
\boldsymbol{u}=(\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a})/v_{\textrm{th}a}
, so that
\boldsymbol{c}_{a}=\boldsymbol{y}v_{\textrm{th}b}+\boldsymbol{u}v_{\textrm{th}a}
. However, the resulting
integrals would yield the runaway effect, and were never evaluated. It is necessary to get rid of the runaway effect,
and approximate the
f_{a}
with small drifts
u\ll 1
, and in the first step

\displaystyle f_{a}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\frac{n_{a}}{\pi^{3/2}v_{\textrm{th}a}^{3}}e^{-\alpha^{2}y^{2}}\Big[1-2\alpha(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})-u^{2}+2\alpha^{2}(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})^{2}\Big]\Big[1-\frac{m_{a}}{T_{a}p_{a}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\cdot\big(\boldsymbol{y}v_{\textrm{th}b}+\boldsymbol{u}v_{\textrm{th}a}\big)

(H17)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{m_{a}^{2}}{5T_{a}^{2}p_{a}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\cdot\big(\boldsymbol{y}v_{\textrm{th}b}+\boldsymbol{u}v_{\textrm{th}a}\big)\big(y^{2}v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}+2\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}v_{\textrm{th}a}v_{\textrm{th}b}+u^{2}v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}\big)\Big],

where
\alpha=v_{\textrm{th}b}/v_{\textrm{th}a}
. Distribution function (
H17
) needs to be further reduced
to the “semi-linear approximation”, where the difference in temperatures is not restricted,
but one keeps only precision
o(\boldsymbol{u})
and also neglects all the cross-terms such as
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}
, keeping only

\displaystyle f_{a}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\frac{n_{a}}{\pi^{3/2}v_{\textrm{th}a}^{3}}e^{-\alpha^{2}y^{2}}\Big[1-2\alpha(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})-\frac{m_{a}}{T_{a}p_{a}}(\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{y})v_{\textrm{th}b}\Big(1-\frac{2}{5}\alpha^{2}y^{2}\Big)\Big].

(H18)

We want to obtain

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}=2\frac{c_{ab}}{m_{a}}\big(1+\frac{m_{a}}{m_{b}}\big)\int f_{a}\frac{\partial H_{b}}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}d^{3}v,

(H19)

and we split the calculation to two integrals of (
H12
). The first integral
\sim\boldsymbol{y}

calculates

\displaystyle\int\boldsymbol{y}\Big(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{y^{2}}-\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y^{3}}\Big)f_{a}d^{3}v

\displaystyle=\frac{n_{a}v_{\textrm{th}b}^{3}}{\pi^{3/2}v_{\textrm{th}a}^{3}}4\pi\Big[\frac{\alpha}{3\alpha^{2}(1+\alpha^{2})^{3/2}}\boldsymbol{u}+\frac{m_{a}}{T_{a}p_{a}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}v_{\textrm{th}b}\frac{1}{10\alpha^{2}(1+\alpha^{2})^{5/2}}\Big],

(H20)

where we have used

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha^{2}y^{2}}y^{4}\Big(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{y^{2}}-\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y^{3}}\Big)dy=-\frac{1}{2\alpha^{2}(1+\alpha^{2})^{3/2}};

(H21)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha^{2}y^{2}}y^{4}\big(1+\frac{2}{5}\alpha^{2}y^{2}\big)\Big(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{y^{2}}-\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y^{3}}\Big)dy=-\frac{3}{10\alpha^{2}(1+\alpha^{2})^{5/2}},

(H22)

and the second part of (
H12
) calculates

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int e^{-y^{2}}\Big(\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}-2\boldsymbol{y}(\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{y})\Big)f_{a}d^{3}v=\frac{n_{a}v_{\textrm{th}b}^{3}}{\pi^{3/2}v_{\textrm{th}a}^{3}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}2\pi\frac{\alpha^{2}}{(1+\alpha^{2})^{5/2}}.

(H23)

For a quick conversion to collisional frequencies, it is useful to write

\displaystyle\nu_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{8}{3\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{n_{b}}{v_{\textrm{th}a}^{3}(1+\alpha^{2})^{3/2}}\frac{c_{ab}}{m_{a}^{2}}\big(1+\frac{m_{a}}{m_{b}}\big).

(H24)

Putting the results together yields the final result

\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}=\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}(\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a})+\nu_{ab}\frac{3}{5}\frac{\mu_{ab}}{T_{ab}}\Big(\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}-\frac{\rho_{a}}{\rho_{b}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\Big),

(H25)

recovering equation (41b) of
Schunk 1977
(before derived by
Burgers 1969
). Alternatively
\mu_{ab}/T_{ab}=2/(v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}+v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2})
.
As a double check,
\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}=-\boldsymbol{R}_{ba}
and for self-collisions
\boldsymbol{R}_{aa}=0
, as it should be. The contribution coming from
the heat flux is known as the
thermal force
.

H.3
Heat flux exchange rates

To calculate the heat flux contributions, one needs to calculate

\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{ab}^{(3)}\,{}^{\prime}=\frac{\delta\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{ab}\,^{\prime}}{\delta t}=\frac{1}{2}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{ab}^{(3)}-\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}-\frac{1}{\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)},

(H26)

where
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}=0
for the 8-moment model (cross-term
\boldsymbol{R}_{ab}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}
would be neglected anyway) and where

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{ab}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{a}\int f_{a}\Big[(\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}\cdot\boldsymbol{c}_{a})\boldsymbol{c}_{a}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2}\Big]d^{3}v

(H27)

\displaystyle+m_{a}\int f_{a}\Big[\frac{1}{2}(\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab})\boldsymbol{c}_{a}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}\cdot\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\Big]d^{3}v.

We have used
\textrm{Tr}[\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{c}\boldsymbol{c}]^{S}=2(\boldsymbol{A}\cdot\boldsymbol{c})\boldsymbol{c}+\boldsymbol{A}|\boldsymbol{c}|^{2}
, and because the diffusion tensor is symmetric

\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{S}=2\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}
, and
\textrm{Tr}[\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}^{S}\boldsymbol{c}]^{S}=2(\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}})\boldsymbol{c}+4\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{c}
. By assuming no restriction on the temperature difference,
we have verified (with a great help of Maple) that the “semi-linear” heat flux contributions (45)-(49) of
Schunk 1977

(derived before by
Burgers 1969
) are indeed correct for Coulomb collisions (with
z_{st}=3/5
,
z_{st}^{\prime}=13/10
,
z_{st}^{\prime\prime}=2
and also

z_{st}^{\prime\prime\prime}=4
). For Coulomb collisions, the final result (after subtraction of
\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}
)
is written in a compact form in Section
II.3
; see equation (
32
).

In the “linear approximation” where the temperature differences are small, the result simplifies into

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{ab}^{(3)}=\frac{\delta\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{ab}}{\delta t}=\nu_{ab}\Big[-\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}D_{ab(1)}+\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\frac{\rho_{a}}{\rho_{b}}D_{ab(4)}+p_{a}(\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\frac{m_{b}+\frac{5}{2}m_{a}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}\Big],

(H28)

where the introduced constants are defined in (
H30
), (
H31
). Alternatively, by summing over all the ’b’ species and
separating the self-collisions

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{a}^{(3)}=\frac{\delta\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}}{\delta t}=-\frac{4}{5}\nu_{aa}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}-\sum_{b\neq a}\nu_{ab}\Big[D_{ab(1)}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}-D_{ab(4)}\frac{\rho_{a}}{\rho_{b}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}-p_{a}(\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\frac{m_{b}+\frac{5}{2}m_{a}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}\Big];

(H29)

\displaystyle D_{ab(1)}=\frac{1}{(m_{a}+m_{b})^{2}}\Big(3m_{a}^{2}+\frac{1}{10}m_{a}m_{b}-\frac{1}{5}m_{b}^{2}\Big);

(H30)

\displaystyle D_{ab(4)}=\frac{1}{(m_{a}+m_{b})^{2}}\Big(\frac{6}{5}m_{b}^{2}-\frac{3}{2}m_{a}m_{b}\Big),

(H31)

recovering equations (41e)-(43) of
Schunk 1977
; see also equations (34)-(36) of
Killie et al. 2004
.
The entire heat flux contributions thus are

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{a}^{(3)}\,{}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\,^{\prime}}{\delta t}=\frac{1}{2}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{a}^{(3)}-\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\boldsymbol{R}_{a}

(H32)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\Big[\frac{4}{5}\nu_{aa}+\sum_{b\neq a}\nu_{ab}\Big(D_{ab(1)}+\frac{3}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\frac{\mu_{ab}}{T_{ab}}\Big)\Big]+\sum_{b\neq a}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\nu_{ab}\frac{\rho_{a}}{\rho_{b}}\Big(D_{ab(4)}+\frac{3}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\frac{\mu_{ab}}{T_{ab}}\Big)

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{2}p_{a}\sum_{b\neq a}\nu_{ab}\frac{m_{b}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}),

and enter the r.h.s. of evolution equation for the heat flux vector, for example in its simplest form

\frac{d_{a}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}}{dt}+\Omega_{a}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{m_{a}}\nabla T_{a}=\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{a}^{(3)}\,{}^{\prime}.

(H33)

Importantly, in comparison to the BGK operator, the r.h.s also contains all the heat fluxes
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}
. Formally, it is still possible
to obtain a result for
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}
in a quasi-static approximation, as a solution of equation

\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\frac{\bar{\nu}_{a}}{\Omega_{a}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}=-\frac{\vec{\boldsymbol{a}}_{a}}{\Omega_{a}},

(H34)

where we defined

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{5}\nu_{aa}+\sum_{b\neq a}\nu_{ab}\Big(D_{ab(1)}+\frac{3}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\frac{\mu_{ab}}{T_{ab}}\Big);

(H35)

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{a}}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{m_{a}}\nabla T_{a}-\sum_{b\neq a}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\nu_{ab}\frac{\rho_{a}}{\rho_{b}}\Big(D_{ab(4)}+\frac{3}{2}\frac{p_{a}}{\rho_{a}}\frac{\mu_{ab}}{T_{ab}}\Big)

(H36)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{3}{2}p_{a}\sum_{b\neq a}\nu_{ab}\frac{m_{b}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a}),

which has the following exact solution

\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}=-\frac{1}{\bar{\nu}_{a}}(\vec{\boldsymbol{a}}_{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{\hat{b}})\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}-\frac{\bar{\nu}_{a}}{\Omega_{a}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{a}^{2}}\vec{\boldsymbol{a}}_{a\perp}+\frac{\Omega_{a}}{\Omega_{a}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{a}^{2}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\vec{\boldsymbol{a}}_{a}.

(H37)

Nevertheless, the heat fluxes of various species are coupled.

H.4
One ion-electron plasma

Considering a one ion-electron plasma (so
n_{e}=Z_{i}n_{i}
) with small differences in temperature, and neglecting ratios
m_{e}/m_{i}

the ion and electron heat fluxes de-couple.
For the electron species
D_{ei(1)}=-1/5
,
D_{ei(4)}=6/5
,
\mu_{ei}=m_{e}
and by using abbreviation
\delta\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{u}_{e}-\boldsymbol{u}_{i}
then

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\rho_{e}\nu_{ei}\delta\boldsymbol{u}+\nu_{ei}\frac{\rho_{e}}{p_{e}}\frac{3}{5}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e};

(H38)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}}{\delta t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}\Big(\frac{4}{5}\nu_{ee}-\frac{1}{5}\nu_{ei}\Big)-\nu_{ei}p_{e}\delta\boldsymbol{u}.

(H39)

The entire heat flux contributions are

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{e}^{(3)}\,{}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bar{\nu}_{e}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}+\frac{3}{2}\nu_{ei}p_{e}\delta\boldsymbol{u};

(H40)

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{5}\nu_{ee}+\frac{13}{10}\nu_{ei};

(H41)

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{a}}_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{e}}{m_{e}}\nabla T_{e}-\frac{3}{2}\nu_{ei}p_{e}\delta\boldsymbol{u},

(H42)

yielding solution for the electron heat flux (split into thermal part and frictional part)

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}^{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\kappa_{\parallel}^{e}\nabla_{\parallel}T_{e}-\kappa_{\perp}^{e}\nabla_{\perp}T_{e}+\kappa_{\times}^{e}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\nabla T_{e};

(H43)

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}^{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{2}\frac{\nu_{ei}}{\bar{\nu}_{e}}p_{e}\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\parallel}+\frac{3}{2}\frac{\bar{\nu}_{e}\nu_{ei}}{\Omega_{e}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{e}^{2}}p_{e}\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\perp}-\frac{3}{2}\frac{\Omega_{e}\nu_{ei}}{\Omega_{e}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{e}^{2}}p_{e}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\delta\boldsymbol{u},

(H44)

with thermal conductivities

\kappa_{\parallel}^{e}=\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{e}}{\bar{\nu}_{e}m_{e}};\qquad\kappa_{\perp}^{e}=\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{e}}{m_{e}}\frac{\bar{\nu}_{e}}{(\Omega_{e}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{e}^{2})};\qquad\kappa_{\times}^{e}=\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{e}}{m_{e}}\frac{\Omega_{e}}{(\Omega_{e}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{e}^{2})}.

(H45)

The thermal conductivities have the same form as the BGK conductivities. The difference is only that while for the BGK operator

\bar{\nu}_{e}=\nu_{ee}+\nu_{ei}
, now we have to use (
H41
). By using
\nu_{ee}=\nu_{ei}/(Z_{i}\sqrt{2})
from equation (
182
)

\bar{\nu}_{e}=\big(\frac{1}{Z_{i}\sqrt{2}}\frac{4}{5}+\frac{13}{10}\big)\nu_{ei};\quad\textrm{for}\quad Z_{i}=1:\quad\bar{\nu}_{e}=1.866\nu_{ei}.

(H46)

The momentum exchange rates are also split to friction part and thermal part

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{e}^{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\rho_{e}\nu_{ei}\Big[\Big(1-\frac{9}{10}\frac{\nu_{ei}}{\bar{\nu}_{e}}\Big)\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\parallel}+\Big(1-\frac{9}{10}\frac{\bar{\nu}_{e}\nu_{ei}}{\Omega_{e}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{e}^{2}}\Big)\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\perp}+\frac{9}{10}\frac{\Omega_{e}\nu_{ei}}{\Omega_{e}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{e}^{2}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\delta\boldsymbol{u}\Big];

(H47)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{e}^{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{2}\frac{\nu_{ei}}{\bar{\nu}_{e}}n_{e}\nabla_{\parallel}T_{e}-\frac{3}{2}\frac{\bar{\nu}_{e}\nu_{ei}}{\Omega_{e}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{e}^{2}}n_{e}\nabla_{\perp}T_{e}+\frac{3}{2}\frac{\Omega_{e}\nu_{ei}}{\Omega_{e}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{e}^{2}}n_{e}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\nabla T_{e}.

(H48)

In comparison, the
Braginskii 1965
result for
Z_{i}=1
reads

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{e}^{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\rho_{e}\nu_{ei}\Big[0.51\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\parallel}+\Big(1-\frac{6.42x^{2}+1.84}{x^{4}+14.79x^{2}+3.77}\Big)\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\perp}+\frac{x(1.70x^{2}+0.78)}{x^{4}+14.79x^{2}+3.77}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\delta\boldsymbol{u}\Big];

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{e}^{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-0.71n_{e}\nabla_{\parallel}T_{e}-\frac{5.10x^{2}+2.68}{x^{4}+14.79x^{2}+3.77}n_{e}\nabla_{\perp}T_{e}+\frac{x((3/2)x^{2}+3.05)}{x^{4}+14.79x^{2}+3.77}n_{e}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\nabla T_{e},

(H49)

where
x=\Omega_{e}/\nu_{ei}
. The heat flux and associated thermal force of Burgers and Schunk therefore finally explain
the entire mathematical structure of Braginskii equations, i.e. all the terms are finally present, only the numerical values are different.

Examining the obtained numerical values, for example in the limit of strong magnetic field with
Z_{i}=1

(where for simplicity we neglect all the ratios
\nu_{ei}/\Omega_{e}
) yields

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\rho_{e}\nu_{ei}(0.518\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\parallel}+\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\perp})-0.80n_{e}\nabla_{\parallel}T_{e};

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}^{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+0.80p_{e}\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\parallel},

(H50)

which is very close to Braginskii values

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\rho_{e}\nu_{ei}(0.513\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\parallel}+\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\perp})-0.71n_{e}\nabla_{\parallel}T_{e};

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}^{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+0.71p_{e}\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\parallel}.

(H51)

Note that both results (
H50
), (
H51
) contain the same symmetrical constants 0.8 and 0.71 in the
frictional heat flux
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}^{u}
and the thermal force
\boldsymbol{R}_{e}^{T}
. This is known as the Onsager symmetry, and it is also valid
for a general magnetic field strength and a general charge, as can be seen by comparing (
H44
) and (
H48
).

Continuing with the strong magnetic field and examining the perpendicular heat conductivities yields (
Z_{i}=1
for
\kappa_{\perp}^{e}
)

\kappa_{\perp}^{e}=4.66\frac{p_{e}\nu_{ei}}{m_{e}\Omega_{e}^{2}};\quad\kappa_{\times}^{e}=\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{e}}{m_{e}\Omega_{e}},

(H52)

and both match Braginskii exactly. Nevertheless, the parallel heat conductivity (which is independent of magnetic field strength;
Z_{i}=1
)

\kappa_{\parallel}^{e}=1.34\frac{p_{e}}{\nu_{ei}m_{e}},

(H53)

which is quite low in comparison to the Braginskii value of 3.16.

Ion species

For ion species
D_{ie(1)}=3
,
D_{ie(4)}=-3m_{e}/(2m_{i})
and identical proton and electron temperatures,
momentum exchange rates (
H25
) yield

\boldsymbol{R}_{i}=\rho_{i}\nu_{ie}\delta\boldsymbol{u}-\nu_{ie}\frac{\rho_{i}}{p_{e}}\frac{3}{5}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}=-\boldsymbol{R}_{e},

(H54)

and
\boldsymbol{R}_{e}
was already calculated. Furthermore, collisional heat flux contributions (
H32
)-(
H37
) simplify into

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{i}^{(3)}\,{}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bar{\nu}_{i}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{i};

(H55)

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{5}\nu_{ii}+3\nu_{ie};

(H56)

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{a}}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{i}}{m_{i}}\nabla T_{i},

(H57)

where notably the electron heat flux
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}
cancels out exactly for equal temperatures.
Ion frequencies should be thus added according to

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{4}{5}+3\frac{\sqrt{2}}{Z_{i}}\sqrt{\frac{m_{e}}{m_{i}}}\Big)\nu_{ii};\quad\textrm{for}\quad Z_{i}=1:\quad\bar{\nu}_{i}=0.899\nu_{ii};

(H58)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{5}\Big(1+\frac{15}{2Z_{i}}\sqrt{\frac{m_{e}}{2m_{i}}}\Big)\nu_{ii}.

The model of Burgers-Schunk yields ion heat flux

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\kappa_{\parallel}^{i}\nabla_{\parallel}T_{i}-\kappa_{\perp}^{i}\nabla_{\perp}T_{i}+\kappa_{\times}^{i}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\nabla T_{i},

(H59)

with ion thermal conductivities

\kappa_{\parallel}^{i}=\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{i}}{\bar{\nu}_{i}m_{i}};\qquad\kappa_{\perp}^{i}=\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{i}}{m_{i}}\frac{\bar{\nu}_{i}}{(\Omega_{i}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{i}^{2})};\qquad\kappa_{\times}^{i}=\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{i}}{m_{i}}\frac{\Omega_{i}}{(\Omega_{i}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{i}^{2})},

(H60)

where frequencies are added according to (
H58
).
Importantly, the ion-electron contributions are not completely negligible, and without them
\bar{\nu}_{i}=(4/5)\nu_{ii}=0.8\nu_{ii}
.

However, in the work of
Braginskii 1965
the ion-electron collisions are neglected for the
ion heat fluxes and viscosities, and only ion self-collisions are accounted for. This can be seen from his ion coefficients which do
not depend on
Z_{i}
. Neglecting the ion-electron collisions, the model of Burgers-Schunk yields

\kappa_{\parallel}^{i}=\frac{25}{8}\frac{p_{i}}{\nu_{ii}m_{i}};\qquad\kappa_{\perp}^{i}=2\frac{p_{i}}{m_{i}}\frac{\nu_{ii}}{\Omega_{i}^{2}+(4/5)^{2}\nu_{ii}^{2}};\qquad\kappa_{\times}^{i}=\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{i}}{m_{i}}\frac{\Omega_{i}}{\Omega_{i}^{2}+(4/5)^{2}\nu_{ii}^{2}}.

(H61)

For the parallel conductivity
\kappa_{\parallel}^{i}\sim 25/8=3.125
, in comparison to Braginskii
3.906
. In the strong magnetic field limit

\kappa_{\perp}^{i}=2\frac{p_{i}}{m_{i}}\frac{\nu_{ii}}{\Omega_{i}^{2}};\qquad\kappa_{\times}^{i}=\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{i}}{m_{i}\Omega_{i}}.

(H62)

and both match Braginskii exactly (!).
If ion-electron collisions are taken into account, these Burger-Schunk coefficients change into (for
Z_{i}=1
)

\kappa_{\parallel}^{i}\sim 2.78
,
\kappa_{\perp}^{i}\sim 2.24
and
\kappa_{\times}^{i}\sim 5/2
, and the perpendicular
\kappa_{\perp}^{i}
would suddenly
not match Braginskii.
It would not make sense that electron
\kappa_{\perp}^{e}
matches Braginskii exactly (for strong B-field) and ion
\kappa_{\perp}^{i}
does not, which
is a definitive indication that ion-electron collisions are neglected in Braginskii.

Including the ion-electron collisions, the
\kappa_{\perp}^{i}
in the strong B-limit reads

\kappa_{\perp}^{i}=\frac{p_{i}\nu_{ii}}{m_{i}\Omega_{i}^{2}}\Big(2+\frac{15}{Z_{i}}\sqrt{\frac{m_{e}}{2m_{i}}}\Big).

(H63)

Neglecting ion-electron collisions with respect to ion-ion (self) collisions, is analogous to neglecting

0.1
with respect to
0.8
, the contribution is not tiny.

Appendix I
Comparison of various models with Braginskii (electrons)

Focusing at the parallel direction, the momentum exchange rates
\boldsymbol{R}_{e\parallel}
and electron heat flux
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e\parallel}

can be written in a general form

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{e\parallel}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\alpha_{0}\rho_{e}\nu_{ei}\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\parallel}-\beta_{0}n_{e}\nabla_{\parallel}T_{e};

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e\parallel}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+\beta_{0}^{*}p_{e}\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\parallel}-\gamma_{0}\frac{p_{e}}{m_{e}\nu_{ei}}\nabla_{\parallel}T_{e}.

(I1)

Braginskii 1965
values of
\alpha_{0};\beta_{0}=\beta_{0}^{*}
and
\gamma_{0}
are given in his Table 2, page 25.
The model of
Burgers 1969
-
Schunk 1977
is given by

\alpha_{0}=1-\frac{9}{10}\frac{\nu_{ei}}{\bar{\nu}_{e}};\quad\beta_{0}=\beta_{0}^{*}=\frac{3}{2}\frac{\nu_{ei}}{\bar{\nu}_{e}};\quad\gamma_{0}=\frac{5}{2}\frac{\nu_{ei}}{\bar{\nu}_{e}};\qquad\bar{\nu}_{e}=\Big(\frac{1}{Z_{i}\sqrt{2}}\frac{4}{5}+\frac{13}{10}\Big)\nu_{ei},

(I2)

or equivalently

\alpha_{0}=\frac{\sqrt{2}+Z_{i}}{\sqrt{2}+(13/4)Z_{i}};\quad\beta_{0}=\beta_{0}^{*}=\frac{15Z_{i}}{4\sqrt{2}+13Z_{i}};\quad\gamma_{0}=\frac{25Z_{i}}{4\sqrt{2}+13Z_{i}}.

(I3)

The model of
Killie et al. 2004
discussed in Section
I.2
yields

\alpha_{0}=1-\frac{9}{35}\frac{\nu_{ei}}{\bar{\nu}_{e}};\quad\beta_{0}=\frac{3}{7}\frac{\nu_{ei}}{\bar{\nu}_{e}};\quad\beta_{0}^{*}=\frac{3}{2}\frac{\nu_{ei}}{\bar{\nu}_{e}};\quad\gamma_{0}=\frac{5}{2}\frac{\nu_{ei}}{\bar{\nu}_{e}};\quad\bar{\nu}_{e}=\Big(\frac{1}{Z_{i}\sqrt{2}}\frac{16}{35}+\frac{11}{35}\Big)\nu_{ei}.

(I4)

Other included models are described bellow.

In Table
I.1
, we compare the parallel friction force, in Table
I.2
the parallel thermal force,
in Table
I.3
the parallel thermal heat flux (thermal conductivity
\kappa_{\parallel}^{e}
), and
in Table
I.4
the parallel frictional heat flux.
Furthermore, in Table
I.5
we compare
\kappa_{\perp}^{e}
in the strong magnetic field limit.

Parallel friction force
\boldsymbol{R}_{e}^{u}=-\alpha_{0}\rho_{e}\nu_{ei}\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\parallel}
, coefficient
\alpha_{0}
is plotted,
or parallel electrical resistivity

.
The model of Burgers-Schunk is more precise than Killie et al.. The model of Landshoff for
N=1

, emphasized with bold font) from

, and values of Landshoff for other
Z_{i}
might be slightly incorrect.

Z_{i}=2,3
in his Table II are slightly incorrect, and we used values from

), which now also match Landshoff (
N=2
).

Z_{i}=16
is also from (

).
From
Kaneko & Taguchi 1978
;
Kaneko & Yamao 1980
and
Ji & Held 2013
, the “final” value for
Z_{i}=1
is
\alpha_{0}=0.50612
, and the result of Spitzer-Härm is correct.

n_{e}
and
T_{e}
constant
in the definition of
\nu_{ei}
, the friction force
\sim\alpha_{0}\nu_{ei}
actually increases with increasing
Z_{i}

(and the electrical conductivity decreases).

Table I.5:
Perpendicular electron heat conductivity
\kappa_{\perp}^{e}=\gamma_{1}^{\prime}p_{e}\nu_{ei}/(m_{e}\Omega_{e}^{2})
,
in the limit of strong magnetic field (so the conductivity is small), coefficient
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}
is plotted.
Braginskii values are from his Table II.
Interestingly, the Burgers-Schunk model matches Braginskii values exactly. In fact, both models yield
the same analytic expression
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}=(\sqrt{2}/Z_{i})+13/4
, see (
60
),
so the numerical comparison between Burgers-Schunk and Braginskii is a bit meaningless
(and the reason why
Z_{i}=16
value was omitted in our table).
The table shows that the model of Killie et al. is imprecise.

We include the numerical model of
Spitzer & Härm 1953
(see also
Spitzer 1962
), with their notation
discussed in Section
I.1
, which reads

\alpha_{0}=\frac{3\pi}{32\gamma_{E}};\quad\beta_{0}=\frac{3}{2}\frac{\gamma_{T}}{\gamma_{E}};\quad\beta_{0}^{*}=4\frac{\delta_{E}}{\gamma_{E}}-\frac{5}{2};\quad\gamma_{0}=\epsilon\delta_{T}\frac{320}{3\pi},

(I5)

with numerical values of
\gamma_{E},\gamma_{T},\delta_{E},\delta_{T},\epsilon
given by Table III in
Spitzer & Härm 1953
. For Lorentzian plasma
(
Z_{i}=\infty
) the coefficients are
\gamma_{E}=\gamma_{T}=\delta_{E}=\delta_{T}=1
and
\epsilon=2/5
.
We also include the model of
Landshoff 1949
;
Landshoff 1951
, who calculated several transport coefficients (with the inclusion of magnetic field)
before Spitzer and Braginskii, and studied convergence with increasing Laguerre polynomials from
N=1
to
N=4
(in his work
i=N+1
). The model is interesting because
for
N=1
it matches the values of Burgers-Schunk, and for
N=2
it matches Braginskii. His model can be figured out to be

\alpha_{0}=\frac{1}{Z_{i}}\big(\triangle_{00}/\triangle\big)^{-1};\qquad\beta_{0}=\beta_{0}^{*}=\frac{5}{2}\frac{\triangle_{01}/\triangle}{\triangle_{00}/\triangle};\qquad\gamma_{0}=\frac{25}{4}Z_{i}\Big(\frac{\triangle_{11}}{\triangle}-\frac{(\triangle_{01}/\triangle)^{2}}{\triangle_{00}/\triangle}\Big),

(I6)

with coefficients from Table I of
Landshoff 1951
. We plot his highest-order model for
N=4
. The models of Landshoff were calculated with higher numerical
precision in the work of
Kaneko 1960
, where the following conversion has to be used

\alpha_{0}=\frac{1}{e^{\textrm{I}(0)}};\qquad\beta_{0}=\beta_{0}^{*}=-\,\frac{5}{2}\frac{b^{\textrm{I}(0)}}{e^{\textrm{I}(0)}};\qquad\gamma_{0}=\frac{25}{4}\Big[b^{\textrm{I}(-1)}-\frac{(b^{\textrm{I}(0)})^{2}}{e^{\textrm{I}(0)}}\Big],

(I7)

with values in his Tables I,II,III. In his work
M=N+1
, and values for models from
N=1
to
N=5
are given, even though only for
Z_{i}=1
.
The model is easily comparable with
Landshoff 1951
because the same coefficients are given.
In our comparison tables, we thus slightly correct these
Z_{i}=1
values of Landshoff (
N=4
) with the higher precise ones of Kaneko.
In a later work of
Kaneko & Taguchi 1978
;
Kaneko & Yamao 1980
calculations with up to
M=50
were made, and
the notation is changed into
b^{\textrm{I}(0)}\to b_{1}^{\textrm{I}(0)}
,

b^{\textrm{I}(-1)}\to b_{1}^{\textrm{I}(1)}
. From their work and the recent work of
Ji & Held 2013
who used up to 160 Laguerre polynomials,
the correct values for charge
Z_{i}=1
read
\alpha_{0}=0.50612
;
\beta_{0}=0.70287
;
\gamma_{0}=3.2031
.

For the work of
Balescu 1988
, who was the first to recover Braginskii with the moment approach of Grad, the following conversion has to be used

\alpha_{0}=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\sigma}_{\parallel}};\qquad\beta_{0}=\beta_{0}^{*}=-\,\sqrt{\frac{5}{2}}\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}_{\parallel}}{\widetilde{\sigma}_{\parallel}};\qquad\gamma_{0}=\frac{5}{2}\big(\widetilde{\kappa}_{\parallel}^{e}-\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}_{\parallel}^{2}}{\widetilde{\sigma}_{\parallel}}\big),

(I8)

with numerical values for
Z_{i}=1
given on his page 239, Table 4.1.
For his 13-moment model (
N=1
) the results are equal to Burgers-Schunk, and for his
21-moment model (
N=2
) the results are equal to Braginskii. However, for his 29-moment model (
N=3
) the coefficients of Balescu were
shown to be imprecise by
Ji & Held 2013
, see their Table I, who were able to exactly pin-point analytic
errors in the collisional matrices of Balescu. That the Balescu
N=3
values are indeed incorrect can be
quickly double-checked by comparison with the
M=4
model of
Kaneko 1960
, from where the Balescu parameters
should be
\widetilde{\sigma}_{\parallel}=e^{\textrm{I}(0)}=1.964
;
\widetilde{\alpha}_{\parallel}=\sqrt{5/2}b^{\textrm{I}(0)}=-0.887
;

\widetilde{\kappa}_{\parallel}^{e}=(5/2)b^{\textrm{I}(-1)}=1.666
, agreeing with the modern calculations of
Ji & Held 2013
.

I.1
Notation of Spitzer-Härm 1953

The exact values of parallel transport coefficients (with the exception of parallel viscosity) were first numerically obtained by
Spitzer & Härm 1953
.
Essentially, the perturbation
\phi_{e}
(or
f^{(1)}_{e}
) around a Maxwellian

f_{e}=f_{e}^{(0)}(1-\phi_{e})
that satisfies the Fokker-Planck equation was found numerically, and the obtained result was used to calculate the transport coefficients.
No magnetic field is present in their work, and the results can
be interpreted as applying to unmagnetized plasmas, or to magnetized plasmas in the direction parallel to magnetic field lines.
Similarly to Braginskii (Chapter 2 & 4), the paper treats a one ion-electron plasma (with
n_{e}=Z_{i}n_{i}
).

The notation of
Spitzer & Härm 1953
can be very confusing. The results are given in a form

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma\boldsymbol{E}+\alpha\nabla T_{e};

(I9)

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\textrm{Spitzer}}_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\beta\boldsymbol{E}-K\nabla T_{e},

(I10)

with coefficients
\sigma,\alpha,\beta,K
given by their equations (33)-(36). These coefficients contain a quantity

C^{2}
. This quantity is only defined by a sentence after equation (16) of their previous paper by
Cohen et al. 1950
,
which reads “
C^{2}
is the mean square electron velocity”,
meaning
C=\sqrt{3T_{e}/m_{e}}
with the important factor of 3 present (we use the same notation as Braginskii,
with the Boltzmann constant equal to one).
Rewriting their coefficients in (
I9
), (
I10
) to our notation yields

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{32}{3\pi}\frac{e^{2}n_{e}}{m_{e}\nu_{ei}}\gamma_{E};\qquad\alpha=\frac{16}{\pi}\frac{en_{e}}{m_{e}\nu_{ei}}\gamma_{T};

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{128}{3\pi}\frac{ep_{e}}{m_{e}\nu_{ei}}\delta_{E};\qquad K=\frac{320}{3\pi}\frac{p_{e}}{m_{e}\nu_{ei}}\delta_{T},

(I11)

where numerical values of
\gamma_{E},\gamma_{T},\delta_{E},\delta_{T}
are given in Table III of
Spitzer & Härm 1953
.
Coefficients (
I11
) are essentially normalized with respect to a Lorentzian plasma
Z_{i}=\infty
(meaning when
electron-electron collisions are negligible), in which case
\gamma_{E}=\gamma_{T}=\delta_{E}=\delta_{T}=1
.

Unfortunately,
Spitzer & Härm 1953
do not define their
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\textrm{Spitzer}}_{e}
and only describe it as a “the rate of flow of heat”.
The heat flux is also not defined in the book of
Spitzer 1962
, however he notes (equation 5.45), that from the thermodynamics of irreversible processes the model
closely satisfies

\beta=\alpha T_{e}+\frac{5}{2}\frac{T_{e}}{e}\sigma.

(I12)

Equation (
I12
) should be the Onsager symmetry. In historical literature, there are three other major possibilities how to
define the heat flux, the first two choices are

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\,***}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{m_{a}}{2}\int\boldsymbol{v}|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}f_{a}d^{3}v=\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\frac{5}{2}p_{a}\boldsymbol{u}_{a}+\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}+\frac{\rho_{a}}{2}|\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{a};

(I13)

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\,**}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\boldsymbol{v}\Big(\frac{m_{a}v^{2}}{2}-\frac{5}{2}T_{a}\Big)f_{a}d^{3}v=\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\boldsymbol{u}_{a}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}+\frac{\rho_{a}}{2}|\boldsymbol{u}_{a}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{a}.

(I14)

The nonlinear terms can be neglected. Spitzer is not using the second choice, and the first choice is almost correct, except that for
the electron heat flux only
(5/2)p_{e}\boldsymbol{u}_{e}
would be created, and not the whole current
\boldsymbol{u}_{e}-\boldsymbol{u}_{i}
.
The third choice is the definition of
Chapman & Cowling 1939
, where the heat flux is defined with respect to
average
velocity
of all the species
\langle\boldsymbol{u}\rangle\equiv(\sum_{a}\rho_{a}\boldsymbol{u}_{a})/\sum_{a}\rho_{a}
according to

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}^{\,*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{m_{a}}{2}\int(\boldsymbol{v}-\langle\boldsymbol{u}\rangle)|\boldsymbol{v}-\langle\boldsymbol{u}\rangle|^{2}f_{a}d^{3}v=\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}+\frac{5}{2}p_{a}\boldsymbol{w}_{a}+\frac{\rho_{a}}{2}|\boldsymbol{w}_{a}|^{2}\boldsymbol{w}_{a}+\boldsymbol{w}_{a}\cdot\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)},

(I15)

where
\boldsymbol{w}_{a}=\boldsymbol{u}_{a}-\langle\boldsymbol{u}\rangle
. For an ion-electron plasma
\langle\boldsymbol{u}\rangle=\boldsymbol{u}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{w}_{e}=\boldsymbol{u}_{e}-\boldsymbol{u}_{i}
.
Thus, to satisfy (
I12
) the correct interpretation seems to be

\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\textrm{Spitzer}}_{e}=\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}^{\,*}=\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}+\frac{5}{2}p_{e}\delta\boldsymbol{u}=\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}-\frac{5}{2}\frac{T_{e}}{e}\boldsymbol{j},

(I16)

where
\boldsymbol{j}=-en_{e}\delta\boldsymbol{u}
and
\delta\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{u}_{e}-\boldsymbol{u}_{i}
.

Result (
I9
) should be viewed as part of the evolution equation for
\partial\boldsymbol{u}_{e}/\partial t
(here written in a steady state with all other terms neglected),
and substituting the electric field into (
I10
) then yields

\displaystyle en_{e}\boldsymbol{E}=\boldsymbol{R}_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{en_{e}}{\sigma}\boldsymbol{j}-en_{e}\frac{\alpha}{\sigma}\nabla T_{e};

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Big(\frac{\beta}{\sigma}-\frac{5}{2}\frac{T_{e}}{e}\Big)\boldsymbol{j}-\epsilon K\nabla T_{e};\quad\textrm{where}\quad\epsilon=1-\frac{\alpha\beta}{\sigma K}=1-\frac{3}{5}\frac{\delta_{E}\gamma_{T}}{\delta_{T}\gamma_{E}}.

(I17)

The numerical coefficient
\epsilon
is given in Table III of
Spitzer & Härm 1953
as well. Or equivalently, by using (
I11
)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{3\pi}{32\gamma_{E}}\rho_{e}\nu_{ei}\delta\boldsymbol{u}-\frac{3}{2}\frac{\gamma_{T}}{\gamma_{E}}n_{e}\nabla T_{e};

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+\Big(4\frac{\delta_{E}}{\gamma_{E}}-\frac{5}{2}\Big)p_{e}\delta\boldsymbol{u}-\epsilon\delta_{T}\frac{320}{3\pi}\frac{p_{e}}{m_{e}\nu_{ei}}\nabla T_{e}.

(I18)

In this form the results can be directly compared to Braginskii, with relations

\alpha_{0}=\frac{3\pi}{32\gamma_{E}};\quad\beta_{0}=\frac{3}{2}\frac{\gamma_{T}}{\gamma_{E}};\quad\beta_{0}^{*}=4\frac{\delta_{E}}{\gamma_{E}}-\frac{5}{2};\quad\gamma_{0}=\epsilon\delta_{T}\frac{320}{3\pi}.

The Onsager symmetry then reads

\frac{3}{2}\gamma_{T}=4\delta_{E}-\frac{5}{2}\gamma_{E},

(I19)

which the model satisfies approximately, and for the Lorentz case exactly. The largest difference appears for
Z_{i}=2
,
where the l.h.s of (
I19
) is 0.621 and the r.h.s. is 0.607, so Spitzer’s claim that the equation (
I12
)
is satisfied to about 1 part in thousand seems a bit exaggerated, or we are interpreting his results incorrectly.
The model of
Spitzer & Härm 1953
and
Spitzer 1962

is criticized in the monograph of
Balescu 1988
, Part 1, p. 266.
Nevertheless, the coefficients
\alpha_{0}
,
\beta_{0}
and
\gamma_{0}
in the model of
Spitzer & Härm 1953
are the correct answer, and
in comparison with
Kaneko & Taguchi 1978
;
Kaneko & Yamao 1980
or
Ji & Held 2013
, these coefficients are valid for 3 decimal digits.
For numerical simulations that employ the heat flux of
Spitzer & Härm 1953
, it seems logical to simply ignore the imprecise
\beta_{0}^{*}
values,
and enforce the Onsager symmetry
\beta_{0}^{*}=\beta_{0}
in their model by hand.

I.2
Model of Killie et al. 2004

Instead of the 8-moment distribution function of Grad (
H1
) used in the model of Burgers-Schunk,

Killie et al. 2004
argued it is better to use

f_{a}=f_{a}^{(0)}\Big[1-\frac{m_{a}^{2}|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2}}{5T_{a}^{2}p_{a}}\Big(1-\frac{m_{a}|\boldsymbol{c}_{a}|^{2}}{7T_{a}}\Big)\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\Big],

(I20)

yielding collisional contributions (which we did not verify) for small temperature differences

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{a}=\rho_{a}\sum_{b}\nu_{ab}(\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a})+\sum_{b}\nu_{ab}\frac{3}{5}\frac{\mu_{ab}}{T_{ab}}\Big[\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\Big(1-\frac{5}{7}\frac{m_{b}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}\Big)-\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\frac{\rho_{a}}{\rho_{b}}\Big(1-\frac{5}{7}\frac{m_{a}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}\Big)\Big],

(I21)

and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{a}^{(3)}=\frac{\delta\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}}{\delta t}=-\frac{16}{35}\nu_{aa}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}-\sum_{b\neq a}\nu_{ab}\Big[D_{ab}^{(1)}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}-D_{ab}^{(4)}\frac{\rho_{a}}{\rho_{b}}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}-p_{a}(\boldsymbol{u}_{b}-\boldsymbol{u}_{a})\frac{m_{b}+\frac{5}{2}m_{a}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}\Big];

(I22)

\displaystyle D_{ab}^{(1)}=\frac{1}{(m_{a}+m_{b})^{3}}\Big(3m_{a}^{3}-\frac{1}{2}m_{a}^{2}m_{b}-\frac{2}{5}m_{a}m_{b}^{2}-\frac{4}{35}m_{b}^{3}\Big);

(I23)

\displaystyle D_{ab}^{(4)}=\frac{1}{(m_{a}+m_{b})^{3}}\Big(\frac{6}{5}m_{b}^{3}-\frac{171}{70}m_{b}^{2}m_{a}-\frac{3}{7}m_{b}m_{a}^{2}\Big).

(I24)

Similarly to Burgers-Schunk, they also provide equations for unrestricted temperature differences.
Considering an ion-electron plasma yields
D_{ei}^{(1)}=-4/35
,
D_{ei}^{(4)}=6/5
and

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\rho_{e}\nu_{ei}\delta\boldsymbol{u}+\nu_{ei}\frac{\rho_{e}}{p_{e}}\frac{6}{35}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e};

(I25)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{e}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}}{\delta t}=-\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}\Big(\frac{16}{35}\nu_{ee}-\frac{4}{35}\nu_{ei}\Big)-\nu_{ei}p_{e}\delta\boldsymbol{u},

(I26)

with total collisional contributions

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{e}^{(3)}\,{}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bar{\nu}_{e}\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}+\frac{3}{2}\nu_{ei}p_{e}\delta\boldsymbol{u};

(I27)

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{16}{35}\nu_{ee}+\frac{11}{35}\nu_{ei};

(I28)

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{a}}_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{e}}{m_{e}}\nabla T_{e}-\frac{3}{2}\nu_{ei}p_{e}\delta\boldsymbol{u}.

(I29)

This yields the heat flux solution equivalent to equation (
H43
)-(
H45
), with the only difference
that the frequencies are now added according to

\bar{\nu}_{e}=\Big(\frac{1}{Z_{i}\sqrt{2}}\frac{16}{35}+\frac{11}{35}\Big)\nu_{ei};\quad\textrm{for}\quad Z_{i}=1:\quad\bar{\nu}_{e}=0.6375\nu_{ei}.

(I30)

The momentum exchange rates then read

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{e}^{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\rho_{e}\nu_{ei}\Big[\Big(1-\frac{9}{35}\frac{\nu_{ei}}{\bar{\nu}_{e}}\Big)\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\parallel}+\Big(1-\frac{9}{35}\frac{\bar{\nu}_{e}\nu_{ei}}{\Omega_{e}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{e}^{2}}\Big)\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\perp}+\frac{9}{35}\frac{\Omega_{e}\nu_{ei}}{\Omega_{e}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{e}^{2}}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\delta\boldsymbol{u}\Big];

(I31)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{e}^{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{7}\frac{\nu_{ei}}{\bar{\nu}_{e}}n_{e}\nabla_{\parallel}T_{e}-\frac{3}{7}\frac{\bar{\nu}_{e}\nu_{ei}}{\Omega_{e}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{e}^{2}}n_{e}\nabla_{\perp}T_{e}+\frac{3}{7}\frac{\Omega_{e}\nu_{ei}}{\Omega_{e}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{e}^{2}}n_{e}\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\nabla T_{e},

(I32)

and direct comparison with Braginskii is done according to

\alpha_{0}=1-\frac{9}{35}\frac{\nu_{ei}}{\bar{\nu}_{e}};\quad\beta_{0}=\frac{3}{7}\frac{\nu_{ei}}{\bar{\nu}_{e}};\quad\beta_{0}^{*}=\frac{3}{2}\frac{\nu_{ei}}{\bar{\nu}_{e}};\quad\gamma_{0}=\frac{5}{2}\frac{\nu_{ei}}{\bar{\nu}_{e}};\quad\bar{\nu}_{e}=\Big(\frac{1}{Z_{i}\sqrt{2}}\frac{16}{35}+\frac{11}{35}\Big)\nu_{ei}.

(I33)

Examining the numerical values for
Z_{i}=1
, for example the parallel heat conductivity reads

\kappa_{\parallel}^{e}=3.92p_{e}/(\nu_{ei}m_{e})
. This is a big improvement in the model of
Killie et al. 2004
, the conductivity is almost 3 times larger than
the 1.34 value of Burgers-Schunk, and much closer to the correct value 3.20.
Other results are (strong B-field,
Z_{i}=1
)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\rho_{e}\nu_{ei}(0.60\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\parallel}+\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\perp})-0.67n_{e}\nabla_{\parallel}T_{e};

\displaystyle\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}^{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2.35p_{e}\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\parallel},

(I34)

and the thermal force value 0.67 is now closer to the correct value 0.70 as well.
However, the frictional heat flux
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}^{u}
is quite large (over 3 times larger than it should be, 2.35 vs 0.70).
Importantly, the Onsager symmetry between
\vec{\boldsymbol{q}}_{e}^{u}
and
\boldsymbol{R}_{e}^{T}
is broken,
which can be also seen from general results (
I32
), (
H48
).
Nevertheless, the model indeed improves the parallel thermal heat flux and the parallel thermal force of Burgers-Schunk.

Appendix J
10-moment model (viscosity)

To calculate the collisional contributions for the stress-tensor with the Landau operator,
one uses the following 10-moment distribution function of Grad

f_{b}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime})=\frac{n_{b}}{\pi^{3/2}v_{\textrm{th}b}^{3}}e^{-\frac{|\boldsymbol{c}_{b}|^{2}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}}\Big[1+\frac{m_{b}}{2T_{b}p_{b}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{b}:\boldsymbol{c}_{b}\boldsymbol{c}_{b}\Big].

(J1)

As a reminder
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}:\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}=0
. By using symmetries and Gaussian integration it is possible to show that

\displaystyle\int\boldsymbol{c}_{b}\boldsymbol{c}_{b}e^{-\frac{|\boldsymbol{c}_{b}|^{2}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}}d^{3}v^{\prime}=\frac{\pi^{3/2}}{2}v_{\textrm{th}b}^{5}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}};\qquad\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}:\int\boldsymbol{c}_{b}\boldsymbol{c}_{b}\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}e^{-\frac{|\boldsymbol{c}_{b}|^{2}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}}d^{3}v^{\prime}=0;

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}:\int\boldsymbol{c}_{b}\boldsymbol{c}_{b}\boldsymbol{c}_{b}\boldsymbol{c}_{b}e^{-\frac{|\boldsymbol{c}_{b}|^{2}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}}d^{3}v^{\prime}=\frac{\pi^{3/2}}{2}v_{\textrm{th}b}^{7}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}.

(J2)

The last integral is a special case of (
J48
).
Thus, the distribution function (
J1
) correctly reproduces density, fluid velocity, and full
pressure tensor
m_{b}\int\boldsymbol{c}_{b}\boldsymbol{c}_{b}f_{b}d^{3}c_{b}=p_{b}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}
, so the distribution function
is well defined.

J.1
Rosenbluth potentials

By using variables
\boldsymbol{x}=(\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{v})/v_{\textrm{th}b}
and
\boldsymbol{y}=(\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{u}_{b})/v_{\textrm{th}b}

with
\boldsymbol{c}_{b}=(\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y})v_{\textrm{th}b}
, we need to calculate Rosenbluth potentials

\displaystyle H_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{f_{b}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime})}{|\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{v}|}d^{3}v^{\prime}

(J3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n_{b}}{\pi^{3/2}v_{\textrm{th}b}}\int\frac{e^{-|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}}{x}\Big[1+\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{b}}{p_{b}}:(\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y})(\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y})\Big]d^{3}x;

\displaystyle G_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int|\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{v}|f_{b}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime})d^{3}v^{\prime}

(J4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n_{b}v_{\textrm{th}b}}{\pi^{3/2}}\int xe^{-|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}\Big[1+\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{b}}{p_{b}}:(\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y})(\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y})\Big]d^{3}x.

By using integrals (
J40
) and (
J44
), final results for the Rosenbluth potentials are

\displaystyle H_{b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n_{b}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}}\Big\{\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y}+\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{b}}{p_{b}}:\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}\Big[\textrm{erf}(y)\frac{3}{4y^{5}}-\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\Big(\frac{1}{y^{2}}+\frac{3}{2y^{4}}\Big)\Big]\Big\};

\displaystyle G_{b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n_{b}v_{\textrm{th}b}\Big\{\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}+\big(y+\frac{1}{2y}\big)\textrm{erf}(y)+\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}}{p_{b}}:\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}\Big[-\frac{3}{4\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{y^{4}}+\big(-\frac{1}{4y^{3}}+\frac{3}{8y^{5}}\big)\textrm{erf}(y)\Big]\Big\}.

(J5)

We will need derivative

\displaystyle\frac{\partial H_{b}}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n_{b}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}\boldsymbol{y}\Big(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{y^{2}}-\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y^{3}}\Big)

(J6)

\displaystyle+\frac{n_{b}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}\frac{2}{p_{b}}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{y})\Big[\textrm{erf}(y)\frac{3}{4y^{5}}-\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\Big(\frac{1}{y^{2}}+\frac{3}{2y^{4}}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle+\frac{n_{b}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}p_{b}}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}:\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y})\boldsymbol{y}\Big[\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\Big(\frac{1}{y^{2}}+\frac{5}{2y^{4}}+\frac{15}{4y^{6}}\Big)e^{-y^{2}}-\frac{15}{4}\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y^{7}}\Big],

As a double check, applying
\partial/\partial\boldsymbol{v}\cdot
at the last expression recovers
-4\pi f_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})
, where for example

\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{y})=0;\qquad\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot\big[(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{b}:\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y})\boldsymbol{y}\big]=\frac{5}{v_{\textrm{th}b}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}:\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}.

(J7)

The entire dynamical friction vector for the 10-moment model then becomes

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\frac{c_{ab}}{m_{a}^{2}}\big(1+\frac{m_{a}}{m_{b}}\big)\frac{n_{b}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}\Big\{\boldsymbol{y}\Big(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{y^{2}}-\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y^{3}}\Big)

(J8)

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{p_{b}}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{y})\Big[\textrm{erf}(y)\frac{3}{4y^{5}}-\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\Big(\frac{1}{y^{2}}+\frac{3}{2y^{4}}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{p_{b}}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}:\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y})\boldsymbol{y}\Big[\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\Big(\frac{1}{y^{2}}+\frac{5}{2y^{4}}+\frac{15}{4y^{6}}\Big)e^{-y^{2}}-\frac{15}{4}\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y^{7}}\Big]\Big\}.

For the diffusion tensor, to perform the subsequent analytic calculations in a clear way, it is useful to write the second Rosenbluth potential

G_{b}
by introducing
A_{1}
,
A_{2}

\displaystyle G_{b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n_{b}v_{\textrm{th}b}\Big[A_{1}+\frac{1}{p_{b}}\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}:\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}\big)A_{2}\Big],

(J9)

where

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}+\big(y+\frac{1}{2y}\big)\textrm{erf}(y);

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{4\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{y^{4}}+\big(-\frac{1}{4y^{3}}+\frac{3}{8y^{5}}\big)\textrm{erf}(y).

(J10)

The required derivatives then are

\displaystyle\frac{\partial G_{b}}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}=n_{b}\Big[\frac{\boldsymbol{y}}{y}A_{1}^{\prime}+\frac{2}{p_{b}}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}\cdot\boldsymbol{y})A_{2}+\Big(\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}}{p_{b}}:\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}\Big)\frac{\boldsymbol{y}}{y}A_{2}^{\prime}\Big],

(J11)

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial G_{b}}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}\partial\boldsymbol{v}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n_{b}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}}\Big\{\big(\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{y}-\frac{\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}}{y^{3}}\big)A_{1}^{\prime}+\frac{\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}}{y^{2}}A_{1}^{\prime\prime}

(J12)

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{p_{b}}\Big[\frac{\boldsymbol{y}}{y}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}\cdot\boldsymbol{y})+(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}\cdot\boldsymbol{y})\frac{\boldsymbol{y}}{y}\Big]A_{2}^{\prime}+\frac{2}{p_{b}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}A_{2}

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}}{p_{b}}:\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}\Big)\Big[\big(\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{y}-\frac{\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}}{y^{3}}\big)A_{2}^{\prime}+\frac{\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}}{y^{2}}A_{2}^{\prime\prime}\Big]\Big\}.

As a double check, applying
(1/2)\textrm{Tr}
at the last expression recovers
H_{b}
.

After a slight re-arangement suitable for calculations, the entire diffusion tensor then becomes

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{D}_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\frac{c_{ab}}{m_{a}^{2}}\frac{n_{b}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}}\Big\{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\frac{A_{1}^{\prime}}{y}+\frac{\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}}{y^{2}}\big(A_{1}^{\prime\prime}-\frac{A_{1}^{\prime}}{y}\big)

(J13)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{p_{b}}\Big[2\boldsymbol{y}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}\cdot\boldsymbol{y})+2(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}\cdot\boldsymbol{y})\boldsymbol{y}+(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}:\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y})\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\Big]\frac{A_{2}^{\prime}}{y}

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{p_{b}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}A_{2}+\frac{1}{p_{b}}\big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}:\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}\big)\frac{\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}}{y^{2}}\big(A_{2}^{\prime\prime}-\frac{A_{2}^{\prime}}{y}\big)\Big\},

with “coefficients”

\displaystyle\frac{A_{1}^{\prime}}{y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\frac{1}{y}-\frac{1}{2y^{3}}\big)\textrm{erf}(y)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{y^{2}};

\displaystyle A_{1}^{\prime\prime}-\frac{A_{1}^{\prime}}{y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(-\frac{1}{y}+\frac{3}{2y^{3}}\big)\textrm{erf}(y)-\frac{3}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{y^{2}};

\displaystyle A_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\frac{3}{4y^{4}}-\frac{15}{8y^{6}}\big)\textrm{erf}(y)+\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\big(\frac{1}{y^{3}}+\frac{15}{4y^{5}}\big);

\displaystyle\frac{A_{2}^{\prime}}{y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\frac{3}{4y^{5}}-\frac{15}{8y^{7}}\big)\textrm{erf}(y)+\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\big(\frac{1}{y^{4}}+\frac{15}{4y^{6}}\big);

\displaystyle A_{2}^{\prime\prime}-\frac{A_{2}^{\prime}}{y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(-\frac{15}{4y^{5}}+\frac{105}{8y^{7}}\big)\textrm{erf}(y)-\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\big(\frac{2}{y^{2}}+\frac{10}{y^{4}}+\frac{105}{4y^{6}}\big).

(J14)

Or explicitly in its entire form

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{D}_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\frac{c_{ab}}{m_{a}^{2}}\frac{n_{b}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}}\Big\{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\Big[\big(\frac{1}{y}-\frac{1}{2y^{3}}\big)\textrm{erf}(y)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{y^{2}}\Big]+\frac{\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}}{y^{2}}\Big[\big(-\frac{1}{y}+\frac{3}{2y^{3}}\big)\textrm{erf}(y)-\frac{3}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{y^{2}}\Big]

(J15)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{p_{b}}\Big[2\boldsymbol{y}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}\cdot\boldsymbol{y})+2(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}\cdot\boldsymbol{y})\boldsymbol{y}+(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}:\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y})\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\Big]\Big[\big(\frac{3}{4y^{5}}-\frac{15}{8y^{7}}\big)\textrm{erf}(y)+\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\big(\frac{1}{y^{4}}+\frac{15}{4y^{6}}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{p_{b}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}\Big[-\frac{3}{4\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{y^{4}}+\big(-\frac{1}{4y^{3}}+\frac{3}{8y^{5}}\big)\textrm{erf}(y)\Big]

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}}{p_{b}}:\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}\Big)\frac{\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}}{y^{2}}\Big[\big(-\frac{15}{4y^{5}}+\frac{105}{8y^{7}}\big)\textrm{erf}(y)-\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\big(\frac{2}{y^{2}}+\frac{10}{y^{4}}+\frac{105}{4y^{6}}\big)\Big]\Big\}.

J.2
Viscosity calculation

For species “a”, the distribution function in semi-linear approximation reads

\displaystyle f_{a}(\boldsymbol{v})=\frac{n_{a}}{\pi^{3/2}v_{\textrm{th}a}^{3}}e^{-\alpha^{2}y^{2}}\Big[1-2\alpha(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{p_{a}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}:\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}\Big].

(J16)

It can be seen that at the semi-linear level, there is no new contribution to the momentum equation.
For the pressure tensor equation, we need to calculate the following collisional contributions

\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{ab}^{(2)}=m_{a}\int f_{a}\big[\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\big]^{S}d^{3}v+m_{a}\int f_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}d^{3}v,

(J17)

where we have used that the diffusion tensor is symmetric.
Starting with the second term, and using the derived formulas (
J45
)-(
J48
),
integration over the diffusion tensor then yields

\displaystyle m_{a}\int f_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}d^{3}v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\frac{c_{ab}}{m_{a}^{2}}\frac{n_{b}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}}\frac{\rho_{a}}{\pi^{3/2}}\alpha^{3}\Big\{+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\frac{4\pi}{3}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(2A_{1}^{\prime}y+A_{1}^{\prime\prime}y^{2}\big)e^{-\alpha^{2}y^{2}}dy

(J18)

\displaystyle+\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}}{p_{b}}8\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big[\frac{3}{5}y^{3}A_{2}^{\prime}+y^{2}A_{2}+\frac{1}{15}y^{4}A_{2}^{\prime\prime}\Big]e^{-\alpha^{2}y^{2}}dy

\displaystyle+\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}}{p_{a}}\frac{8\pi}{15}\alpha^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}y^{4}\big(A_{1}^{\prime\prime}-\frac{A_{1}^{\prime}}{y}\big)e^{-\alpha^{2}y^{2}}dy\Big\},

and further 1D integration brings the following result

\displaystyle m_{a}\int f_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}}_{ab}d^{3}v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\frac{c_{ab}}{m_{a}^{2}}\frac{n_{b}}{v_{\textrm{th}b}}\frac{\rho_{a}}{\pi^{3/2}}\alpha^{3}\Big\{+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\frac{4\pi}{3}\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}\sqrt{1+\alpha^{2}}}

(J19)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}}{p_{b}}\frac{4\pi}{15}\frac{1}{(1+\alpha^{2})^{3/2}}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}}{p_{a}}\frac{4\pi}{15}\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}(1+\alpha^{2})^{3/2}}\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}\frac{m_{b}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}\Big[\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}(v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}+v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2})-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}}{p_{b}}\frac{v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}}{5}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}}{p_{a}}\frac{v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}}{5}\Big].

Similarly, the first term in (
J17
) calculates

\displaystyle m_{a}\int f_{a}\big[\boldsymbol{A}_{ab}\boldsymbol{c}_{a}\big]^{S}d^{3}v=\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}\Big[-\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}+\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}}{p_{b}}\frac{3}{5}\frac{v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}}{(v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}+v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2})}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}}{p_{a}}\frac{5v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2}+2v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}}{5(v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}+v_{\textrm{th}b}^{2})}v_{\textrm{th}a}^{2}\Big].

(J20)

Adding (
J19
)+(
J20
) yields final collisional contributions for the r.h.s. of the pressure tensor equation,
which can be written in the following convenient form

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{ab}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\frac{\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}(T_{b}-T_{a})\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}-2\frac{m_{a}\nu_{ab}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}\frac{T_{b}}{T_{ab}}\Big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}-\frac{T_{a}n_{a}}{T_{b}n_{b}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}\Big)

(J21)

\displaystyle-\frac{\nu_{ab}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}\Big[\frac{6}{5}m_{b}-\frac{4}{5}\mu_{ab}\frac{T_{b}-T_{a}}{T_{ab}}\Big]\Big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}+\frac{\rho_{a}}{\rho_{b}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}\Big),

with reduced mass and reduced temperature

\mu_{ab}=\frac{m_{a}m_{b}}{m_{a}+m_{b}};\qquad T_{ab}=\frac{m_{a}T_{b}+m_{b}T_{a}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}.

Introducing
\sum_{b}
over all species, result (
J21
) identifies with equation (44) of
Schunk 1977
(derived before by Burgers).
It is valid in the semi-linear approximation, for unrestricted temperature differences.
For Coulomb collisions, viscosity calculated through the Rosenbluth potentials (for the Landau collisional operator) thus
yields the same result as the Boltzmann collisional operator.
By explicitly separating the self-collisions

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{a}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{b}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{ab}^{(2)}=\frac{\delta\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}}{\delta t}

(J22)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\,\frac{6}{5}\nu_{aa}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}+\sum_{b\neq a}\Big[2\frac{\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}(T_{b}-T_{a})\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}-2\frac{m_{a}\nu_{ab}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}\frac{T_{b}}{T_{ab}}\Big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}-\frac{T_{a}n_{a}}{T_{b}n_{b}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle-\sum_{b\neq a}\Big[\frac{\nu_{ab}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}\Big(\frac{6}{5}m_{b}-\frac{4}{5}\mu_{ab}\frac{T_{b}-T_{a}}{T_{ab}}\Big)\Big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}+\frac{\rho_{a}}{\rho_{b}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}\Big)\Big],

where the “famous”
6/5
constant is present. As a double check, calculating the energy exchange rates yields

Q_{ab}=\frac{1}{2}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{ab}^{(2)}=3\frac{\rho_{a}\nu_{ab}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}(T_{b}-T_{a}),

(J23)

as it should be.

Collisional contributions for the stress-tensor thus are

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{a}^{(2)}\,{}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}}{\delta t}=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{a}^{(2)}-\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{a}^{(2)}

(J24)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\,\frac{6}{5}\nu_{aa}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}-\sum_{b\neq a}\Big[2\frac{m_{a}\nu_{ab}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}\frac{T_{b}}{T_{ab}}\Big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}-\frac{T_{a}n_{a}}{T_{b}n_{b}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle-\sum_{b\neq a}\Big[\frac{\nu_{ab}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}\Big(\frac{6}{5}m_{b}-\frac{4}{5}\mu_{ab}\frac{T_{b}-T_{a}}{T_{ab}}\Big)\Big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}+\frac{\rho_{a}}{\rho_{b}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}\Big)\Big],

and enter the r.h.s of its evolution equation, for example written in its simplest form

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}}{dt}+\Omega_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{b}}\times\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}\big)^{S}+p_{a}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}}_{a}=\frac{\delta\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}}{\delta t}.

(J25)

Importantly, in the collisionless regime the r.h.s of (
J25
) simply goes to zero.
It is possible to write a general solution in quasi-static approximation, but the stress-tensors
of various species are coupled.

J.3
Small temperature differences

For a particular case of small temperature differences between species

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{a}^{(2)}=\frac{\delta\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}}{\delta t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\,\frac{6}{5}\nu_{aa}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}

(J26)

\displaystyle-2\sum_{b\neq a}\frac{m_{a}\nu_{ab}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}\Big[\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{a}-\frac{n_{a}}{n_{b}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{b}+\frac{3}{5}\frac{m_{b}}{m_{a}}\Big(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}+\frac{\rho_{a}}{\rho_{b}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}\Big)\Big],

where one uses
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{p}}}=p\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}+\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}
, recovering equation (41d) of
Schunk 1977
.
Finally, for the stress tensor

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{a}^{(2)}\,{}^{\prime}=\frac{\delta\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{a}}{\delta t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\,\frac{6}{5}\nu_{aa}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}-2\sum_{b\neq a}\frac{m_{a}\nu_{ab}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}\Big[\big(1+\frac{3}{5}\frac{m_{b}}{m_{a}}\big)\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{a}^{(2)}-\frac{2}{5}\frac{n_{a}}{n_{b}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}\Big].

(J27)

J.4
One ion-electron plasma

For a plasma consisting of one ion species and electrons, in the first step

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{i}^{(2)}\,{}^{\prime}=\frac{\delta\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{i}}{\delta t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Big(\frac{6}{5}\nu_{ii}+2\nu_{ie}\Big)\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{i}+\frac{4}{5}\nu_{ie}\frac{n_{i}}{n_{e}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{e};

(J28)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{e}^{(2)}\,{}^{\prime}=\frac{\delta\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{e}}{\delta t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{6}{5}\big(\nu_{ee}+\nu_{ei}\big)\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{e}+\frac{4}{5}\nu_{ei}\frac{\rho_{e}}{\rho_{i}}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{i}.

(J29)

Nevertheless, because for example for the parallel viscosity the ion
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{i}
is larger than the electron
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{e}

by a factor of
\sqrt{m_{i}/m_{e}}
, the coupling is only weak and the last terms
in the above expressions can be for simplicity neglected. Then,

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{i}^{(2)}\,{}^{\prime}=\frac{\delta\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{i}}{\delta t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bar{\nu}_{i}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{i};\qquad\bar{\nu}_{i}=\frac{6}{5}\nu_{ii}+2\nu_{ie}=\frac{6}{5}\Big(1+\frac{5}{3}\frac{\sqrt{2}}{Z_{i}}\sqrt{\frac{m_{e}}{m_{i}}}\Big)\nu_{ii};

(J30)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{e}^{(2)}\,{}^{\prime}=\frac{\delta\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{e}}{\delta t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bar{\nu}_{e}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}^{(2)}_{e};\qquad\bar{\nu}_{e}=\frac{6}{5}(\nu_{ee}+\nu_{ei})=\frac{6}{5}\Big(1+\frac{1}{Z_{i}\sqrt{2}}\Big)\nu_{ei}.

(J31)

In a quasi-static approximation, one derives the following viscosity coefficients

\displaystyle\eta_{0}^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p_{a}}{\bar{\nu}_{a}};\quad\eta_{1}^{a}=\frac{p_{a}\bar{\nu}_{a}}{4\Omega_{a}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{a}^{2}};\quad\eta_{2}^{a}=\frac{p_{a}\bar{\nu}_{a}}{\Omega_{a}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{a}^{2}};\quad\eta_{3}^{a}=\frac{2p_{a}\Omega_{a}}{4\Omega_{a}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{a}^{2}};\quad\eta_{4}^{a}=\frac{p_{a}\Omega_{a}}{\Omega_{a}^{2}+\bar{\nu}_{a}^{2}},

(J32)

which have the same form as the BGK viscosities. The difference is that while for the BGK operator

\bar{\nu}_{i}=\nu_{ii}+\nu_{ie}
and
\bar{\nu}_{e}=\nu_{ee}+\nu_{ei}
, here the frequencies have to be added according to
(
J30
), (
J31
).

Importantly, because for ion viscosities
Braginskii 1965
neglected the ion-electron collisions,
direct comparision with Braginskii has to done with
\bar{\nu}_{i}=(6/5)\nu_{ii}
. Using this approximation, the parallel viscosities
of the Burger-Schunk model are

\eta_{0}^{i}=\frac{5}{6}\frac{p_{i}}{\nu_{ii}};\qquad\eta_{0}^{e}=\frac{5}{6}\frac{Z_{i}\sqrt{2}}{(1+Z_{i}\sqrt{2})}\frac{p_{e}}{\nu_{ei}},

(J33)

where
5/6=0.83
, contrasting with Braginskii ion value of
0.96
.
Considering specific case
Z_{i}=1
for the electron viscosity
\eta_{0}^{e}=0.49p_{e}/\nu_{ei}
, contrasting with Braginskii
value
0.73
.

J.5
Strong magnetic field limit

Examining the strong magnetic field limit, viscosities for ions become

\eta_{1}^{i}=\frac{3}{10}\frac{p_{i}\nu_{ii}}{\Omega_{i}^{2}};\qquad\eta_{2}^{i}=\frac{6}{5}\frac{p_{i}\nu_{ii}}{\Omega_{i}^{2}};\qquad\eta_{3}^{i}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{p_{i}}{\Omega_{i}};\qquad\eta_{4}^{i}=\frac{p_{i}}{\Omega_{i}},

(J34)

(with relations
\eta_{2}^{a}=4\eta_{1}^{a}
;
\eta_{4}^{a}=2\eta_{3}^{a}
valid for both electrons and ions).
All four viscosities match Braginskii exactly !
Similarly, for electrons in the strong magnetic field limit the Burgers-Schunk model yields

\eta_{1}^{e}=\frac{3}{10}\Big(1+\frac{1}{Z_{i}\sqrt{2}}\Big)\frac{p_{e}\nu_{ei}}{\Omega_{e}^{2}};\qquad\eta_{2}^{e}=\frac{6}{5}\Big(1+\frac{1}{Z_{i}\sqrt{2}}\Big)\frac{p_{e}\nu_{ei}}{\Omega_{e}^{2}};\qquad\eta_{3}^{e}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{p_{e}}{\Omega_{e}};\qquad\eta_{4}^{e}=\frac{p_{e}}{\Omega_{e}}.

(J35)

Evaluation for
Z_{i}=1
yields
\eta_{1}^{e}=0.51p_{e}\nu_{ei}/\Omega_{e}^{2}
and again all match
Braginskii exactly. If Braginksii provided electron viscosities for different
Z_{i}
values,
all four viscosity coefficients (except of parallel
\eta_{0}
) would match his results exactly.

If ion-electron collisions are considered, the gyroviscosities
\eta_{3}^{i}
,
\eta_{4}^{i}
given by (
J34
) remain unchanged,
and the perpendicular viscosities become

\eta_{1}^{i}=\frac{p_{i}\nu_{ii}}{\Omega_{i}^{2}}\frac{3}{10}\Big(1+\frac{5}{3}\frac{\sqrt{2}}{Z_{i}}\sqrt{\frac{m_{e}}{m_{i}}}\Big);\qquad\eta_{2}^{i}=\frac{p_{i}\nu_{ii}}{\Omega_{i}^{2}}\frac{6}{5}\Big(1+\frac{5}{3}\frac{\sqrt{2}}{Z_{i}}\sqrt{\frac{m_{e}}{m_{i}}}\Big),

(J36)

where again
\eta_{2}^{i}=4\eta_{1}^{i}
holds. That the result (
J36
) is indeed correct,
can be checked against the 2-Laguerre equation (89b) of
Ji & Held 2013
when written in strong B-limit.
(Use
\zeta=(1/Z_{i})\sqrt{m_{e}/m_{i}}
;
r_{i}=\Omega_{i}\hat{\tau}_{ii}
;
\eta_{2}^{i}=\hat{\eta}_{2}^{i}p_{i}\hat{\tau}_{ii}
,
with conversion
\hat{\tau}_{ii}=\tau_{ii}/\sqrt{2}
because we use Braginskii definition of
\tau_{ii}
; see Section
VIII.2
).
Interestingly, the result is not changed in their 3-Laguerre model (or higher order models).
The same is true for the perpendicular heat conductivities
\kappa_{\perp}^{a}
.

J.6
Table of integrals

To calculate the first Rosenbluth potential
H_{b}
, we used the following integrals

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}\int\frac{1}{x}e^{-|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}d^{3}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}\pi^{3/2}\frac{\textrm{erf}(y)}{y};

(J37)

\displaystyle\int\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{x}e^{-|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}d^{3}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\boldsymbol{y}\pi\Big[\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{y^{2}}+\sqrt{\pi}\Big(\frac{1}{y}-\frac{1}{2y^{3}}\Big)\textrm{erf}(y)\Big];

(J38)

\displaystyle\int\frac{\boldsymbol{x}\boldsymbol{x}}{x}e^{-|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}d^{3}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\pi\Big[\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{2y^{2}}+\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}\textrm{erf}(y)\Big(\frac{1}{y}-\frac{1}{2y^{3}}\Big)\Big]

(J39)

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}\pi\Big[e^{-y^{2}}\Big(\frac{1}{y^{2}}-\frac{3}{2y^{4}}\Big)+\sqrt{\pi}\textrm{erf}(y)\Big(\frac{1}{y}-\frac{1}{y^{3}}+\frac{3}{4y^{5}}\Big)\Big],

and so

\displaystyle\int\frac{(\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y})(\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y})}{x}e^{-|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}d^{3}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\pi\Big[\frac{e^{-y^{2}}}{2y^{2}}+\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}\textrm{erf}(y)\Big(\frac{1}{y}-\frac{1}{2y^{3}}\Big)\Big]

(J40)

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}\pi\Big[\sqrt{\pi}\textrm{erf}(y)\frac{3}{4y^{5}}-e^{-y^{2}}\Big(\frac{1}{y^{2}}+\frac{3}{2y^{4}}\Big)\Big].

To calculate the second Rosenbluth
G_{b}
we used

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}\int xe^{-|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}d^{3}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}\pi\Big[e^{-y^{2}}+\sqrt{\pi}\big(y+\frac{1}{2y}\big)\textrm{erf}(y)\Big];

(J41)

\displaystyle\int x\boldsymbol{x}e^{-|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}d^{3}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\boldsymbol{y}\pi\Big[\big(1+\frac{1}{2y^{2}}\big)e^{-y^{2}}+\sqrt{\pi}\big(y+\frac{1}{y}-\frac{1}{4y^{3}}\big)\textrm{erf}(y)\Big];

(J42)

\displaystyle\int x\boldsymbol{x}\boldsymbol{x}e^{-|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}d^{3}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\pi\Big[\big(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{4y^{2}}\big)e^{-y^{2}}+\sqrt{\pi}\big(\frac{y}{2}-\frac{1}{8y^{3}}+\frac{1}{2y}\big)\textrm{erf}(y)\Big]

(J43)

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}\pi\Big[\big(1+\frac{1}{y^{2}}-\frac{3}{4y^{4}}\big)e^{-y^{2}}+\sqrt{\pi}\big(y+\frac{3}{2y}-\frac{3}{4y^{3}}+\frac{3}{8y^{5}}\big)\textrm{erf}(y)\Big],

and so

\displaystyle\int x(\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y})(\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y})e^{-|\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}d^{3}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\pi\Big[\big(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{4y^{2}}\big)e^{-y^{2}}+\sqrt{\pi}\big(\frac{y}{2}-\frac{1}{8y^{3}}+\frac{1}{2y}\big)\textrm{erf}(y)\Big]

(J44)

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}\pi\Big[-\frac{3}{4y^{4}}e^{-y^{2}}+\sqrt{\pi}\big(-\frac{1}{4y^{3}}+\frac{3}{8y^{5}}\big)\textrm{erf}(y)\Big].

To calculate the viscosity, the Rosenbluth potentials were integrated by the following scheme

\displaystyle\int\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}f(y)e^{-\alpha^{2}y^{2}}d^{3}y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{3}\int y^{2}f(y)e^{-\alpha^{2}y^{2}}d^{3}y=\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}\frac{4\pi}{3}\int_{0}^{\infty}y^{4}f(y)e^{-\alpha^{2}y^{2}}dy;

(J45)

\displaystyle\int\boldsymbol{y}(\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}\cdot\boldsymbol{y})f(y)e^{-\alpha^{2}y^{2}}d^{3}y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}\frac{4\pi}{3}\int_{0}^{\infty}y^{4}f(y)e^{-\alpha^{2}y^{2}}dy;

(J46)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}:\int\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}f(y)e^{-\alpha^{2}y^{2}}d^{3}y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(J47)

where in our case functions
f(y)
are well-behaved, so these integrals hold.
Additionally, for any symmetric (
3\times 3
) matrix
\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{A}}}

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{A}}}:\int\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}f(y)e^{-\alpha^{2}y^{2}}d^{3}y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{A}}}+(\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{A}}})\frac{\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}}{2}\Big]\frac{8\pi}{15}\int_{0}^{\infty}y^{6}f(y)e^{-\alpha^{2}y^{2}}dy,

(J48)

and for the stress-tensor
\textrm{Tr}\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}}_{b}^{(2)}=0
(the integral can be calculated for example by splitting

\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{A}}}:\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}
explicitly to components, and then by using symmetries).

J.6.1
Spherical integration

For example to obtain integrals (
J39
),
one introduces orthogonal reference frame in the x-space with unit vectors
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1},\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2},\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{3}
,
where the direction of
\boldsymbol{y}
forms axis
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{3}=\boldsymbol{y}/y
, so that

\boldsymbol{x}=x\sin\theta\cos\phi\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}+x\sin\theta\sin\phi\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2}+x\cos\theta\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{3},

(J49)

which then allows to first perform integral over
d\phi

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\boldsymbol{x}d\phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi x\cos\theta\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{3};

(J50)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\boldsymbol{x}\boldsymbol{x}d\phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi x^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\bar{\bar{\boldsymbol{I}}}+\pi x^{2}(3\cos^{2}\theta-1)\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{3}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{3},

(J51)

and then over
d\theta dx
.

Appendix K
Braginskii heat flux (11-moment model)

We use the usual
reducible
Hermite polynomials with perturbation of the distribution function

f_{b}=f_{b}^{(0)}(1+\chi_{b})
(see details in Appendix
B
)

\chi_{b}=\frac{1}{10}\tilde{h}_{i}^{b(3)}\tilde{H}_{i}^{b(3)}+\frac{1}{280}\tilde{h}_{i}^{b(5)}\tilde{H}_{i}^{b(5)},

(K1)

where

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{b(3)}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{jk}\tilde{H}^{b(3)}_{ijk}=\tilde{c}_{i}^{b}(\tilde{c}_{b}^{2}-5);

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{b(5)}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{jk}\delta_{lm}\tilde{H}^{b(5)}_{ijklm}=\tilde{c}_{i}^{b}(\tilde{c}_{b}^{4}-14\tilde{c}_{b}^{2}+35).

(K2)

For clarity of calculations, we here only consider the heat flux part of
\chi_{b}
(i.e. 11-moment model) but the full 21-moment model can be implicitly assumed for the
final collisional contributions at the semi-linear level. The orthogonality relations are (species indices are dropped)

\displaystyle\int\tilde{H}^{(3)}_{i}\tilde{H}^{(3)}_{j}\phi^{(0)}d^{3}\tilde{c}=10\delta_{ij};\qquad\int\tilde{H}^{(5)}_{i}\tilde{H}^{(5)}_{j}\phi^{(0)}d^{3}\tilde{c}=280\delta_{ij},

(K3)

yielding (
K1
). By using this perturbation
\chi_{b}
one can directly calculate the heat flux vector and the 5th-order moment
vector

\displaystyle\vec{q}^{\,b}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{m_{b}}{2}\int f_{b}c_{i}c^{2}d^{3}c=\frac{p_{b}}{2}\sqrt{\frac{T_{b}}{m_{b}}}\tilde{h}^{b(3)}_{i};

\displaystyle{X}^{b(5)}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{b}\int f_{b}c_{i}c^{4}d^{3}c=p_{b}\frac{T_{b}}{m_{b}}\sqrt{\frac{T_{b}}{m_{b}}}\big(\tilde{h}^{b(5)}_{i}+14\tilde{h}^{b(3)}\big),

(K4)

or one can directly calculate Hermite moments

\displaystyle\tilde{h}^{b(3)}_{i}=\frac{2}{p_{b}}\sqrt{\frac{m_{b}}{T_{b}}}\vec{q}^{\,b}_{i};

\displaystyle\tilde{h}^{b(5)}_{i}=\frac{1}{p_{b}}\sqrt{\frac{m_{b}}{T_{b}}}\Big(\frac{m_{b}}{T_{b}}{X}^{b(5)}_{i}-28\vec{q}_{i}^{\,b}\Big).

(K5)

Note that we have chosen to define all the vectors and tensors (including
X^{b(5)}_{i},\tilde{H}^{(3)}_{i},\tilde{H}^{(5)}_{i}
etc.) without any
additional normalization factors, so they are directly obtained from higher-order tensors by just applying contractions.
The sole exception is the heat flux vector which contains a factor of
1/2
, to match its usual definition.
As also noted after equation (
B41
), the reminder of this
exception in the index notation is the arrow on the heat flux vector components
\vec{q}_{i}
.
We will again use the Rosenbluth potentials, and not the center-of-mass transformation. However, this time we will keep working
with the Hermite fluid moments, which has a nice advantage that the expressions can be kept in partially dimensionless form.

K.1
Rosenbluth potentials

By introducing

\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{b}=\sqrt{\frac{m_{b}}{T_{b}}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{u}_{b});\qquad|\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{v}|=\sqrt{\frac{T_{b}}{m_{b}}}|\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{b}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}|;\qquad\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}=\sqrt{\frac{m_{b}}{T_{b}}}(\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{u}_{b}),

(K6)

so that our previously used
\boldsymbol{y}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}/\sqrt{2}
, the Rosenbluth potentials read

\displaystyle H_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{f_{b}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime})}{|\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{v}|}d^{3}v^{\prime}=n_{b}\sqrt{\frac{m_{b}}{T_{b}}}\int\frac{\phi^{(0)}_{b}}{|\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{b}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}|}(1+\chi_{b})d^{3}\tilde{c}_{b};

\displaystyle G_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int|\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{v}|f_{b}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime})d^{3}v^{\prime}=n_{b}\sqrt{\frac{T_{b}}{m_{b}}}\int|\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{b}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}|\phi^{(0)}_{b}(1+\chi_{b})d^{3}\tilde{c}_{b},

(K7)

and calculate

\displaystyle H_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n_{b}\sqrt{\frac{m_{b}}{T_{b}}}\Big[\frac{1}{\tilde{y}}\textrm{erf}\Big(\frac{\tilde{y}}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{e^{-\tilde{y}^{2}/2}}{10}\Big(\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}}^{b(3)}+(\tilde{y}^{2}-5)\frac{\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}}^{b(5)}}{28}\Big)\Big];

\displaystyle G_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n_{b}\sqrt{\frac{T_{b}}{m_{b}}}\Big[\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}e^{-\tilde{y}^{2}/2}+\big(\tilde{y}+\frac{1}{\tilde{y}}\big)\textrm{erf}\big(\frac{\tilde{y}}{\sqrt{2}}\big)

(K8)

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{\textrm{erf}(\tilde{y}/\sqrt{2})}{5\tilde{y}^{3}}-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{e^{-\tilde{y}^{2}/2}}{5\tilde{y}^{2}}\Big)\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}}^{b(3)}-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{e^{-\tilde{y}^{2}/2}}{140}\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}}^{b(5)}\Big].

The derivatives calculate by using
\partial/\partial v_{i}=\sqrt{m_{b}/T_{b}}\partial/\partial\tilde{y}_{i}
and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial H_{b}}{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n_{b}m_{b}}{T_{b}}\Big[\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{e^{-\tilde{y}^{2}/2}}{\tilde{y}^{2}}-\frac{\textrm{erf}(\tilde{y}/\sqrt{2})}{\tilde{y}^{3}}\Big)

(K9)

\displaystyle-\,\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{e^{-\tilde{y}^{2}/2}}{10}\Big(\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}}^{b(3)}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}}^{b(3)})+(\tilde{y}^{2}-5)\frac{\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}}^{b(5)}}{28}-(\tilde{y}^{2}-7)\frac{\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}}^{b(5)})}{28}\Big)\Big],

and by further applying
(\partial/\partial\boldsymbol{v})\cdot
recovers
-4\pi f_{b}
. It is useful to write the second Rosenbluth potential as

G_{b}(\boldsymbol{v})=n_{b}\sqrt{\frac{T_{b}}{m_{b}}}\Big[\tilde{A}_{1}+\tilde{A}_{3}\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}}^{b(3)}+\tilde{A}_{5}\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}}^{b(5)}\Big],

(K10)

where
